First published in the African Writers Series in: 1971

[Page ]
Front matter
[Page xi ]
Introduction
[Page xii ]
Very little is known about the mythology of the many peoples of the Congo. As far as I know
there is no comprehensive survey in English. The most authoritative works in this field have
been written by missionaries who spent twenty to forty years among the people in the
villages and learned to speak several dialects. The great majority of their works are written in
flowery Flemish. The present book presents a choice from this treasure-trove of stories, to
which I have added a number of tales from my own research collection. The reader will soon
find that Congolese legends are different from many other tales. There is a mystery at the
bottom of each story that leaves us puzzled: the inexpressible secret of the human spirit. We
are frequently struck by the profound wisdom that is hidden in a simple story.
The Congo, that immense land, was carved out of Central Africa by King Leopold of the
Belgians a century ago. Now a republic, it is inhabited by fifteen million people, speaking
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
2 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 4 ---
more than three hundred languages. Each language is spoken by a separate people with an
identity of its own.
Every people has its own manners and customs, its own way of dressing and hut-building, its
own proverbs and riddles, its own jokes and songs, its own folk-tales. In every language there
is a way to say 'Once upon a time there was a king', and the stories are all different. Every
people has dozens of stories, which are told at night when the fires flame up and the owls
hoot in the trees: stories of spirits and witches, of serpents and monsters.
Of the thousands of stories that are told in the Congo, only a
[Page xiii ]
few have been selected. Some come from the open savannah, some from the forest and some
from the river banks.
Of course, no printed version of any tale can rival the Congolese story-tellers themselves.
Whoever has heard an African narrator tell a story to his family and friends in his own
inimitable way, will agree that much that he communicates to his audience cannot be caught
and confined in a book. All the characters of the drama are introduced with their own voices:
the wild pig's snorting, the snake's hissing, the birds' fluting, the lion's lazy yawn. Alas, the
Roman alphabet has no letters with which these languages could be printed.
There are many ways of telling stories in the Congo. Usually it is after nightfall that the call
can be heard: 'Tell a story, story- teller!' There must be a fire to sit round, and the story-teller
is mostly an elderly person who 'has forgotten more than you have seen in your life'. There
are special expressions in use for begging a story-teller to begin his story and, once he has
started, to make him go on. In the middle of the story the narrator will stop and refuse to go
on unless more tobacco is provided, for without a smoking pipe the ideas will not come.
The nights in Africa are an exciting background to thrilling tales. There is always a concert of
crickets and cicadas, and the frogs are croaking in their pools. The bats are fluttering
overhead and the owls are hooting plaintively. In the distance a hyena's howl may be heard, or
a jackal's laughter. In addition, there are a thousand noises in the savannah and in the forest,
that cannot be identified, hence the proverb: 'The wilderness never sleeps', meaning that one
can expect to be attacked at any time. Proverbs are an integral part of all traditional stories,
and I have done my best to render them as closely to the original as possible without leaving
the Western reader completely puzzled, although in some cases the proverb is intended to be
mysterious, as when the diviner Bongenge is anxious to conceal the real intention of his
revelations. 'Many proverbs tire even the elders.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
3 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 5 ---
[Page xiv ]
Some of my friends complain that the stories I tell them from Africa are weird and often
shocking, much different from good old Grimm's tales. To this I can only answer: 'Don't
expect a fresh guinea fowl to taste like your own old chicken.'
The reader is invited to join us on a criss-cross journey through the Congo, listening to one or
two tales here, a long linked narrative there, a large collection elsewhere. There are tales for
every taste: history and oral traditions as well as tales of mystery, magic and miracles. Each
chapter is devoted to the tales of a different people.
A straight translation of any of these tales, either from the original language, or from the
Flemish (or occasionally French) as given in a journal or learned work, has not been possible.
The missionaries wrote for one another, for their learned friends, and fellow anthropologists
and linguists. A great deal of local knowledge, and familiarity with the Congolese languages,
was simply taken for granted. Whoever has had the privilege of attending conferences with
these scholarly fathers will agree that only those who had, like them, devoted more than
twenty years of study to the subject could follow their discussions.
It has therefore been thought that readers who are interested in folk-lore and mythology but
have no time for detailed study would appreciate the material better if it were rearranged to
some extent. What was implied in the narrative has been made explicit, what was taken for
granted has been explained. Obscure passages have been interpreted.
Several years of preparatory study in the anthropology of the Congo were necessary before
the rewriting in English of the first story could even be started. This study, however, has been
a pleasure. I hope the English-reading public will enjoy these myths and legends as much as I
did when I first met them.
JAN KNAPPERT
[Page [1] ]

--- PAGE 6 ---
I The Nilotic Alur of Lake Albert
[Page [2] ]
The Alur live in the extreme north-east of the country, on the shore of Lake Albert; the
opposite Ugandan shore is the home of the Acholi, who are related to them. Their culture is
dominated by kings, and by the priests who possess the secret of rain-making. That is to say,
they understand an art which the backward Europeans have not yet learned: rain comes at
their command after they have performed the correct ritual and pronounced the secret
formula. He who possesses the pouch that contains the mysterious rain-stones has a right to
the throne.
1. THE UNDERWORLD
In the days of this story, Anguza was still a young man. One day he went out on the lake in
his boat to catch fish. He rowed and rowed until he was far away from the shore, for he had
been told that the fish were bigger there and more numerous. Suddenly he saw that the
water-drops on his oar had changed into pearls. Each time he swung the blade out of the
water there was a spray of shining pearls. He baled some of the lake water into his boat and
pure pearls rattled over the bottom. He looked with attention at the water of the lake and saw
big white fish swimming below the surface. He saw that the fish were as big as goats; in fact,
they looked in every way like goats.
He jumped out of the boat and into the lake. Lower, lower, lower he sank. Then he stood on
the bottom and saw lush green grass everywhere. And there were the goats---hundreds of
them.
[Page [3] ]
They belonged to the Lake God, Jokinam, who came forward to welcome the young
earthling. He made him his shepherd, and so Anguza tended the goats of the lake-bottom and
lived a good life, with plenty of meat to eat and milk to drink.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
5 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 7 ---
One day he said to the Lake God: 'I want to go home to my people.'
The Lake God said: 'As you wish, but on one condition: if you tell the people on earth what
you have seen here you will die at once.'
Then the Lake God took him back to the surface, up, up, until he saw his boat. He was put
down in it and rowed back to the shore. He hid the pearls in a cave and told nobody what he
had seen. From time to time he travelled to the city to sell some of his pearls to the jewellers.
He became a rich man with many cows. He married and had many children.
But one day, at a beer party, he forgot himself, and began to boast: 'I have lived on the bottom
of the lake. I have been the herdsman of the Lake God's goats ...' At once he fell down, as
dead as a stone. The elders of the village consulted the diviner regarding Anguza's sudden
death.
The Lake God spoke through the mouth of the diviner: 'Whoever divulges the secrets of the
lake, will die. Anguza did not keep his promise, and he died.'
2. THE KING OF THE COUNTRY BENEATH THE EARTH
A man went to his brother to borrow an axe. His brother said: 'Do not break it or lose it!'
The man promised he would not, and went to the forest to cut a tree. He hit the tree three
times with his brother's axe; then the blade broke and fell into a hole in the ground. The
young man knelt down and peeped into the hole, but saw nothing.
[Page [4] ]
He put in his foot but felt no bottom. He put in his whole leg, and suddenly he sank down and
vanished into the bowels of the earth.
There he found himself on a high-road. He travelled along it until he arrived at a town. In the
town was the palace of the country's king. When the young man called at the gate of the king's
enclosure, he was met by the vizier. The vizier asked him his business, and he answered that
he had lost the blade of his brother's axe. The vizier said: 'Wait here.' He went and told the
king that a visitor from the country above had called. The king decided that he would see his
guest the next morning. The vizier came back and led the young man to the guest-house,
where he was given an excellent meal and a bed.
The next day he was taken before the king, who was sitting on his throne. He ordered a stool
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
6 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 8 ---
for the guest, which was a great honour. The young man saw that the eyes of the King beneath
the Earth were like stars, sparkling fire. He was frightened but the vizier told him: 'Do not
fear. Tomorrow you will go home with your brother's axe.'
The next morning he was given a cow and four goats, and his brother's axe-blade, which had
been found by the people of the country beneath the earth. The vizier said to him:
'Remember that you must never tell anybody what you have seen here. If you do, you will die
at once.'
The young man was shown the road out of the earth-country; it led to a cave in a rock.
Suddenly he knew that he was near his village. He drove the cow and the goats home and
returned the axe to his brother. From that time they lived in peace.
3. THE ROCK SPIRIT
There was a beautiful rock standing in the middle of the land. A woman went there to sit
down in its shade and eat her pot of
[Page [5] ]
porridge with fish and meat. Although the woman knew that there was a spirit there, who
lived in the rock, she did not offer any of her food to the spirit, not even one little fish. Of
course, the spirit was annoyed by the woman's selfishness and decided to punish her.
The woman had her baby with her in a carry-cloth, and a basket of millet. She had put both
down on the stone before she started her meal. When she had finished, she took up her
basket but could not lift her child. She put the basket down again so that she could lift her
baby. But then she found that the basket was stuck to the rock and she could not get it off the
ground. She decided that she would rather have the basket of millet than her baby, so she put
her baby back on the stone, and was now able to take away her basket. With that she went
home, leaving the baby lying there on the rock.
Some time later people passed by that place and to their surprise heard a child's voice crying
from the rock. They went and looked but 
saw nothing . They went to the village and told the
people there what they had heard. The father of the child heard the story and became
suspicious that this might be his own child, which had been missing for some time. He
consulted a diviner who said: 'Go to the rock and take an offering of porridge, meat, and fish.
Put it on the rock and invoke the spirit who lives in the rock, Jokichana.'
So the man went to the rock, together with his mother, taking his offerings. When they came
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
7 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 9 ---
near the rock, they heard the voice of the child singing: ' Wuye, wuye , the spirit has taken
me, the spirit has kept me, and I did not even eat a thing.'
The father put a pot of porridge on the rock, and a dish of fish, and one of meat. Then the
people began to dance while the drummers drummed away. The diviner invoked the spirit of
the rock. After a time the rock began to tremble. Suddenly it split open and the child
appeared from inside. The grandmother took him in her arms and comforted him. The people
were overjoyed,
[Page [6] ]
saying: 'Thank the great spirit of the rock, praise the great god of the rock!'
This story does not tell us what happened to the mother in the end. It is clear that she ran
away, for it was her husband's mother who comforted the baby. Obviously she was a witch,
for only witches neglect their children. All good women would rather have their babies than
their baskets. To leave a child exposed to the sun and the hyenas is worse than killing it.
4. THE BIRD SPIRIT
A man set a bird-snare and the same night a bird got trapped in it. The next morning the man
sent his eldest boy to see if a bird had been caught, and, if so, to bring it home. The boy went
out and found the bird. It was singing to him in human language: 'Little boy, little boy, kiri
kija kija , what have you come here for? What are you going to do?'
The boy replied with the same tune: 'I have come to see the snare, to see Daddy's snare. If a
bird is in it, I must bring it home. Daddy wants to eat it!'
Having sung a song with the bird, the boy could not bring himself to take it home with him;
he was afraid. He ran home and told his father: 'Daddy, Daddy, I saw a bird in your snare, and
the bird could sing in the Alur language!'
His father said: 'Impossible, birds do not sing words, they just twitter. You, second son, go
and take that bird out, and bring it here.'
His second boy went out, but when he arrived near the snare, the bird was singing to him:
'Little boy, Little boy ...', exactly the same words as it had used to his brother.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
8 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 10 ---
The boy ran home and said: 'It is true, Father, the bird sings like a human being. It sang a
song for me!'
[Page [7] ]
'Impossible!' said the father, and decided to go and see for himself. He found the bird singing
to him: 'Man, man! Kiri, kiri, kija! What have you come to do?'
'I have come to see my snare; I have come to catch you, to kill you, to eat you!'
The father found himself answering with the same tune---he could not help it. Nevertheless,
he took the bird from his snare and brought it home with him. He ordered his daughter to
make porridge, and his wife to cook the bird. Then he invited all the elders of the village to
come and eat the bird near the village sanctuary. He carried the dish with the well-prepared
bird on it to the ancestor-shrine. The elders began to eat the porridge.
Suddenly the cooked bird on the dish lifted its head, opened its eyes, rose up, grew feathers,
opened its wings, and flew up to the sky. They could not eat the bird; it was the spirit of one
of their fathers.
5. THE SNAKE GOD
A king was sitting on his throne surrounded by his councillors. Suddenly they saw a huge
mamba snake descending from the mountain---down, down, down, closer and closer. The tail
of the snake stayed on the mountain, the body uncoiled and extended down the slope, and the
head soon reached the village. It entered the king's enclosure, it circled round the king's
throne, round and round, until the king was entirely entwined. He was powerless and could
not move.
A young man said: 'We must catch mice and frogs and offer them to the snake.'
The king called out: 'A good counsel! Go and do so, all of you!'
The men went in all directions to catch mice and frogs. They
[Page [8] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
9 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 11 ---
collected hundreds of them, which they brought to the snake. The snake ate them all, until at
last it was satisfied and withdrew to the mountain.
The king called the young man and said: 'Where did you learn that good counsel?'
The youth said: 'From my father's brother.'
The king said: 'Bring your uncle here; I will make him my vizier. He shall be in charge of all
the cattle in my kraal. And to you too, my son, I am very grateful, for thanks to you the snake
released me. Without you, who knows what might have happened? Now that we have given
our sacrifice to the snake, it is content.'
This story is told to warn children that they must always remember what their elders tell
them. So the people say today. The original meaning, however, may have been very different.
The king says: 'We have paid the snake for what we have received.' So it was the snake who
gave the people food. The wealth of the earth, on which people live, is symbolized by the
snake.
6. THE PRINCESS FROM HEA VEN
In the country of Pwalo there lived a young king whose name was Mblukwa. One evening, as
he was walking on earth, he was seen by a princess in heaven who was called Niachero,
'Daughter of the star'. She fell in love with the handsome young king and decided to travel to
earth and marry him. She called her servants and descended to the land of Pwalo. When she
arrived in the town of King Mblukwa, the radiant princess announced: 'I have come to marry
the king.'
The king was quickly informed that the star-princess had arrived from the skies in order to
become his wife. The councillors advised him that it would be a good thing to marry her,
indeed it would be inauspicious, or even dangerous, to refuse. No persuasion was
[Page [9] ]
needed, however. The king said he was delighted to hear the words of the celestial princess.
'I will marry her! Minister! Call the elders and tell them to put on their ceremonial garments.
Everything must be arranged for the celebration! Tell the women of the town to prepare their
best dishes for the great party!'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
10 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 12 ---
The people said: 'We are glad to hear these words from Your Majesty! For us, your people,
hearing is obeying.'
The bride, Princess Niachero, requested all the people to wash and bathe before coming to
the feast, and to put on spotless clothes. No black vegetables were allowed, and only meat
was to be prepared as relish with the porridge. All the people agreed and promised to arrive
clean and scrubbed.
A huge meal was prepared, with lots of meat, and the drums sounded for the dance. The
celebrations lasted three days, after which all the people were tired and slept.
The princess stayed three months with the king as his wife. Then she said: 'Now you must tie
the animals together for my bride-price. I want to go and visit the people in my home
country.'
The king agreed and gave her twenty-five goats. He also ordered six strong men to
accompany her, as her bodyguard. He added two more goats, one for provisions, and one as a
parting gift of his own to his celestial bride.
The party set out and travelled for two days until they reached a high mountain. Suddenly, a
thick cloud was swept down by a strong wind. The cloud enveloped all the seven people and
the twenty-seven goats, and rose up with them into the sky. When they reached the
sky-country, they heard drumming and singing in the distance.
They came to a house, and the princess said to the men. 'You must stay here in this house.
Remember that you are not allowed to lift the lid of any of the pots. I will first go and join my
people in the dance.'
When she arrived at the place where they were dancing, she
[Page [10] ]
was welcomed with shouts of joy. 'Our daughter has come back!' The celestials embraced her,
gave her food, and invited her to join the dancing.
Suddenly, all the people looked up, for they sensed something ominous. Sure enough, there it
was: a cloud of locusts had risen up like dust in the desert. The celestials wondered if there
was anyone in their town; anyone in the house-with-the-pots? Someone must have lifted the
lid of the locust-jar!
They hurried to the house-with-the-pots, and found the six servants with locusts all over
them, like flies crawling on carrion. The servants were almost dead: the sky-people had
arrived just in time. They collected all the locusts together and put them back in their jar.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
11 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 13 ---
They said to the servants: 'Do you see now why you were told not to lift the lid of any of
these pots? Will you now stop bringing misfortune on your own heads?'
The Daughter of the Star stayed with her parents for a month. She then bid farewell to them
and called her husband's servants. The cloud arrived and enveloped them again. It conveyed
them back to earth and put them gently on the field near the foot of the mountain.
The Princess from Heaven had come back to her beloved husband.
7. THE FROG BLOWS THE TRUMPET
In ancient times there was a king who had two sons, the Frog and the Lizard. One day the
king fell ill with a fatal disease, and he realized that he had not long to live. The king decided
to name his successor. He sent word to his sons that the one who was the first to arrive at his
court would become king after him. When they heard this message, the two princes at once
ground corn for the journey and set out on their way---the Frog from the
[Page [11] ]
water in which he lived, the Lizard from the rock on which he basked.
The Lizard was the first to start. He did not wait to start at the same time as the Frog, to see
who could run the fastest. He thought: 'I am better than that fat Frog. He is terribly ugly, and
what's worse, he travels on his anus.'
The Frog perceived what the Lizard thought, and saw that he had started ahead of him. He
took a yatkot , which is the branch of a certain tree known only to professional rain-makers.
He pounded it to powder and strewed it in the water. At once black clouds appeared on the
horizon and obscured the sun. A fierce wind began to blow, shaking the trees. Soon the first
drops of rain dug holes in the dust, then a cascade of water tumbled down. The Frog was
content. He travelled briskly in this nice wet weather: jump, jump, jump.
The Lizard had, up till then, been far ahead, racing along. Suddenly he felt cold: the sun
disappeared, clouds collected. The Lizard looked for a place to shelter, for he did not like
rain. He found a hole in a tree and hid in it. There he waited for the rain to stop.
At last, the Frog arrived at the king's court. He told his heralds to blow their trumpets. The
guardsmen at the king's gate ran to the king and announced: 'His Highness the Frog has
arrived. We have heard his trumpeters!' The king ordered all his men to be assembled in the
hall. When the Frog entered, the king made it known that the Frog would inherit the kingdom
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
12 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 14 ---
and all that was in it.
When the sun began to shine again, the Lizard emerged from under the tree and finally
arrived at court. He came just in time to hear the Frog's trumpeters announce that he would
become king. The Frog said to the Lizard: 'You have a skin of beautiful colours, but I have
won.'
Whenever frogs begin to croak, people say: 'The frogs blow the trumpets of royal dignity. It
will soon begin to rain.'
[Page [12] ]
8. WHY BATS HANG FACE-DOWN
The Bat was a king in his country. He had many many sons and daughters. Cows, goats and
chickens he possessed in great numbers. He ate only meat, which is a luxury only wealthy
men in Africa can afford.
One day Lightning arrived. He too was a king, and he wanted to conclude a treaty of blood-
brotherhood with Bat.
King Bat rejoiced when King Lightning arrived. He treated him regally. He ordered his
servants to slaughter a cow. Even in times of famine cows are seldom slaughtered. The food
was well cooked and Lightning ate ravenously. There were many meat dishes, as befitted
royalty.
King Bat possessed a platter of a type used only by kings. As soon as Lightning saw it, he
coveted it and said: 'My friend, give me that platter. I want nothing else. I will be eternally
grateful to you.'
King Bat declined politely: 'Other things I will give you, anything you want you may ask for,
but this platter is an inheritance. It belongs to the kingship.'
King Lightning's blood began to boil, and he spoke menacing words: 'Do you not prefer to
live in peace? Give that platter to me!' King Bat refused again.
Lightning flashed with anger. He ran up into the clouds, raging. From there, he struck down
on all the possessions of King Bat: his houses and his kraals, his cattle, his goats and
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
13 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 15 ---
chickens, and all his food baskets were destroyed.
Grief gripped King Bat when he saw fire destroy all his wealth. 'Henceforth I will look down
towards the earth. I will never see the sky again. My children will have to live under the
leaves of the trees and under the roofs of caves. From now on, we bats will turn our backs to
heaven, in deep contempt for its greed.'
[Page [13] ]
Since that day, bats can be seen hanging upside down, with their numerous children.
9. WHOSE IS THE CHILD?
The hawk and the robin laid an egg, each in her own nest. The eggs hatched; the hawk had a
black chick, the robin a white one. The robin went out to catch insects for her baby. When
she came back she found a black chick in her nest. During her absence, the hawk had changed
the chicks and taken the nice white chick for herself, giving the ugly black one to the robin.
The robin claimed her child but the hawk would not give it up. They quarrelled, and at last
they decided to bring their case before the king.
The king gave each of the two mothers a cup, and said: 'I am now going to kill the white
chick. The mother whose cup is full of tears is the true mother of the white chick.'
The king slowly raised his knife. The hawk cried out loud. The robin shed tears in silence.
The king inspected the two cups. He found the hawk's cup entirely dry, but the robin's cup
brimmed over with tears.
The king said: 'You hawk, your crying is only noise-making. It is not a sign of true sorrow.
The robin is the mother of the white chick. Tears are the sign of real love.'
The king had only pretended that he was killing the chick. Then he gave it to the robin, who
took it to her nest and was very happy. Since that day, robins can be seen in great numbers,
but only occasionally does one see a hawk, high in the sky.

10. THE TWO BIRD-CATCHERS
Two boys went to the bush and set their snares. One caught a pigeon. The other one was less
fortunate. He caught nothing but
[Page [14] ]
a spider, which he let free. The next day the first boy was again lucky. He caught a fat
guinea-fowl, whereas the other one caught some lightning which had become entangled in the
string. The boy set the lightning free to go back to the sky, for human beings cannot eat
lightning.
The next day, the king called the two boys and told them to cut some grinding stones out of
the rock for him. This is a difficult job which only professional stone-knappers can do. The
grinding stone had to be hewn out of the rock in the proper shape. The boy who had always
been unlucky now called the lightning and asked if it would cut some grinding stones for him
out of the rock. The lightning came and flashed repeatedly until the whole rock was split into
grinding stones. All the people heard it thundering.
Next, the king desired a star from the sky. The boys did not know how to climb up there, but
the unlucky boy asked the spider. The spider had made a huge net that reached from the earth
up to the sky. It climbed up, plucked a star from the night- sky and brought it down for the
boy, who gave it to the king.
The king was delighted with his new star, and he gave the boy many cows and baskets full of
food. So the unlucky boy was now very happy, for he was a rich man. With all those cows he
could obtain a wife, and with the baskets full of millet he could give a party, as the women
could brew beer from it.
So now he had become a man of standing, whereas the lucky hunter had eaten his birds, so he
had nothing. It is a good thing to be generous.
11. THE GIRL WHO W ANTED DA WN'S DRESS
A girl lived in a certain town with her father's sister. She was so beautiful that many young
men came to ask for her hand in marriage, but she answered each of them that she wanted the
red
[Page [15] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
15 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 17 ---
dress of Dawn as her bride-price. So they all went away disappointed, until one young man
decided to consult the girl's aunt. That of course was a very wise decision.
The aunt, however, was an even queerer character than the girl, because instead of good solid
food she ate only dung. All the other young men had made caustic remarks about the pieces
of dirt that were lying there in the mud in front of the old lady's hut. But this young man was
polite and greeted her according to the rules.
She greeted him and said: 'I must go to the field now to pick some fruits. I will come back
presently. Will you guard my food?'
At that she pointed at the heaps of dung lying in her courtyard. The young man said he would,
and sat down to wait for her return. He found the stench repulsive but he reflected that during
his hunting adventures he had survived more dangerous hazards, and after all he was there for
a purpose. Suddenly it started raining. As the courtyard sloped down towards the gate, the
dung would soon be washed away. He remembered having promised that he would look after
it, so he realized he had to take it to safety.
He looked around for something to put it on but saw nothing, and there was no time to
search: it was pouring with rain. So he had to carry all the pieces inside with his bare hands,
as he considered that the broom would damage them. He reflected it would be rude to wash
his hands after his work. After all, it was the lady's food.
When she came back she was most grateful to the young man for having saved her precious
dung from being washed away by the rain. She said: 'Good, now you may go and wash your
hands, and I will prepare you something you like.'
And so she did: it was a dish of millet with ants, which the people there consider a great
delicacy. Then she told the young man to state his business, and when he said he wanted her
advice on how to go about wooing her niece she explained:
'You are a considerate young man, therefore I will help you. Go and tell the girl that she must
sleep with you. But remember: she
[Page [16] ]
must sleep near your feet and you must sleep near her feet. I will give her unground millet.
As soon as the cock crows you must tell her that you want her to cook porridge of it. She will
not refuse.'
The young man was overjoyed at these words, and that night the girl consented to sleep with
him. They lay down in the manner indicated by the old lady. The next morning, when the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
16 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 18 ---
cock crew, the young man said to the girl: 'Now I want you to make my morning porridge, for
I have to go out on a journey.'
She asked: 'Where is the flour I can make porridge with?'
He said: 'I brought you some unground millet. In our country the women can make porridge
of unground millet.'
She said: 'But it must be ground!'
He replied: 'Clever women can do it!'
She actually tried to make porridge. She put the millet in water and cooked it, but of course it
never became porridge. The young man pretended impatience. The sun rose. The girl said:
'How do the women in your country do it? I can't.'
The man replied: 'And you wanted a husband who could bring you Dawn's red garment? Who
has ever heard of that? Of course the women in my country grind their millet properly. Who
has ever heard of porridge made of unground grains? I only told you that to show you how
conceited you are, because you insist on having the dress of Dawn.'
The girl replied: 'I have nothing to say; all my words are finished. I promise to go with you.
Your wise words have conquered me.'
The young man said: 'Humility wins.'
12. DO NOT ASK FOR SUFFERING
An Alur went to pay a visit to his friend, a Gungu; he found him smoking a pipe. Now it is
believed that pipe-smoking gives great wisdom---perhaps because in the winding wisps of
smoke spirits
[Page [17] ]
can be seen, who give fresh ideas to the smokers. The Alur asked: 'Why do you smoke
pipes?'
The Gungu said: 'My friend, you are asking me what suffering is! The thing which makes me
smoke this pipe is the multitude of my worries!'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
17 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 19 ---
The Alur was greatly impressed. He said: 'My friend, I want you to give me some of your
suffering. And a few worries as well!'
The Gungu answered: 'My friend, if you really want some of my suffering, it is very easy to
get. Just send your children along tomorrow to collect it here.'
The next morning the Alur sent four of his children to the house of the Gungu, saying: 'Go to
my friend near the lake and fetch me some suffering.'
The Gungu asked them, when they arrived, what they had come for, and they replied: 'Our
father said you have some misery for him.'
The Gungu went inside, took a blackbird, put it in a box and said: 'Here, take this to your
father.'
The children went home with the box, but on the way they said: 'We want to see our father's
misery!'
They opened the box and at once the blackbird flew out and was gone. One of the boys
accused the others: 'You wanted to see your father's misery, now you have let it escape.' They
started a terrific fight, and in a few hours they were dead, all four of them.
The father waited and waited. At last he set out on the road, and soon he found his four
children, all lying dead along the roadside. His grief was great. The Gungu came along the
road to see his Alur friend. He found him in deep distress.
The Gungu asked: 'Why do you have sorrow?'
The Alur answered: 'The children I sent to you came along this road; they threw away my
misery. They must have started quarrelling, and they have killed each other; none remain.'
The Gungu man said: 'My friend, one does not ask for suffering.
[Page [18] ]
You asked for suffering, and look at you now: it has totally overwhelmed you.'
This is what the elders tell their children these days: 'Remember, fear those who smoke
pipes, for their brains are big, they have much cunning. The pipes they smoke bring sad
circumstances back to their memories.'
Even more can be learned from this beautiful story, my children: do not ask for something
you do not know about. Do not think it must be wonderful if you do not know it. And do not
open any box unless your father gives you permission.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
18 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 20 ---
13. THE HORNBILL AND THE LARK
Arum, the hornbill, and Ulando, the lark, were disputing with each other as to who was the
best singer in the kingdom. They decided to go to the Court together and let the king decide.
The hornbill said: 'You are an insignificant little bird; of course you will lose not only the
contest, but your wives as well. They will all come to me.'
They arrived before the king and stated their business. The king agreed to be adjudicator in
their song-contest. He told the hornbill to begin. The hornbill sang:
When I am all dressed up in pearls,
Do I not equal kings and earls?
The king said: Enough! Now I want to hear the lark.
The lark sang thus:
If only I could drink the royal water,
In which the king has washed his shining body!
If only I could lick the skins of leopards,
With which the king has girt his shining body!
The king was well pleased with this song of praise and concluded
[Page [19] ]
that the lark had easily won the contest. A goat was slaughtered for the lark, but for the
hornbill it was only a castrated dog. For the hornbill, the king ordered his Lendu slave-girls to
cook sorghum porridge; for the lark, the king's own daughters cooked porridge of good clean
millet. As a good-bye present, the hornbill received twenty dogs; the lark was given ten
goats, ten sheep and ten cows.
On their way back they had to cross the river, which had swollen dangerously.
The hornbill said: 'You, my friend lark, are small, and evidently incapable of guiding all your
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
19 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 21 ---
thirty animals across. Leave them to me, I will take them across first, then come back for
you.'
So the hornbill took the cattle across, leaving the dogs with the lark, but he never came back.
He went straight to his house and told all the villagers that he had won the contest against the
lark, so that he was now a wealthy man. His wives welcomed him as a great hero. When the
wives of the lark heard this they decided to join them. They hastily packed their belongings
and moved to the compound of Mr Hornbill, because, they said: 'A man with many cattle and
sheep is rich and we want to live with a rich man, not with a poor man.'
The lark was still waiting on the bank of the river when a messenger of the king arrived there,
and helped him across. They came to the village of the hornbill, and the king's messenger
told the people that the lark, not the hornbill, had found favour with the king. The lark
handed the dogs over to the hornbill and drove his cattle, his sheep, and his goats to his own
kraal. Of course his wives went with him.
The hornbill was left behind, with no beasts in his kraal except dogs. His own wives then
deserted him and went over to the lark's compound because, they said: 'We do not want to
live with a pauper, we want to eat meat with our porridge.'
The women all went to live with the lark, who was now a wealthy man with influence
because of his many relatives.
[Page [20] ]
This story contains many lessons. The first is that a small person may have a good brain: it
may not be sensible to dispute with him. Secondly, a king, or a chief dislikes rivals or men
who think they are equals. It is with sweet words of praise that a man catches favours.
Thirdly, you can learn from this story that women are fickle and not loyal to their husbands.
They want a comfortable life, and so they go where the wealth is.
14. THE ELDEST SISTER OF THE GIRAFFES
In the old days, all the women wore 'tails', 
Upocho , made of bark-cloth, reeds, or unworked
cotton lint. A wino , a real tail of an elephant, buffalo, or giraffe, was a priceless ornament for
a girl. It showed not only that she had mighty hunters in the family , but it was also highly
prized as a charm which gave the wearer the power of the elephant, the energy of the buffalo,
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
20 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 22 ---
or the speed and elegance of the giraffe---the latter qualities being especially appreciated by
girls who wanted to excel at dancing. Decorated with coloured beads, the tail was a symbol of
fertility. Swishing to and fro over their buttocks, it was a maddening sight for all eager young
men.
The giraffe is the ideal metaphor for the girl: soft-skinned and long-necked, it strides over the
savannah as if on a dance floor. Giraffes can easily keep up with a car driving as fast as the
uneven savannah allows. Which man would not be proud of conquering such an elegant
animal and offering its tail to the 'fastest' girl?
Okiro had a sister, whose prospective husband had paid the whole bride-price for her. So,
Okiro told his sister: 'I want you to go and join your husband. From now on you belong to
him.'
The girl said she would go only if he gave her a present.
'Do you want a brass bracelet?'
'No!'
'Do you want a goat?'
'No!'
[Page [21] ]
'Do you want a loin-skirt, ornamented with strings of beads? Do you realize that such an
apaya is worth a cow?'
'I do not want one.'
'Then what do you want?'
'I want the tail of a giraffe. I do not wish to go to my husband without a wino , the brush of a
giraffe-tail.'
Okiro went to consult the diviner, who gave his advice: 'When you arrive in the village of the
giraffes, remain behind their house, let them sleep till midnight. Open the door and blow the
fire so that you may see the big sister of the giraffes. Cut off her tail, then run away.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
21 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 23 ---
Okiro set out for the village of the giraffes and waited till dark behind the house. The female
giraffes (in this language giraffes are always feminine) went to bed and soon they were
snoring. He blew into the fire so that it sparkled and he could see the biggest girl-giraffe. He
quickly cut off her tail and ran for his life. It was still dark when he arrived in the hut of his
own sister. He gave her the tail-trophy and told her to go and sleep with her husband at once.
She did.
The next morning, the giraffe girls rose early and went out to sweep the yard, singing a song
of their own:
Who has to sweep the yard and make it all tidy?
The robin's tail must sweep the yard and make it tidy.
The turtle-dove's tail must sweep the yard and tidy it.
Suddenly the eldest sister noticed that her tail was missing. She began to cry: 'They have
stolen my tail! Where shall we find the man who cut it off?'
The other long-necked girls saw the stump and were very cross. 'Don't cry, we will go and
find out!'
They went out on the road and met some cowherds, and asked to hear their songs of triumph.
The cowherds shouted: 'Hit! We are the sons of Ogwaro, the killer of buffalo bulls.'
The giraffes knew that these were not the men they wanted
[Page [22] ]
They walked on for a long time until they saw some men cultivating. The men's cry was:
'Stand fast! We are the sons of Ulango, the strong Tree!'
The giraffes travelled on until they met some men who were playing undilo , which is a kind
of golf. Their cry of triumph, however, was: 'We are the men of Okiro!'
Okiro himself, who was in their midst, could not help shouting excitedly: 'Okiro the strong
man, who cut the tail off the biggest giraffe!'
When the giraffes heard that, they knew they had found the man they were looking for. They
all surrounded Okiro and shouted joyfully: 'Here is our man, here is the bridegroom!'
And to Okiro: 'You cut our sister's tail, now we want you to become her husband.' Okiro
agreed, not knowing that the giraffes hid their real intentions in the inside of their livers.
They said: 'Okiro, we want you to accompany us. We want someone to help us find some
firewood.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
22 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 24 ---
Okiro said: 'I will send one of my men with you who knows the place.'
They said: 'We refuse, we wish to go with you and no one else.'
It was then that Okiro realized that he was in danger of his life. He possessed twenty dogs,
nineteen big ones and a small one. He told his mother: 'When I am out with the giraffes, if
you see a leaf falling into your flour, then kill a cow for the dogs, tell them to eat their fill,
then send them out to find me.'
Okiro set out with the giraffe-girls. They found a tree. Okiro said: 'Why don't you chop this
one down?'
They refused, saying they wanted only the best wood. The truth was, they wanted to get him
as far away from home as possible. Therefore they refused another tree, and another. Finally,
they saw a very tall tree called tido , which has a very slender and smooth stem.
The giraffes said to Okiro: 'Go and climb that tree, we want some of the top branches of it.'
[Page [23] ]
Okiro said: 'Give me my axe!'
They said: 'You climb up there first, then we will give you the axe.'
When he was high up, they began to cut the tree, singing:
Chop, dear little axe, chop!
Hit the tree that we may eat!
Chop, dear little adze, chop!
Fell it so that we may be satisfied!
As soon as the tree began to stagger, a lark flew out of its foliage, singing: ' 
Rititi! My dear
tree! Do not let my children die! Stand back where you were!'
The tree regained its composure and steadied itself. Okiro saw that nevertheless its end was
near, so he sent off a leaf. The leaf fell far away, and right in the flour his mother was
grinding. When she saw it, she slaughtered a cow and let the dogs eat it. Then she told them:
'Go and find out what is happening to the master!'
The dogs ran. As soon as Okiro saw his dogs approaching, he began to sing:
Bay ones, bay ones!
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
23 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 25 ---
Save me from the buff ones!
By the buff ones he meant, of course, the giraffes. He set the dogs on the giraffes, who were
all killed in a few minutes. Okiro climbed down from the tree.
The dogs asked him: 'We saved you, what will you give us?'
Okiro said: 'What do you mean? You are all my own dogs.'
They replied: 'Without a reward we cannot save you!'
Okiro promised them a goat, then a cow, but they refused. He offered them all his
possessions, but they still refused. They said: 'We just want to devour you yourself.'
Okiro replied: 'So be it. Go on, eat me!'
So Okiro was devoured by his own dogs. The corpses of the giraffes were left lying on the
ground. The dogs returned home by
[Page [24] ]
themselves. When they were asked by Okiro's men what had happened to their master, they
answered simply: 'We saved him, then we ate him.'
At once the dogs were locked up in a pen by Okiro's relatives, who gave them a good hiding,
saying: 'We will give you no more food and plenty of beating until you throw up all that you
have eaten. We will kill you dogs if you don't return Okiro.'
So the dogs said: 'We will vomit our master.'
The dogs went behind the house and began to vomit out Okiro. One surrendered an arm, his
fellow another arm. Two others threw up his legs, one a whole head. The parts of the body
reassembled themselves. But they could not come to life or move, for his liver was still
absent, which one small dog had gobbled up. The others said to him: 'We want you to vomit
out the liver, just as we have returned the other parts.' So the small dog gave up the liver. It
returned to its place. The body was whole. Okiro stirred, then rose to his feet.
The lesson of this story? Never give in to women or dogs, for that will mean your end. They
must never be allowed to be self- willed.
15. ALA AND THE OLD HAG
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
24 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 26 ---
A man's strength is of vital importance to him. He can lose it if someone bewitches him, like
the old hag in this story. He has to incorporate the meat, that is the vital forces, of six oxen,
before his strength is restored.
Two young men were on their way to a dancing party. They were singing and full of good
cheer. Suddenly there appeared an old woman who begged them: 'Please take me on your
backs!'
One of the men said: 'I won't, you smell and you have dirty wounds!' The other one, his name
was Ala, had pity on her, and took her on his back. As soon as the old witch had settled down
on his back, she began to pinch him and to push her dirty feet into
[Page [25] ]
his sides. His whole body ached and he wandered about aimlessly, for she was beginning to
get him in her power. After several hours, hunger gnawed his stomach and he lamented his
fate. Thirst, too, visited him; it settled in his throat and made it ache.
At last they came to a river. Ala said: 'Old woman, climb down, let us drink!' But she said:
'You can drink with me on your back.' So he had to keep her on his back all the time.
After many hours, he found a dead buffalo, lying in the grass. Ala said: 'Woman, come down,
let us skin this animal. Let me make a fire so we can cook the meat and eat it.' She said: 'Go
on, skin it with me on your back!' At last he asked: 'Don't you want to eat?' She replied: 'Yes,
and you will put some good strips of meat for me on your shoulders.'
Ala went on wandering about in the savannah with the woman on his back for three months.
Finally he fell down and lay on the ground. The old hag stepped off his back and looked at
him closely, for a long time. When she was at last satisfied that he was dead, she went away.
Ala got up and ran, ran for his life and freedom. She turned and saw him go, but it was too
late, she could not overtake him. She took off her legs and arms and rolled behind him, like a
boulder rolling down a river-bed, when the rains have come.
Ala arrived home. His sister saw him and called out: 'Mother, Ala has come back!' Their
mother said: 'Do not joke with a mourning mother.' It was Ala himself, only he was very
weak. They killed an ox which he ate, then another, then another, six in all, until finally his
strength had returned.
Suddenly the old hag appeared and claimed her mount. They treated her politely, and gave
her a stool, asking her to wait until her steed would be ready. In the meantime they quickly
dug a pit, and covered it with grass. Then they put a cauldron of water to heat over the fire.
When all was ready, they said: 'Wouldn't you like to have a wash?' The old woman went
across the patch where fresh grass had been put out---to dry, apparently, for thatching. A
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
25 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 27 ---
[Page [26] ]
well-mannered person would not have walked across it. However, she did, and as the pit lay
beneath it, she fell down into it. Quickly they poured the hot water over her so that she died.
Then they filled in the earth on top of her.
Ala's body regained its strength gradually. Everyone agreed that the old woman had been a
dangerous witch who had sucked out his strength. Ala had been lucky to escape.
16. ROM
A man's vital power is in his genitals, and by embracing a woman he plants the seed of a
future generation. He who embraces his own mother loses his power by sending it back to
where it came from.
The young man Rom has to die as a man, to be reborn as a plant, to be uprooted and to be
preserved in a preserving jar, before he can be born again as a man. He is then a new person,
the son of the jar that gave birth to him, and the plant that was pushed into the mouth of the
jar, and which transformed itself into a new man.
A young man called Rom was at a dance, where the boys and the girls dance opposite one
another in two rows. The girl opposite Rom tapped him on the head with her hand, as a sign
that she wanted him. The next evening, Rom sat down near the door of his hut, with his
adungu guitar, to sing a song of invitation. It was a song full of longing, which, Rom hoped,
would inspire the girl with equal longing for him, so that she would come to his hut that night
and give herself to him. After all, she had started the affair already.
In the darkness, he saw a female figure approach, all dressed up as a girl would be when
going out to meet her lover. She went into his hut with him, and that night they slept together
in his bed. The next morning, when dawn entered the hut, Rom saw that the woman he had
slept with was his mother. She had heard his
[Page [27] ]
melancholy songs, and she knew the girl was not coming. Longing to make love with her own
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
26 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 28 ---
son had overcome her, and she had dressed herself like a young girl going out to meet her
lover.
Rom was horrified. He could not reproach his own mother: does the chick rebuke the hen?
So he took his adungu and composed a little song:
I entered the lap I came out of,
My strength went back where it came from.
He took ten hunting spears from his hut and went out into the bush. He found a tall tree and
planted his ten spears in the ground, bunched together, with the blades pointing upwards.
Then he climbed the tree, and from the highest branch threw himself down on to the blades.
He died at once. There his body hung, on his own spears. He had atoned for his sin of incest
by killing his body.
The rains came. Sheets of rain poured down, the earth was washed away , the spears fell over,
the body was washed down into a hole and covered with mud. The green season came, when
ten thousand plants grow up. Out of the muddy hole grew an evergreen tree. An old woman
passed by and wanted to pick the leaves for her vegetable soup, but the tree cried: 'Do not
dismember me! Pull me out whole and take me with you, please!'
Surprised and frightened that a tree could talk, the old woman obeyed. She wanted to lay the
tree in the sunshine in her yard, so that she would have dried leaves for vegetables for many
days. But the tree cried: 'Please do not dry me out, I shall die of thirst. Rather put me in a jar
and shut it!'
The old woman thought this a good idea, as it is customary in that region to pickle vegetables
in a jar. So she crammed the tree into her largest jar, added water and pickle, and put a flat
stone on the top, to seal it. Inside the jar, the vegetable tree began to ferment, and was
transformed into the old Rom.
One morning, when the old woman was just entering her
[Page [28] ]
store-hut, she was struck by an unexpected sight: the flat stone lifted itself from the jar, and
there appeared the face of a handsome young man. The woman was speechless, but the young
man stepped out of the jar, smiled, and said: 'Good morning.' He looked strong and healthy,
but he was naked, so the woman went out and found a piece of cloth for him to cover his
loins with.
Soon the whole district had heard the news that in the old lady's store-hut a vegetable jar had
borne a very handsome young man, who had been a tree in a previous existence. They all
came to see him and brought him presents, hoping to propitiate him, for it is evident that only
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
27 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 29 ---
a powerful spirit can be born as the son of a tree and a jar. When all the people were together
they decided unanimously to acclaim him as king of their country. His arrival in their midst
had been a miraculous one; it showed that he possessed the secret of victory over death,
which is the greatest power a man can have. It made him fit to be a king.
17. MOLA
The underworld, the land under the earth where the dead people go, has a mysterious
fascination for all the peoples on earth. There are various ways of entering it: by jumping into
a lake, or by straying into a cave. The dead enter it when they are interred, the souls of the
deceased live there a life similar to that on earth. A great chief on earth may look forward to a
life as chief in the land below. He will have cattle, servants and wives. If he so wishes, he
may appear as a lion, visible to terrestrials, and help his children's children with sudden
problems and emergencies. People are both terrified when they meet him and grateful to him,
just as a little child is awed and thrilled on hearing father's voice. The lion frightens us but he
is also a member of the family.
Jealousy among co-wives is one of the commonest themes for story-tellers in Africa, since
polygamy is dominant everywhere. The wife who feels neglected by the shared husband may
begin to nurse
[Page [29] ]
a grudge against the other women in the household. She may in her despair, turn to
witchcraft.
Witchcraft can be performed by means of magical objects, or by the power of words. 'Singing'
is sometimes used in African languages as a euphemistic term for 'bewitching'. The absent-
minded humming of old women may be in reality the casting of a spell on an unsuspecting
victim. And also the envious wife sings her husband into the earth, in other words, she sends
him to the land of the dead. But witchcraft has its limits; it is not easy to kill a good chief.
Chief Mola finds himself in the illusive land of the spirits, where nothing keeps its form. His
father rules there in the shape of a lion. The one thing which the dead desire most, in their
shivery abode of dripping forest and toadstools, is fire. Fire warms chilly spirits: as we know
from Greek mythology, Ulysses lit a fire in the underworld and saw the ghosts crouch around
it, hankering for warmth.
Many beautiful features in these stories and songs result from word-play. The word 
Mola
means 'copper bracelet' as well as 'Lord,' so that when the third wife sings: 'I am my Mola's
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
28 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 30 ---
mola,' she is really saying: 'I am my Lord's bracelet.' The implication is, of course, erotic.
Similarly the word for 'game' also means 'meat' and 'animals', so when the guards accuse Mola
of stealing the king's (mushroom-) meat, they are using the word that also means 'animals'.
Mola's father, the lion, who rules the underworld, gives his son 'animals' to keep in the world
of mortals. It also means he gives him 'flesh', i.e. a human body , which we all need in this
world. He thus receives life from his father for the second time. In the third wife's final song,
all three motifs return: the husband's resurrection, the fire in the family hearth, and the
awakening lion.
Mola had two wives. The first wife was faithful and had borne him children. The second was
a fickle woman, greedy and jealous. Now Mola had taken a third wife, who admired him as a
great chief. She was so devoted to him that she praised him even while she worked.
Whatever she was doing, grinding, pounding, or harvesting, she would praise his name in
songs like this one:
[Page [30] ]
Mola my husband is great like my father.
Mola my master, a lion in the hills.
When he is away, I scan the hill slopes.
His strength crushes me like the eagle crushing a buck's shoulder- blade.
I am the field for his hoe, the soil waiting for the first rains.
I hang like a liana from the mighty branches of a forest-giant.
I find shelter in his presence like the velvet monkey in the thick foliage of the mahogany
tree.
I am my Mola's 
mola , I fit round his strong arm.
I am like the bats hanging from the ceiling of the caves in the rocks.
He is the rock that no spear can kill ...
The second wife was annoyed with these praises for a husband who did not, she felt, give her
enough presents. What was worse, the singing pleased her husband so much that he lavished
most of his attentions on the third wife. So the second wife decided to sing him a song of her
own.
Mola, my brother, be silent in the mountain ...

--- PAGE 31 ---
It contained many more lines that no one could understand. Mola began to sink into the
ground, like a tree in a flood. At last he disappeared altogether, and earth covered his head.
The first and third wives began to wail loudly.
Mola found himself in the land below. He travelled along a path until he came to a copse. He
decided to build a house there, but as soon as he started cutting a tree, all the trees began to
cry for help. So he walked on.
Then he came to a forest where he found hundreds of mushrooms. He collected a large
number until he was suddenly surrounded by the king's soldiers, who accused him of stealing
the royal herd. He said: 'I was only collecting mushrooms.' He opened his bag and found
animals. He emptied his bag and the animals turned into mushrooms again. He looked at the
soldiers but he saw only trees.
[Page [31] ]
He went on and met an old woman. She said: 'Guard my millet field while I am away. But do
not try to catch the birds, just chase them by shouting.' She vanished, and at once a flock of
orange birds descended and began to eat the millet. He tried to catch one, but as soon as he
touched it all the birds turned into flames that consumed the millet. He burnt his fingers and
went away. Behind a bush he met a great lion who told him: 'You are trespassing in my
kingdom, but if you bring me the fire, I will give you half of my cattle.' Mola went on and
found Lightning, who was sitting in an old tree. He concluded a pact of friendship with
Lightning, who gave him a flaming torch. Mola brought the torch to King Lion, who was so
pleased that he gave him many cows.
Suddenly, he heard the voice of his mother crying and complaining: 'Oh my son, who always
worked for me, he used to hoe my fields! Alas for his mother! Who will now harvest my
millet for me? I am old and weak!'
When Mola heard this he asked permission of the lion to return to his own country, because
his old mother urgently needed help with the crops. The lion agreed that this was a valid
reason and called his guards to guide Mola to the door. The guards showed him a cave and
Mola drove his cattle through the narrow opening. He found himself in the bushes at the foot
of the hill not far from his own village. He drove the cows to his own kraal.
There was great rejoicing in his compound; when the children saw him they raced out to
meet him. The hunting dogs yelped around him. The news soon spread: Mola has come back

from the underworld a rich man. His mother said: 'Chase away that evil woman who
bewitched you.' The villagers stoned the second wife. The third wife took her nanga , a
stringed instrument, and sang:
My husband has come back from the land of graves.
He lives again like fire in the morning,
The fire that is lit when dawn is grey,
When all the people shiver in the dew,
[Page [32] ]
The flames spring up among the smouldering logs,
The family feels warm and comforted,
The brilliant sparks will cause the pot to sing,
So that the water bubbles like a brook,
And soon the meal is ready for the men.
My husband brought home the cattle of his father
The lion king who rules the land of manes.
Now will the babies drink the lukewarm milk
And run along the paths with shining skins.
My husband is the tree whom lightning struck,
But soon the shoots and saplings reappeared,
And fresh green leaves adorned the supple twigs,
The power to live still lingers in the trunk,
Invincible, it reaches for the sky,
The rising stem with new and shining bark,
The heavy branches spreading like a roof,
An awning where the women sit and sing
While making baskets, sheltered from the sun.
My husband had a-risen from his sleep,
The lion wakes, stretching his lazy claws,
With their well-whetted nails like millet-knives.
He has been dozing on a hot afternoon,
When only sparrows twitter in the bush,
Now he is yawning so that the branches shake,
Now he is roaring and the far hills answer.
18. ZANGU AND THE LION
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
31 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 33 ---
In the story of Zangu the lion appears again as the principal agent of death and resurrection.
The saviour, the character who rescues others from death, becomes their owner. They were
dead; now in their new lives they have nowhere to go, they stay with their rescuer and make
him their king. A man who has killed a lion is as desirable as a wealthy man to have as
brother-in-law. And a good hunter's wife is never without meat.
[Page [33] ]
Ajulu ('Crown Crane') was the name of a lovely girl. Her brother told her: 'Go and guard the
millet!' She said: 'What do I do if the lion appears?' He said: 'I will build you a platform to sit
on.' There she sat all day, shouting at the birds who came in flocks to eat the millet. At last
the lion appeared and attacked her. He was an enormous animal. He rose on his hind legs and
reached across the platform with his front claws. The girl shrieked and cried for help, but her
brother was nowhere to be seen.
In the bush there was a small hut in which Zangu lived with his old mother. They had no
cattle, so that he could not get a bride. When he heard Ajulu crying he came running. Though
he ran faster than the redbuck, the lion could eat faster still. When Zangu arrived, the lion had
gone, with Ajulu in his stomach.
Zangu took his spear and his matchet, and started out in search of the lion. He found him
asleep lying on a rock. He took his spear and slit the lion's throat. He opened the lion's
stomach carefully with his hunter's knife, and out stepped Ajulu, breathing the fresh evening
breeze. And out came also many other people, women and children, whom the lion had
devoured during his life.
They all praised Zangu as a hero and acclaimed him as their chief. They decided to settle
there and they built their village near the rock. Ajulu's brother agreed that Zangu should
marry her, for he had saved her from death.
19. NYARUPOKO
The more one learns about African languages and African ways of thinking, the more one
realizes how well each language fits the culture to which it belongs. What seems to be a mere
pun when one first learns the double meaning of a word, later acquires a new dimension,
when one learns the deeper implications of these meanings in the culture of the people.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
32 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 34 ---
The knife, as in other cultures, is a symbol of marriage. It is
[Page [34] ]
composed of two parts: the male part is the iron blade which fits with a pin in the wooden
shaft. 1
The pot is also a symbol, more particularly a symbol of woman. When the pot is being used
to fetch water for the young man, it is doing wifely duty, for that is what a woman does for
her husband. Pots are kept on shelves under the roofs of the huts. Stores of meat and millet
are also kept there, as well as all the secrets of the family, such as heirlooms, treasures and
charms. Most pots are made of calabashes or gourds, and it is supposed by some that these
fruits retain their magical power to produce life. Like Rom, Nyarupoko lives in a pot in
vegetative condition, but with the possibility of one day regaining her original human form,
when she is loved.
The lark 
Ulando is the messenger of Alur mythology because it can sing so deceptively that
its tones resemble those of African languages which are all tonal.
Ten girls set out on a trip round several villages to see if they could find themselves suitable
husbands. They arrived in the first village and were greeted by the boys, who said: ' Morembe
(peace and prosperity) to all of you, but most of all to that beautiful girl who walks behind!'
The nine girls who walked in front were angry and decided to punish that one girl for her
beauty, and so changed her into a knife.
The girl who usually walked in front put the new knife in her girdle, and after that they
marched on in single file to the next village. There, the boys greeted them: ' 
Morembe to all
nine of you and also to that beautiful shining knife you have!' The girls marched on and in the
bush they changed the knife into an upoko . An upoko is a round calabash used for milk or
water.
[Page [35] ]
In the next village the girls found nine bridegrooms willing to marry them. The boys' mothers
took the girls home. There was a tenth woman who found only a calabash. She took it home
with her for her son to drink water from.
The next morning everyone went out to work in the fields. Only a little girl stayed in the hut.

33 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 35 ---
Suddenly she saw a maiden come out of the calabash, and jump down from the shelf on
which the pots were placed. She began to grind corn, singing a song:
I am Nyar-upoko, the daughter of the calabash,
go---go---grind flour;
I am Nyar-upoko, the girl of the gourd,
come---come---cook porridge.
I am Nyar-upoko, the woman in the water-fruit
I was thrown away.
She made the porridge, called the little girl, and together they ate their midday meal.
That night, when the people came back, the little girl told her mother and elder brother what
she had seen: 'There is a maiden in the house who lives in that calabash, and her name is
Nyarupoko!'
When her brother heard this he decided to hide and see for himself. The next morning he
pretended to prepare for a day in the fields, but he returned stealthily and hid himself near the
door. He saw a beautiful girl emerge from the calabash on the shelf, jump down and start to
sweep the floor. Then she took a jar, went out to the well to fetch water, and came back,
walking gracefully with the jar on her head. She put water on the fire, ground the millet and
cooked porridge. Then she sat down with the boy's little sister and together they ate their
midday meal, chattering cheerfully.
After the meal, Nyarupoko put away the remainder for the boy's mother. She was on the point
of jumping back into her calabash when the young man sprang up from his hiding-place
[Page [36] ]
and embraced her. She wanted to slide out of his arms and escape but he held her tightly and
whispered: 'I want you to be my wife!' So she stayed in her human shape.
When the other people in the village heard about this, the young men reproached their
mothers: 'Why did you not take the calabash rather than the bickering bride you took for me?'
The nine young women, on hearing this, decided to dispose of the beautiful girl again, this
time once and for all. They persuaded her to go to the woods with them, saying they wanted
her to help them collect firewood. When they came to a marsh, they suddenly seized her and
dumped her amidst the bulrushes, pushing her deep down, covering her with mud and thick
swamp-grass. No one had seen them, except the grey lark, 
Ulando . He flew to the village
and perched on a rooftop while everyone was in the village square. He sang so that all could
hear:
Young man take your fishing rod,

--- PAGE 36 ---
Take your friends with nets and dip-nets,
Follow Ulando to the marshes,
I will show a place for fishing,
You will catch a lovely treasure.
The young men of the village listened to the bird who sang the tones of their language so that
they could understand him. They began at once to prepare for a fishing expedition. They
followed the bird to the marshlands. There the bird perched on a bulrush stalk and sang:
Nyarupoko, the calabash's daughter
Nine girls threw her here into the water.
The men started searching for the girl, but her bridegroom waded to the bulrush where the
bird was sitting, and groped around its roots. There he felt a breast, an arm, he pulled and
lugged until the girl emerged, alive and healthy. Nyarupoko and the young man were very
happy, but the nine wicked girls were chased away from the village for ever.
[Page [37] ]
20. AJULU AND THE WICKED LEPER
Since Biblical times leprosy has been regarded as a punishment from the gods. Lepers are
therefore to be feared, for they are evil-minded. Especially, they can harm people with their
evil eyes. People's physical forms may disappear, but often their voices are permitted to
linger on; babies can be heard crying, and women singing, who were drowned a long time
ago.
The cows that the bridegroom has to pay to his father-in-law retain a mysterious relationship
with the young wife. Beef is eaten only in times of extreme famine, and at solemn
ceremonies, so that one could say that cows are sacred among the Nilotes. One of the cows is
formally presented by the bridegroom at the ceremony at which he receives his bride from
the hands of her father. It is this cow, usually a nice fat one, that is in a mystic way identified
with the bride. It made marriage possible and legal for her; it now pulls the young wife back
to life out of the river.
Once upon a time there were two sisters. Their names were Akuru (Turtle-dove) and Ajulu
(Crown Crane). Once they were pounding the millet together, when the king happened to
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
35 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 37 ---
pass by. He heard them singing in unison, and felt pleased in his heart. He went to see their
father to arrange for a marriage, but as the two girls did not like the prospect of being
separated, he agreed to marry them both. After all, kings are never short of cattle. He sent
some of his men to drive a herd of cows to his father-in-law. After the customary exchange of
presents and the festive meal, the two sisters followed their royal husband to his kraal.
Within a year, both girls presented the king with a son. Soon, the babies were big enough to
be carried in the ubendo , the baby basket in which women carry their babies on their backs.
It is customary at about that time for young mothers to go back to their parents to show off
their new babies to the people at home. They took a pot of porridge each, as provision for the
journey. Then they set out on the path to their village, Akuru in front and Ajulu
[Page [38] ]
behind. Near the river they saw a leprous beggar who begged some food from them. They
gave him a few lumps of their porridge, then went on.
The beggar followed silently. Suddenly he seized Ajulu and threw her in the river. He took
her porridge and ate it all. The next day the king's cowboys, who were watering their cattle,
heard a baby crying but they could not see it. That night they came back. They heard the child
again and they decided to search for it. When they came close to the river-bank, it had gone
completely dark, and the moon was rising. Suddenly they heard a soft voice singing a lullaby,
close by, but no one could be seen:
Dry your tender tears my baby,
I am coming in the moonshine,
When the moon-beams pierce the water,
See your mother is a-waking,
Baby in the tall grass crying,
Wait, your mother's milk is coming,
It is eager to be given.
Suddenly they saw, very hazily, a young woman rise out of the river and walk in the direction
of the crying. There, she lay down and suckled her baby.
The boys hurried to drive the cattle home. The king was on the point of punishing them, for it
is dangerous to bring the cows home after sunset. But when they told him what they had
seen, he was deeply worried. He sent his guards to protect the baby against the wild beasts,
but when they arrived at the river-crossing there was no trace of the baby or of his mother.
The king sent a messenger to the girls' father, who devised a plan. At dawn, he drove the
cows which he had received from the king as the bride-price for Ajulu to the river crossing.
When the cows stood in the river, the father called his daughter: 'Ajulu, Ajulu, if you are truly
Ajulu, let your cows pull you out of the
