[Page [39] ]
water!' And lo! A hand emerged from the water and seized the tail of the biggest cow. The
father drove the cow back on to the bank and so he won his daughter back. She went at once
and found her baby in the tall grass, for which mother cannot find her baby?
The king had the beggar arrested and executed.
21. SHE W ANTED TO MARRY ONLY A KING
The elephant and the leopard are both 'royal' animals, but in a different sense. The elephant is
the biggest, strongest and wisest of the animals and so symbolizes the wise and unperturbed
king. The leopard symbolizes the more flashy type of princeling. As a chief--- and there are
many of that type---he is greedy, ruthless and cruel. The type of ruler who thinks he owns his
people and can do what he likes with them is never loved. The ant-eater will turn up again in
other parts of the Congo. He is the underground ally of man. His capacity to dig tunnels
through the earth shows that he has mysterious powers, which he no doubt obtained in the
land below, the land of death and resurrection.
Awara was a very beautiful girl; she was so famous that many important men came to ask for
her hand in marriage. The Buffalo arrived first. Her father received the mighty visitor with
courtesy, and sent Awara's younger sister, Yenge, to find the girl. Awara sent back word that
she was not interested in marrying Mr Buffalo. 'Let him go. I am not coming!'
The next suitor to arrive was the Elephant. A wara's father received the dignified guest with
the utmost politeness. Yenge was sent to fetch Awara, but she came back saying: 'Awara does
not feel that she wants to see Mr Elephant.' So the Elephant left the house, most indignant.
The next suitor was no lesser personality than His Highness,
[Page [40] ]
Prince Leopard. This time the girl was eager to marry. Arrangements were made, and soon
Awara followed Prince Leopard to his royal kraal. Yenge was allowed to go with her, to keep
her company. Listen to what follows!
Awara did not know that leopards are mean and cruel. As soon as they arrived at the leopards'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
37 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 39 ---
kraal, the leopards secretly prepared two cauldrons to cook the girls in and have a good meal
of feminine flesh. Yenge, however, was clever enough to see through their plans. She called
the Ant-eater, who was an old friend of her father. The Ant-eater emerged from the floor of
the hut in which the two girls were locked up, waiting until the fires were hot enough to cook
them on. Yenge pleaded: 'Please, Mr Ant-eater, dig a hole to our father's kraal.'
The Ant-eater promised he would. He was a blood-brother of their father and digging tunnels
was his daily work. So he dug a tunnel under the ground and guided the girls back to their
home. They were saved. Girls, remember! Rich young men are cruel. Do not turn down a
good man.
22. THE BAMBOO TOWER
This story is an old Congolese version of the tale of the tower of Babel. The same story is
found among the Bakongo and the Baluba.
The Lendu are very stupid people, say the Alur. One night as they gazed at the stars, they
suddenly arrived at a very important conclusion, so they ran and communicated it to their
king: 'We have realized that all those lights up there must prove that it is a prosperous
country , in which many people can afford to keep their fires burning all night. We would
therefore advise Your Majesty to build a tower up to the sky so that we can go and learn from
those people.'
The king ordered that thousands of bamboo poles be cut, and
[Page [41] ]
hauled up to the highest hill in his realm. The first poles were dug into the ground, and
cross-beams were tied to them with lianas. On top of this structure further storeys were
erected, with diagonal poles and transverse beams for reinforcement. The king kept control of
progress on top so that he rose up with every new storey. He wanted to be the first to set foot
on the sky.
The chief minister, Ledza, was in charge at the bottom end. He directed the transportation of
supplies. After nine months he noticed that the bamboo poles at the bottom were beginning
to rot. The whole structure was beginning to sway unsteadily. With great courage and
perseverance, minister Ledza climbed up all the stairs and ladders that had been constructed
in those nine months, to see the king and warn him that his tower was tottering. The king
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
38 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 40 ---
merely said: 'Ledza, you are a liar, go back!' He could not be convinced that something had to
be done, as he was constantly looking up at the sky, expecting all the time to reach it with the
next storey. 'Go back to your duties, don't bother me with the problems of the earth, don't you
see I am trying to meet God?'
The minister Ledza gave up, climbed down and ran away just in time. The whole tower
tottered and collapsed in one terrifying crash, 
ndundu! All the builders perished and only the
minister Ledza escaped.
Today there is still a lake on Mount Ju, surrounded by bamboo. It bears witness to the lack of
intelligence of the Lendu people, according to the Alur.
23. THE SON OF GOD
Why do white people succeed? They must surely have a spirit or a divine power to help
them.
The phrase 'son of God' could also be translated 'a child of the Spirit'. Mondo , the white man,
includes the Arabs.
[Page [42] ]
The ancestor of the Alur was travelling along one day, when he found the son of God lying
dead near the road. He turned his head away and passed by, without so much as covering the
body with some leaves.
The ancestor of the white men came along the same path a little later. He stopped and started
collecting 
akila leaves, which have a very pleasant smell and are placed on the bodies of the
dead to honour them. He covered the body entirely with the leaves, as a respectful son would
do with his father's body.
God saw it all. He called the black man and the white man together and gave each of them a
weapon for hunting. The black man received the bow and arrows; the white man received the
gun and the lightning-powder, so that he could conquer all the other peoples.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
39 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 41 ---
24. THE BROTHERS' FEUD
Stories 24-28 are narrated as true history.
The brothers' feud is the foundation myth of the Alur people; it belongs to the secret history
known only to the elders.
The right-bank corner of the Nile is still called 'Wath-Ley', 'Beach of the Axe'. It appears on
maps as 'Wadeley'. And the fishermen, when passing this cape in their boats, still sacrifice
food and tobacco by throwing it in the river, believing that if they did not make this offering
they would drown in the river.
The Alur and the Kamulega are the descendants of Nyivongo. The Alur settled in the country
to the west, the Kamulega set themselves up as the rulers of the Nyoro in the south. As the
Kamulega are the descendants of the first-born, the others have to go and acquire the royal
dignity from them. The Nyoro now speak a Bantu language.
Rubanga had a son named Kyabambi. Kyabambi in turn had four sons: Nyipir, Nyivongo,
Tiful, and Upodho.
One day Nyipir took Nyivongo's spear and started out to hunt
[Page [43] ]
elephants. He hunted for many days until he spotted an elephant. He pursued it until he
managed to throw his brother's spear into its belly, but the elephant ran away with the spear
sticking out of its body. Nyipir came home and told his story. Nyivongo insisted that he
wanted his spear back. Nyipir offered his brother ten good spears as compensation for the lost
one, but Nyivongo refused: he wanted his own spear back.
Now it was a matter of honour for Nyipir to go back to the bush, spot the same elephant and
recover the spear. He took some tobacco and travelled for many days. At last he met an old
woman to whom he gave some tobacco. She told him that she knew who had his brother's
spear. Some men (probably pygmies) had traced the wounded elephant and killed it. The old
woman told Nyipir where he could find them. He did find them, and demanded to see their
collection of spears. His brother's favourite spear was among them. He bought it back and
brought it home.
Later, the children of the two brothers were playing together in Nyipir's hut. They found a
cowskin pouch which contained one priceless pearl. One of Nyivongo's children took the
pearl, put it in his mouth and swallowed it. When this came to light, Nyipir demanded that
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
40 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 42 ---
Nyivongo return that pearl to him. Nyivongo offered him a hundred pearls and precious
stones, a herd of goats and his pedigree cows. Nyipir refused.
Now it was a matter of honour for Nyivongo to return the property of his brother. He ordered
his servants to take his son and open the boy's belly. They cut the child open and searched the
bowels. Finally they found the pearl and returned it to its owner.
Nyivongo buried his son and said: 'I will no longer live in the same country as Nyipir.' He
ordered his wives to pack all their belongings, and his slaves to load them into boats. The two
brothers, each with their seconds, strode solemnly to the Nile bank to perform the ceremony
of separation. There, on the land-corner, the Nile leaves Lake Albert, flowing northward. On
that cape, Nyipir went and stood facing east, looking towards the land of the
[Page [44] ]
Acholi. Nyivongo faced west, looking across the Nile to the new lands he would settle.
Together they planted a tobacco plant, and struck the ground with an axe: 'May this axe be the
sign that our lands will be from now on divided.'
The elders say that the axe is still there, and also the tobacco tree.
25. THE HEAVENLY TWINS
The Heavenly Twins in Roman mythology were the sons of Jupiter, the god of
thunderstorms. The earthly twins were Romulus and Remus, the sons of Mars, who
descended from the sky. They were reared by a wolf, the animal that is related to the land of
death.
In this story the son of the rain-god turns into a swamp. The little fingers of his twin sons
(probably a euphemism for their genitals) are later found as the main ingredient of the
rain-making charm: the twin-finger amulet ( ngisa ) used by the Alur kings.
The name of the rain-god's son is Ujok, 'son of God', or sometimes Uchak, 'Milk-Drinker',
which can also mean 'the Beginner, the Initiator'. The name of the son born from the second
union of Uchak and the princess is given as Kyabambi, but this does not seem to be his true
name, which ought to be Ukelo, 'The child one keeps to oneself'---the normal name for a boy
born after twins.
Today the birth of twins is an occasion of great rejoicing and celebration with the Alur.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
41 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 43 ---
King Ulezu had an only daughter who disdained all mortal men who came to ask her hand in
marriage. As the king's only child it was her duty to herd the royal cattle. One day as she was
in the pastures, she saw a cloud descend from the sky. In the cloud there was the son of God.
He took off his cloud and showed himself to her in his human shape as the son of Rubanga
the Rain-god. The cattle-herdress, the king's daughter, fell in love with him and agreed to
celebrate their union that very moment right there in the pastures.
[Page [45] ]
Before he left her he said: 'You will bear twins, but your people will be angered by your
having children out of wedlock, and they will kill the twins. Remember to keep the little
fingers as an amulet.' He stepped back into his cloud and rose up to heaven. It all happened as
he had predicted: she gave birth to twins, who were killed by her brothers because she had
not been able to give the name of their father.
The son of God visited the princess a second time. The son who was born from this union
was called Kyambambi.
The father was later ambushed and killed, and eventually turned into a swamp: returning to
the water that was his natural element.
26. THE ABIRA
The Abira are cave-dwelling pygmies, and are among the oldest inhabitants of the Congo.
The Alur cannot settle the land unless they conclude a treaty with these 'people of the earth'
and become their sons-in-law.
Before the Alur people arrived in the land which they now inhabit, the Abira, who were a
very primitive people, lived there. They had tails with switches at the end, like cow tails,
which may seem as if they were ape-like trolls: but perhaps this is only an Alur story. The
Abira lived in the caves in the hills west of the lake; they had no cattle and ate only pumpkins
and wild cucumbers. They were light-shy people who never came out in the open daylight,
but always rooted in the earth like ant-eaters.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
42 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 44 ---
One day the first Alur king arrived from across the water. He was Ojanga-Mbele, and he
brought cattle, millet and sorghum, and the famous amulet that makes rain. He wanted to lure
the Abira out of their holes in the ground but they refused. Then he devised a stratagem. His
men put pieces of beef on their spears and dangled them at the entrances of the caves. Others
had spears
[Page [46] ]
with broad blades on which they offered the Abira salt, which was a commodity totally
unknown to such a backward people. As soon as the Abira had tasted the beef with the salt,
they licked their lips, and wanted more. So they had to come out of their caves and beg King
Ojanga for more beef and salt.
They had to pay a heavy price for it: they were given the option of either leaving the land to
the Alur and migrating, or staying and becoming their servants. They chose to stay, and have
salt with their vegetables. They bought cattle by exchanging their daughters and sisters, and
so they became the fathers-in-law and brothers- in-law of the Alur, who taught them to plant
sorghum and to make gain.
27. THE COW-SACRIFICE
The sacred cow represents the Rain-god Rubanga, who signifies his approval of the new king
through its agency. The name Rubanga also means 'ibis'. It is tempting to compare Rubanga to
Thoth, the Egyptian moon-god, who was always pictured in the form of an ibis. Like
Rubanga, Thoth was the creator and the god of the seasons, who was born from the
primordial waters; his home was the Nile marshes.
Ojanga-Mbele founded the kingdom of the Alur in the hills across the water, but as soon as it
was founded, other factions within the royal family started plotting against him. For a time
they succeeded in gaining power by putting on the throne their own candidate, Udok, son of
Nyimur. Nyimur, Ojanga-Mbele's elder brother, had died young. A third brother, Alal, was
made king by the Abira because he brought them chickens, the meat of which they had never
tasted.
Later, Alal was killed and for a time a woman, Anyumba, was made queen. When she
appeared to be pregnant because she had permitted a slave of the household, Anduka, to
sleep with her, she
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
43 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 45 ---
[Page [47] ]
was bitterly reproached. She replied: 'Do you think a queen is different from other women?' It
was during her reign that the Abira realized they could run through the grass like the
waterbucks in the savannah.
When Ojanga-Mbele brought them millet they at first refused to eat it because they were
afraid it was poison. Finally, an old woman said: 'I will eat it, I am worn out anyway, it does
not matter if I die, and then you will know. Moreover, I am hungry.' She ate it and it tasted
good. Then the timorous Abira took courage, ate the millet-porridge, and liked it.
This won the Abira over to Ojanga-Mbele once again, but his own nobles would not back
him wholeheartedly until he could prove himself to be the rightful king of the Alur at the
annual ceremony of the sacrifice of a cow for Rubanga, the Rain-god. Every year, the king of
the Alur has to perform the ritual of the cow-sacrifice, which can only be performed by the
man whom Rubanga has predestined to be the true king.
Udok presided over the ritual, when Ojanga-Mbele challenged him, saying: 'If you are truly
king, order the cow to kneel for you and pay you homage!' A murmur went up among the
nobles and priests who were present. They had to admit that it was the ancient sign among
the Alur of the legitimacy of a man's claims to the throne. It was God's judgement! Udok
threatened and cajoled the cow, but it would not move. It just stood there in the middle of the
sacrificial place, ruminating and gazing into space.
Then Ojanga-Mbele came forward and spoke out in loud and solemn words, praying to the
Creator: 'Oh Rubanga, if I am the true king of the Alur, if these lands belong to me, if it is my
hand which must slaughter the royal cow in Thy honour, oh Rubanga, if the royal dignity
belongs to me, if I own the throne of Alurland, show Thy sign, may this cow bow down for
me!' Silence fell on the elders, the priests and the noblemen. Slowly, the cow bent both its
front legs and knelt down for Ojanga-Mbele, laying its head on the ground before him. A
whisper went through the
[Page [48] ]
ranks: 'God has spoken.' Ojanga-Mbele was the true king of Alurland.
28. THE SPIRIT OF KING KENO'S DRUM
The kings of Alurland must possess four sacred objects, without which their royal power has
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
44 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 46 ---
no validity: the pouch with the rain- stones, the flaming spear, the royal stool, and the drum,
the king's voice.
Keno was originally king of Ukoro. His eldest son Awinga rebelled against him when Keno
complained that he did not fulfil his filial duties. A winga won the ensuing battle and drove
Keno out of his own kingdom. Keno cursed his son Awinga: 'You have called me a sorcerer,
God will make you a slave!' Keno travelled with his wife to the homestead of his second son,
Umier. Umier received his father with full honour and paid him the respect he owed him, his
king and father.
Since Awinga had substituted might for right in the making of kings, he was now himself
open to being overthrown. Umier marched against him and won: in the ensuing battle,
Awinga was taken prisoner and became his brother's slave. The Angal people too made
Umier their king, and he was also recognized by the Abar, the Pangere, the Parombo whose
sign or totem is the lamb ( 
rombo =sheep) and the Dolo, whose totem is the dolo , the
mantle monkey (hamadryad); its skin is a symbol of royal dignity.
The Acher people, too, came to King Keno and requested him to become their king, and
promised him they would build his royal compound for him. They did, but unfortunately they
built it in an enclave which was surrounded by Lendu territory. The Lendu refused to
recognize Keno as their king. They said they wanted no king, least of all the king of the
Acher.
They brought spears to the land, waged war, and killed King
[Page [49] ]
Keno in battle. When a king dies, his drum may never again be used, for the drum is the
king's voice. That day at sunset, the people of the king's court heard the royal drum coming
home, rolling up-hill and down-hill. All by itself, without human hands, it drummed the
dreadful news: 'King Keno killed, King Keno killed!'
29. WHY PEOPLE DIE
The snake plays a vital role in Congolese mythology, as we shall see farther on. It is clearly a
female spirit. Just as women have the power to bear children, so the snake can creep out of
its own skin, new and fresh.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
45 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 47 ---
The Alur tell the story as follows: Once upon a time there was an old woman, and her
grandchild lived with her. She gave the little girl beans and millet, telling her to cook these.
Then she said: 'My grandchild, I am going to slough my skin behind the house. Remember
that I must not be disturbed.' The girl said yes, but followed her grandmother with her eyes
all the time.
First, the grandmother stripped off her skin from the legs, then the belly. When she had
reached her breast, the little girl came running: 'Grandma, grandma, the porridge is beginning
to boil! Please come and stir it!' When the grandmother heard this, her old skin went back
over her whole body. The whole process of rejuvenating her skin was spoilt.
The old woman said: 'You granddaughter, now you have spoilt my sloughing. Now you will
die with all your children and grandchildren. Not one of your family will ever escape death.
Your food will henceforth be acquired only by hard work. Previously, a little millet was
enough for a big meal. Now you will need a lot of grains for a small meal. Previously, the
birds were your friends, they would come to you and sit with you. From now on they will flee
you and you will be hungry and want to eat them.
[Page [50] ]
Be unhappy! Stay alone! If you had not called me, I could have shown you the secret of
renewing the skin.' The old woman died, and left death behind for all her descendants.
30. THE FAMINE
'Famine makes the Lendu obtain noble wives.' This proverb reminds the Alur people of the
great famine, when the despised Lendu people, the aborigines, could buy the daughters of the
Alur notables for food because they knew where to find wild fruits and how to catch the timid
forest animals. In English we say 'need knows no law'. In Alur: 'Famine makes the slave
king.' The clever slave lays in stock for the future, so he has food in times of famine. He who
feeds the hungry will be called king.
Many years ago there was a great famine in Alurland. The people died like chickens and no
one was left to bury them. No one felt strong enough to perform the ceremonies. Many
people left the country and trekked away to the east across the lake and to the west into the
forest. Many people sold their children to get food; only those who loved their children very
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
46 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 48 ---
much preferred to starve together with them. Even the daughters of the chiefs were married
off to obtain food, for even the rulers were hungry. Men of lowly birth married princesses if
they had food. Even Lendu men acquired wives of noble Alur birth.
People would eat anything. If they found things that only looked like potatoes they ate them,
not waiting to inspect them and see if they were poisonous or taboo. Many people died of
eating the wrong thing, which is, of course, worse than not eating at all.
Men and boys banded together into gangs of thugs and would roam the countryside. The
villagers would meet them, spear in hand, and many were killed. Both sides knew that they
would be killed if they did not kill the other first. If the villagers won, all the
[Page [51] ]
robbers would be slaughtered; if the robbers won, they would take the girls and the children
to sell for food, and raid the whole village so that nothing was left. It was in those days that
the proverb arose: If your son is a lazy fighter, your village is doomed.
The dead bodies were just left to decay, there were so many of them. Even if your brother
were to drop dead near you, your arm would be too weak to lift him up, and there would be
no time to cover his body with sand as you would be hurrying to the place where people had
told you food was available.
The feeling of hunger was stronger than all other feelings. You would say to yourself: 'My
brother is no longer hungry, he needs no more food; I do.' So you would travel on, and the
wild animals would eat your brother's body. Even if your brother had been killed by someone,
and he would offer you food, you would eat with your brother's murderer.
In that time, the slave became king, if he was clever. If he had collected food previously, and
stored it up, the people would make him their ruler. The people would think only of their
empty food baskets, and so if anyone's baskets were filled, they would say: 'Order, we obey!
We will work for you, Oh King! Send us out and feed us, that we may be saved!' Hunger
made them work hard. Hunger made people meek and tame. Even the son of a king would
bow and pay homage to a slave, if the slave had food. The owner of food became the ruler of
men. Not only the ruler of men but often the owner of men, and of women. Daughters were
exchanged for a basket of millet, sons for a goat. A father might sell his family into slavery if
he knew that their new master could give them food, and seeds to plant and cattle to herd,
and if he himself did not have these things.
[Page [53] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
47 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 49 ---
2 The Bakongo
[Page [54] ]
On the other side of the Congo, in the extreme west, along the lower reaches of the Congo
river, there live the Bakongo, the people who gave their name to the country and to the river.
The powerful Congo moves fifteen times faster than the Rhine. Thundering loudly, it comes
crashing down the rocks, shooting through the gorges between high granite walls, foaming
and spraying in many colours. The river turns many sharp corners, butting against mountains,
tumbling down broad slopes strewn with colossal boulders. For two hundred miles, along its
rocky edges, there is the silent forest, evergreen and mysterious. In this vast forest, small
patches have been carved out where the Bakongo have their vegetable gardens. Just before
Matadi the landscape suddenly changes. The grim rocks disappear and give way to rolling
hills; the river widens and opens up into a spacious deep bed. Huge ocean steamers call at
Matadi to load the riches of the Congo. Farther down, there is the delta, lush green islands
and endless reed marshes, widening out towards the soft beaches of the Atlantic. This is the
country of the Bakongo: wild and beautiful.
1. THE JEALOUS WIFE
This story is from an unwritten handbook for chieflings. The moral of the sad tale is: painful
justice is imperative; never favour anyone above another. The story contains nothing magic or
miraculous; on the contrary, it is tragically human. It shows that though conditions may differ,
people differ very little in different cultures.
It is quite normal for a woman in a polygamous household to have
[Page [55] ]
a lover; usually a young man who does not have the wherewithal to buy a wife for himself.
It is the custom of palm-owners, before they climb their trees, to beat a rattle on the tree to
announce their intention. From high up in the tops of their trees they have a free view into
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
48 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 50 ---
everybody's garden, but they must not be known as spies, as this would arouse the hatred of
the villagers. They want to work openly and give everyone time to withdraw, or stop
whatever they are doing that cannot bear the eye of publicity .
Once upon a time there was a chief who had two wives, both of whom he loved, and they
each gave birth to a daughter, one called Pemba and the other Tsonda; the two daughters of
Chief Manza Pungu resembled each other very closely.
The father showed a great love for Pemba, and gave her special favours. He gave Pemba
food, clothes, and ornaments. Masekidi, Tsonda's mother, had to buy ornaments and clothes
for her daughter herself. She asked her husband once why he gave so many beads and yards
of cotton to Pemba and never any to her own daughter, but Chief Manza said: 'Oh well, first
let your daughter grow up a little, then I will give her all the ornaments and clothes she
needs.'
They grew up quickly, and their breasts soon filled out. Father Manza gave new clothes to
Pemba but none to Tsonda. Tsonda's mother thought to herself: 'Perhaps he thinks that
Tsonda is not his daughter but some other man's. If that other girl ever comes into my hut, I
will kill her!'
One day Pemba, who loved her half-sister, said to her: 'Tonight I will come and sleep with
you in your mother's hut, and you may wear all my bracelets and necklaces.' In day-time she
would not have dared to lend them to her sister because of her father. That night, Tsonda's
mother went to her lover and asked him for the ramrod of his gun. She would never have
dared ask her husband for it, for she was not supposed even to enter the chief's hut, where the
arms were kept, without being invited.
[Page [56] ]
She took the ramrod to her hut and laid it with its point in the fire. When it was red-hot she
took it out and walked over to the part of the floor where the two girls were sleeping side by
side on their mats. Blind with anger, she killed the girl with the ornaments on, the girl who
was sleeping farthest from the fire. She could not see very clearly, as she was standing
between the fire and the girls, and she did not look closely, for angry people are usually in a
hurry. She stabbed the glowing ramrod into one of the girl's nostrils and pushed on up until
the girl was dead. This is the surest way of killing a person without leaving a mark, a bruise,
or a wound.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
49 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 51 ---
At daybreak Tsonda's mother rose and looked at the two girls who were lying there in the
pale morning light. Then imagine her horror. The girl with the ornaments on was her own
daughter! She had killed her own dear Tsonda! It could not be true. As Pemba was still
asleep, the mother hoped that Tsonda was too. A vain hope after what she had done the night
before! She touched the girl, but sleeping bodies are often chilly in the early morning, there
in the hills. She shook her daughter, but she did not move, let alone wake up.
The mother now faced the facts. She decided she had to remove the body before the other
girl woke up, lest she be discovered as a murderess. She lifted the body up and put it in a
large basket of the type the women use for harvesting. She removed all the ornaments and put
them on the other girl's sleeping-mat. Then she put some leaves in the basket and carried it
out to the fields. Like every woman she had a plot on which she cultivated groundnuts, and
there she hid her daughter's body under a heap of weeds and grass.
She went back to her hut and found Pemba still asleep. She woke her up and said: 'Now you
had better go home, for Tsonda has already left; she has gone to her grandmother's.' Pemba
was, of course, surprised, the more so since she always gave her half- sister some ornaments
to wear when she went away anywhere out of sight of their father.
[Page [57] ]
That same morning, for the first time, their father took it into his head to call both girls.
Pemba appeared, but Tsonda did not. He called Tsonda's mother, who appeared obediently
before him and apologized for not informing him earlier that Tsonda would be going to her
maternal grandmother's that morning. The chief was surprised that she had started out so
early, but he gave the matter no further thought. The woman remarked bitterly: 'You never
think of her at all, but the one day she goes out you have to call her.'
Having said that, she went back to her hut, took a hoe and went out again to her groundnut
plot. The heap of weeds was already surrounded with buzzing flies. She lifted the body of her
beautiful dead daughter up, laid it in her lap, caressed it and started a song:
Malemba Tsonda, dear Tsonda, my child,
My beauty, I will buy you sweets and food,
Come back please, wake up, you my darling.
Suddenly she heard someone beat a rattle, and she hastily hid the body under the weeds. It
was her daughter's lover, who owned some oil-palms in the plot next to her own, which
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do

--- PAGE 52 ---
afforded a splendid opportunity to have secret encounters with his sweetheart.
The suitor put on his foot-slings and climbed up into the tree. Looking down he saw only the
mother, not his sweetheart, as he had expected, so he called down 'Mother-of-Tsonda, where
is the girl? She told me she would wait for me here, for she wanted some palm-wine.'
The woman said: 'She has gone to her grandmother's this morning.'
The young man said: 'Why did you not allow her to wait for me? I have some palm-wine here
for her.'
'Leave it with me; she told me she wanted to drink it tonight.'
The suitor, having cut the oil-nuts, climbed down, handed a
[Page [58] ]
jug of palm-wine to his prospective mother-in-law, and went away to his oil-pits, where his
nuts were fermenting.
The woman lifted her daughter's body again from the heap of weeds, put it on her lap, poured
some of the palm-wine into the dead mouth, and of course it was spilt. Can a dead girl
swallow? Then she sang again:
My little daughter, my good child,
Drink some of this delicious wine,
Which your lover brought for you,
Don't you want to see him soon?
Don't you want to come back to him?
Don't you want to exchange sweet words?
Why did you put on those beads?
Why the bracelets and the skirt?
Then I thought it was that girl.
And I stabbed my own dear child.
As soon as she had calmed down a little, she hid the body again under the heap of weeds, and
went back to the village. It was well past midday. When her husband saw her he called her
and asked her where their daughter was. He was secretly afraid that his in- laws would keep
her with them, because he had constantly neglected her.
Tsonda's mother said: 'Chief Manza, this is the first day since my daughter was born that you
have ever asked for her. Why are you so anxious to see her? You never wanted to see her
until now. Is it perhaps to give her something? You have never done so yet! And today you
want to see her twice! Perhaps you want her to work for you? Perhaps Pemba refused, or is
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
51 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 53 ---
she too good to work? I told you she is with her grandmother.'
This was the first time in over twelve years of marriage that she had dared speak to her
husband like this. But she had already lost all that she really cared for, and she knew that one
day soon it was bound to come out anyway. What did she have to lose? So she said: 'Her
lover sent her some palm-wine, which she has tasted. I am
[Page [59] ]
sure she is still asleep, for it was very heady. Perhaps if you could send her a jug of gin, and a
skirt with fringes, and a squirrel, she might come back.'
'All right,' said the chief, 'I will give them to her tomorrow.'
'I would prefer to have all these things now, for I shall go to my mother's house at dawn.'
She received her bottle of gin, the best skirt in the house, and a squirrel, all at once. It was
more than she expected, and more than she had ever been given by her husband. She went
and fetched water and cooked the squirrel.
At daybreak, as soon as the cock had shaken the night from his feathers, she rose and went to
her groundnut garden. She took the dead body from the weeds and dead leaves and began
another song:
Malemba Tsonda, dear child,
Your father has given you some gin,
A lovely skirt and even food,
The meat of a delicious squirrel,
He gave all this the day before
The day I have to die for you.
All morning she stayed like this, crying and singing. Towards noon, the owner of the oil-palm
trees, her daughter's lover, came back to pick the ripe nuts. He beat his usual rattle before
starting to climb up the trees. Sadly, he began a song, while making ready:
I am alone, my love has gone afar,
She drinks my wine, she never shows her face.
The girl's mother, who had had time to hide the dead body, told him: 'If you bring some more
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
52 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 54 ---
palm-wine, I will take it to her and I am sure she will soon come back to you.'
So he left another jug of palm-wine with his sweetheart's mother, and went back to his trees.
The woman thought that he had gone away to his oil-pits, so she started singing again:
[Page [60] ]
I lament my little darling,
Look, I changed you, lovely girl.
The young man, who had remained behind the hedge, heard this and was deeply puzzled. It
sounded to him like a song of mourning. He decided to climb up into one of his palms, from
which he had an easy view into the groundnut-garden. He was aghast when he saw his own
sweetheart, lying dead, on her mother's lap, with the flies buzzing round her. He climbed
down and ran to Chief Manza Pungu, and told him: 'Sir, please come and look at your wife
Masekidi. I won't say anything now, just come and see.'
He led the chief to a place where they could see through the shrubs, and when the chief
looked, his heart grew heavy at what he saw in his wife's lap. Chief Manza was furious and
cried out: 'Woman, why did you do this?' His wife's voice failed her, and she started to
tremble. Chief Manza and the young lover lifted the dead body and carried it together to the
village, where it was laid in state.
The chief sent a messenger to his wife's father and her uncles. All the elders arrived quickly.
The suitor did not accuse the chief's wife directly; he wanted the chief to see for himself and
to act as necessary. The chief does not have the right to sit in judgement over his own wife;
only her paternal uncles have that prerogative. The woman seemed to be out of her mind. She
just went on singing:
My little girl, why did you,
why did you put on those lovely beads?
You are so naughty, why don't you come back?
The men tied a liana round her neck and dragged her away. In the village, in front of the
chief's hut, lay the corpse, tied to a pole, and there sat the woman, a rope round her neck,
resigned and waiting for the end. The people were standing round the village square. They
did not dare to talk, but they slapped themselves on the buttocks in terror, for they all feared
that the woman was a
[Page [61] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
53 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 55 ---
dangerous witch, because only witches kill their own children. As the girl was killed with a
ramrod rammed into her nose, no mark could be seen, so they thought that she must have
been killed by witchcraft.
Accordingly, the uncles prepared 
kasa , poison for the divine judgement. She was forced to
drink it, and soon she felt she was dying. Her last words were: 'He, my husband, wanted it
this way, he never gave anything to the child.' Therefore, young men, remember: always be
just and fair to all your wives and children.
2. MY SWEET WITCH
Witchcraft occupies the minds of many people in Africa more than any other single subject.
What would you do, young man, if you found out that your fiancée and her parents were
witches---murderers who ate their victims?
You have the choice between eating human meat together with them and thus becoming a
cannibal, or, if you refuse, becoming a victim, and so being eaten by your own girl-friend.
The remedy against witches is a secret potion. Innocent people will vomit it out, but it burns
the bowels of witches so that they die. Witches can leave their human bodies behind and fly
through the night.
A young man, Matsona, loved a girl whose name was Kitsumuna, and every night he brought
her palm-wine. One day she told him: 'Tomorrow you may not come, come the night after.'
The young man thought that strange, but he did not suspect the real reason, which was much
worse than he thought: she was a witch! However, the young man decided to go and visit his
sweetheart anyway, but just as a precaution he took some palm-nuts and put them in his
loincloth.
He then went out and knocked at her door. She appeared very embarrassed at seeing him, but
she could not refuse to let him in.
[Page [62] ]
So he made love to her; but suddenly, in the middle of the night, he heard the voice of her
father, the village chief, calling from above. It was in reality the chief's spirit, perched on top
of the hut-roof.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
54 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 56 ---
The chief entered the hut the way a bat would fly down from the roof; her mother followed
and several other villagers came flying in, all bodiless. When the spirits were all present, the
chief ordered his daughter Kitsumuna to come and sit with them. She was embarrassed
because she knew that her lover could hear every word, although no one would be able to see
in the dark night, not even witches.
Her father, the chief, gave her a piece of meat and told her to eat it. She dropped it clumsily,
and the other witches too dropped their witches' meat. Therefore the chief witchmaster
decided that not everyone in the village was asleep, and that someone must be overhearing
them. So he flew up, out of the hut and over the village, and sprinkled sleeping water over
the huts to make quite sure that all the innocent people would be soundly asleep.
After his return to the witches' coven, it appeared that the magic water had still not worked on
everyone, for when he tried to pour the sauce made with human blood, he spilt it. This
proved that the sleepless person must be hiding in the girl's own hut. They searched it and
finally found Kitsumuna's suitor Matsona.
He was invited by the chief to join the coven, and at once received a piece of meat. He knew
very well what that meant: he had to contribute a human body at some future coven: the price
for joining was to become a murderer. Since, however, the price for not joining was to
become a victim, he had very little option.
He found a shrewd way out of this dilemma: seeing that his girl-friend's mother was one of
the coven he said, like a well- brought-up young man: 'Of course I accept the meat with
pleasure, but I really could not eat in the presence of my mother-in-law.'
They all agreed that this was impolite, even among witches, so he
[Page [63] ]
was allowed to eat sitting in a corner behind the bed, with his back turned to the others. In
this manner he was able to take the palm- nuts from his pocket and eat them instead so that
everyone could hear him chew. His girl-friend was of course very happy that her lover had
joined the coven. She did not know that he had buried the food she had given him in the mud
floor of the hut. After the meal the chief said: 'Now you must let us have one of your
relatives, your mother for instance, or your grandmother.' The young Matsona said he agreed
because he knew that that was expected of him.
Shortly afterwards the girl's grandmother died. The villagers suspected that she had not died
of old age and urged the chief to send for a diviner. Matsona was the person sent to find a
diviner, and on his way he passed through a near-by village where his future brother-in-law,
Katsumuna's brother, lived. Matsona told him what his errand was, that he had been sent to
find a diviner to investigate the mysterious death of Katsumuna's grandmother.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
55 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 57 ---
Her brother said: 'She is my own grandmother too. If her death has been caused by witchcraft,
I must come and smell out the evil-doers. As you know, I am myself a diviner. I am grateful
to you for telling me all this. I will come with you now, and on my arrival in the village, I will
stay with you to begin with, merely as a future relative, so that I have time to sound the
situation out without arousing suspicion.'
When the appointed day for the divining ritual arrived, the chief assembled all the inhabitants
in the village square. The diviner then asked them to go and stand in groups, dividing free
men from slaves, descendants from in-laws, and so on. The chief was beginning to feel
uneasy, for he sensed that the wise diviner had perceived where the root of the trouble lay, so
the chief's heart was burning with anxiety. Finally, the diviner had narrowed down his
suspects to a single family, all the time singing:
[Page [64] ]
Bapfumu wau ntanda
Baana wau ntanda ...
Free men their rank,
slaves their rank ...
In this manner he singled out the family of the chief, and dismissed all the others as
acquitted. The chief, his wife, and the members of the coven had to drink poison as a divine
judgement, and they all died, including Katsumuna.
3. A MALE WITCH
What would you do, young woman, if you found out not only that your fiancé was a cannibal,
but also that he had decided that you yourself would be on the menu for his next dinner? In
this story you will learn what a fetish is and how it works for its owners. Witches kill their
victims by means of the spirit; in this story they do not even have to touch the girl---she
simply dies of terror. But in spite of their spiritual substance, her father can see them, and
identifies them later in the daytime.
A girl named Malemba had a suitor who was a witch, or should one say he was a male
member of a witches' coven, and communicated with evil spirits. His girl-friend knew
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
56 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 58 ---
nothing about this.
One day the coven prepared a meal of human meat while the young man was away on a
journey. After the meal there was only one human finger left and the coven decided that this
would have to be sufficient for him. So they put it in a basket, added some bananas by way of
garnish, and placed this in his hut on a shelf.
As it happened, that same day Malemba decided to go and visit her lover. She cooked some
meat and vegetables for him and packed them nicely in a basket. When she arrived in his
village darkness had just fallen, so that she could slip into his hut without anyone seeing her.
When she found that he was not at home she
[Page [65] ]
decided to wait for him, and sat down patiently on his bed without preparing a light to see by.
Shortly after, her lover arrived and entered his hut without noticing her because she was
quiet, and said nothing, and there was no light. He did notice, however, the basket of food
which the coven members had placed on his shelf. He took it down and opened it. He found
the bananas and ate them. Then he found the one finger and burst out loudly: 'Those misers!
They left only a finger for me! All right, when I kill my girl-friend Malemba, they will have
nothing but one of her fingers!'
Malemba sat there, petrified. She knew that being there meant certain death, but what could
she do? He had not yet seen her, but as soon as he did he would kill her! But her lover did not
go out; on the contrary, he lit a torch and saw her there on the bed. He said: 'Malemba! Did
you hear what I said?'
She said: 'No! Not a word! I swear!' He took his fetish and made her swear on this sacred
object. She did and, on his insistence, afterwards licked it, so that its substance would kill her
if she lied. Yet her suitor still did not believe her, so he decided it would be better to eat her
at once.
He went out to assemble his fellows. As soon as he had left, the girl slid out of the hut and
ran like a shadow along the path to her own village. Alas! How could she outrun black magic?
The members soon assembled. When they found the hut empty, they decided to pursue the
girl and kill her at once, for no risk should be taken by letting her live.
They took a fetish from its pole in the village, and ordered it to find the direction the girl had
taken. The fetish turned towards the mountains but found no trail, then towards the valley
and there it smelled the girl. The spirit hidden in the statue was called Ngodo-mvindi. As
soon as it smelled its prey, the wooden body of the spirit began to tremble and the men
followed it along the path.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do

--- PAGE 59 ---
Before she had reached her village, the girl, Malemba, heard the
[Page [66] ]
voices of the men shouting behind her, so she decided to turn left towards her mother's
garden. There she hid under a pile of weeds and rubble. As soon as the fetish reached that
path it stopped and turned in the direction where the girl had fled. Suddenly a kubu antelope
leapt up from the fields and started running across the gardens, jumping over fences and
shrubs. The fetish-spirit was misled, and followed the antelope, and so did the men. Perhaps
the 
kubu was a good spirit who wished to help the girl. Who knows? It took the wicked men
hours to pursue the running antelope in the darkness.
At last the fetish-spirit perceived it was mistaken and turned back. By the time the witches
reached the garden of Malemba's mother, the cocks were beginning to crow. Suddenly the
coven turned round and made hastily for their own village. There they put the fetish back on
its pole and sneaked into their huts, just before the ordinary people of the village began to
stir.
The girl, Malemba, lay there under the weeds her mother had gathered into a heap. Before
noon her mother arrived in her garden to gather some groundnuts. There she found her
daughter, more dead than alive, lying gasping as if she were seriously ill. 'Oh, my daughter!'
she cried, picked up her girl and carried her home. In the security of her mother's hut,
Malemba told the story.
That evening her father came home and asked her every detail. She confessed: 'I had gone to
my lover's hut because I wanted his love. I never told you, please forgive me!' As soon as
darkness fell the coven of evil man-eaters was on its way again. This time they made no
mistake, and quickly reached the hut of Malemba's mother. When she saw her 'lover' enter,
the girl could not utter another word. Six wicked young men stood there, and she fell dead on
the floor. The father looked each of them in the face so that he would recognize them later
before the village council.
'You killed her. Wait till we have buried her. Then I will ask your reckoning.'
[Page [67] ]
After the funeral of Malemba, her father accused the six men before the chief's council. They
were summoned and had to drink the poison of divine judgement. They all died miserably.
There are many lessons in this story. Never help anyone to do evil, for you will be killed

--- PAGE 60 ---
yourself. If you are a girl and you want to visit your lover, go to his mother's hut first and stay
with her, so that everybody may know that you are there, and that you are his girl. His mother,
if she had only known of it, would certainly have prevented him from killing his girl. Now
she can only mourn her son.
4. FATHER-IN-LA W BELOW THE EARTH
The people who live in the world below the pond can exist only in the rainy season; they are
like the frogs who sleep in the mud until the rains begin, and then come out and croak
cheerfully.
A man had big plantations in which he grew bananas. Early one morning, he found that the
bush-pigs had helped themselves to the ripe fruits. He pursued them and wounded one in the
tail. He decided to find their owner and to claim damages. He collected his bananas, fresh
ones and damaged ones, and set out on the trail. This ended in a pond, so the man jumped
into the water. Below there, he found a large village and demanded to see the chief.
'Are you the master of the wild pigs? They ate my fruits and I want compensation. I wounded
one in the tail.'
The chief inspected his pigs and found the one with the wounded tail. Then he wanted to see
the fruits, the damaged ones and the fresh ones. He ate some of the fruit and was so pleased
with it that he gave his daughter in marriage to the owner of the banana plantation.
He said: 'Now you will go back to the land above. Remember that you may visit us only in
the rainy season.'
[Page [68] ]
The man took his young wife with him up through the pond to his own country, where he
built her a nice hut and they lived together. After a year she gave birth to a son, who grew up
prosperously, until one day he fell ill. She wanted to go to her father, for she said: 'My father
is a great magical expert, so he will cure our son.'
Alas, it was the dry season, so her husband said: 'We cannot go, for your father forbade us to
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
59 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 61 ---
visit him during the dry season.'
His wife pleaded and pleaded until he gave in and went with her. They arrived at the pond,
and went through it. It was the end of their many happy days together. As soon as they arrived
in the great chief's village, they saw the palm-trees dying, and the women dying while
pounding cassava. The chief died in his house, together with all his wives. Even the chief's
daughter and her baby died as soon as they entered the village. Only the banana- planter, the
pig-hunter, stayed alive. He fled, but wherever he looked he could not find the exit from that
land to the pond in his own world. He sat down disconsolately.
The next day he heard thunder and rain. With the very first drops all the villagers rose up,
alive again.
5. THE BEWITCHED GARDEN
Spirits often appear in the form of birds or bats, or with imperfect human bodies. Greed is
characteristic of evil spirits. Singing is a perfect means of bewitching other creatures, even
spirits. The word 
kinuni means 'little bird'.
A woman chose a plot to cultivate and started weeding. At nightfall she left the field
well-cleared and tidy. But lo! A little bird arrived and began to sing:
Women weeding well, kinuni, kinuni!
A bird breeds weeds, kinuni, kinuni!
[Page [69] ]
The next morning the woman came back with her seedlings, and found to her dismay that the
whole garden was again full of high grass. She ran to her husband and told him what had
happened. He refused to believe her and thought she was trying to cover up her laziness.
However, she was so certain of her case that he decided to help her for just one day.
They went out together and weeded all day until the field was again clear and tidy. Just to
make sure, before nightfall the man put a few snares ready, and sure enough, the next
morning they found the little bird caught in a snare. The man was just on the point of
breaking its legs and wings like twigs, when it began to sing:
I am the little partridge, I protect against misfortune.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
60 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 62 ---
Let me live and do not harm me, that I may protect you.
The woman was anxious that the dangerous bird should be killed: she was thinking of her
fields. The man, however, thought it more prudent not to harm what was evidently a powerful
spirit, and let it fly away.
Many weeks later, the woman went to her parents' home to give birth to her first-born, as was
the custom. When it was time to fetch her back, the man tapped a jarful of palm-wine and
went to his in-laws. On the way he met a spirit in the form of a truncated body: no arms, no
legs, no head. The Trunk asked him politely where he was going and the man answered him.
The Trunk begged his permission to accompany him, and offered to carry the wine- jar.
The man replied, surprised: 'But you have nothing to carry it with.' The Trunk, however,
insisted, and so the man put the jar on top of the Trunk, who carried it all the way.
When they arrived at the in-laws' compound, a hut was assigned to them and they were
brought a lavish meal. The man ate until he was satisfied; the Trunk ate the spoons and
knives too.
The next day they had a goat for breakfast; the man ate his fill,
[Page [70] ]
but the Trunk ate the bones, the horns, the skin, and the pots as well. After this they went
home, the man with his wife carrying her new baby, accompanied by the greedy Trunk.
When they reached the spot where the Trunk had crept out of the earth, it stopped and
demanded:
Now share with me your wife, be generous!
Now share with me your child, be generous!
As soon as his fellows heard that the Trunk was singing for his prey they too crept out of the
earth, one or two first, then a dozen, then a hundred, all writhing bodies. All demanded to eat
live human flesh. The man despaired! At that moment the magic partridge arrived and
demanded to know what was going on. At once it called its fellow birds and told them to start
singing. As soon as they heard the lovely songs the trunks started dancing--- they could not
help it!
The partridge told the man and his family: 'Now go home quickly! Do you see now that the
partridge is your protector?'

6. EXCHANGED NOSES
Magic can do in Africa what surgery does in Europe: it can give a man a handsome face. Ugly
little girls may possess greater powers than clever men. This story has a stern moral.
A father prepared a wooing hut for his daughter Ngombe. This meant that he considered her
marriageable: suitors were invited to come along and try their success. Many tried but they all
failed. The girl told them: 'I am waiting for the man who is worthy of me.'
In a far land there was a cunning young man who had a big defect: his nose was one bloody
sore. So he went to a handsome friend and begged him to lend him his appearance. The
friend was finally persuaded to part with his beauty and wear the ugly
[Page [71] ]
appearance of the lovesick young man. The man went to the girl in her hut, and at once she
saw that he was the right husband for her and agreed to marry him. She could not know that
he was walking about with a borrowed face!
The father consented to the match and the matter was concluded. The young man went back
to his village to prepare the nuptial hut for his young wife. She was due to arrive the next day.
Her parents sent her little sister with her, lest she feel lonely in the foreign land.
The little sister's body was covered with sores, so her big sister usually kept her in a basket so
that no one would see her. The cunning man was a good hunter and trapper, and there was
always plenty of meat and palm-wine for both his wife and her little sister, whose presence
he did not even suspect. He also had to keep in with his creditor, who was obliged to walk
about with his sore nose. He, the clever man, kept him plentifully supplied with palm-wine,
but one day they met face-to-face in the forest, and the creditor demanded his property back.
Mr Clever begged and implored, but the owner of the nice nose insisted on his rights, so they
exchanged noses again.
That night, Mr Clever did not come home before the night had become pitch-dark, for he did
not want anyone to see his ugliness. Before he entered Ngombe's hut he ordered her to
extinguish the fire and to lie down.
When he was sure that she was sleeping he came in, pulled the ramrod out of his gun and
muttered: 'If I am ugly, you shall be ugly too, for I cannot bear you to be as beautiful as you
are.'
He relit the fire and heated his sharp weapon. But the little girl in the basket was not asleep,
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
62 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 64 ---
and what was more, she perceived his evil intentions, and her magic was stronger. She began
to sing a song:
Eh, Ngombe, muana mama!
Oh Ngombe, child of my mother,
Eh, kulala, Ngombe?
Are you sleeping Ngombe?
[Page [72] ]
She went on and on singing it, and her sister's husband could not help dancing. While he was
dancing his garment slid to the floor. He sang a song to make it cling to his limbs and prevent
him from dancing, but this failed: he had to dance until the cock crowed.
Hastily, he took his gun and left before dawn, lest his wife see his ugliness. His little sister-
in-law, however, had seen everything in the light of the fire. She told Ngombe that her
husband had wanted to brand her to make her ugly. Quickly the two girls fled back to their
own parents.
Girls! Always take a husband from your own district, and do not judge by appearances!
7. THE TWO SUITORS
Mbumba's story is a sad one; she has to suffer after her death, and it is not even clear that she
is guilty. Her foolishness causes her to be punished in the shadowy existence after this life.
A girl called Mbumba was friendly to two suitors at the same time. When her father built a
wooing hut for her, both suitors brought her palm-wine, indicating that they both wanted to
spend the night with her. Since she accepted both gifts, it seems that she also accepted both
young men as lovers. When it came to the question of marriage, however, they were both
rejected by her. Both swore revenge.
She did not know that the two young men were members of the witches' guild. They had a
meeting and agreed to abduct the girl to the village of the spirits to punish her for her pride.
In the middle of the night they performed their secret rituals of black magic, and stole the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
63 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 65 ---
girl's true body for ever. This means that they obtained control over the essence-of-health
which must dwell inside a person's body to keep him healthy. If it is not there, what is
[Page [73] ]
left is only the outward shell, the appearance of the body , but its strength is gone.
So the girl fell ill, and became sicker every day; that at least was what the parents saw,
whatever they suspected. Finally she died, in the prime of youth, and now witchcraft was
suspected. But who had done it? The two suitors were there, weeping excessively, to show to
all the people how great their sorrow was.
The next day the girl was interred, and that night the two male witches went to tell the spirits
that they must not give this young soul palm-wine or cooked food. She must eat nothing but
groundnuts and raw palm-juice.
The next night the two young men came back to the graveyard with bananas and pawpaws for
their girl. They told her that she would be killed if she tried to escape. This, one would
suppose, was only intended to frighten her, for she was already dead. Yet it appears that even
the souls of the dead can be punished bodily.
As soon as they had turned their backs she followed them, and near the village she began to
sing:
I am Mbumba Lendo,
You have stolen my body,
You bring only pawpaws,
Cold uncooked bananas,
Stale and tasteless palm-wine,
I will go to mother,
Ask for peppered porridge,
I will go to father,
Ask for salted goat-meat.
The two young men, on hearing this, knew how to deal with it. They cut some twigs,
ambushed the girl's ghost on the road, and beat her savagely so that her body was covered
with bloody stripes and bruises.
'Now go back to the village of the spirits and stay there until we call you. From now on you
belong in Dead Town!'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
64 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 66 ---
[Page [74] ]
The next night they went back to the graveyard with a basket of fruits and again told the ghost
that she must stay there. This time she waited a little longer before she disobeyed. They were
already close to the village when they heard her singing behind them. They were afraid that
the villagers would hear her, and what was worse, dawn was not far away and they would
have to be back in their beds and fast asleep before sunrise, or else the people would suspect
them. No one wanders about at night except on some nefarious business.
They waylaid her and gave her a sound thrashing, hoping to scare her away:
'You must stay out there in dead town, you fool! You eat our food, so you must obey us! Why
did you not choose one of us!'
She complained: 'I could not, for the other one would have taken his revenge anyway!'
Finally, she fled, but hid a bit farther up the road. The two young men hurried home, sneaked
into their beds and pretended to be asleep. Mbumba followed them, although dawn was
rising. She was singing all the way, singing out her sorrow, singing of her suffering, of all that
she had been through: all the grief, all the love, all the foolishness of her life. The people in
the village heard her, and recognized her voice. They all knew that the soul of a dead girl was
coming back, hungering to speak to her parents. The ghost entered her father's house and her
mother woke him up:
'Look, my husband, our daughter, whom we buried, is standing there and wants to speak to
you.'
Then the ghost of poor Mbumba told her father of the many ordeals she had suffered, and
showed him the red bruises on her body. He aroused the villagers and accused the villains.
The council ordered them to drink poison. They did not vomit it out, but died, so they were
guilty.
Girls! Do not love a boy without your father knowing him, and never try to please two men!
[Page [75] ]

3 The Nkundo
[Page [76] ]
The Nkundo belong to a large group of peoples called the Mongo- Nkundo, after the two
main dialects of the group. They live in the central Congo Basin, in the 'armpit' of the Congo
River, east of Cocquilhatville. It is a vast woody region through which many rivers cut their
way, all flowing from east to west. They give their country the appropriate name
Middle-Land (Ngimôkili), for it is situated in the middle of the Congo Basin, which is the
very heart of Central Africa.
1. LONKUNDO'S FAME
The ancestor of the Nkundo is Lo-Nkundo, whose father, a spirit, taught him the secret of
trapping animals. This makes Nkundo into what mythologists call a 'culture hero'.
Another much more sophisticated invention is taken for granted right from the beginning of
the story: the talking drum. It is a wooden drum with a slit in the middle. The left side is
thick, and gives a high-pitched sound; the right side is thinner and gives a lower sound when
struck with the drumstick. These two tones (or notes) correspond with the two basic tones of
the Lo-Nkundo language. Like all great chiefs, Lonkundo has his court-drummer, just as
sea-captains have their telegraphists. These signal drummers, like telegraphists, have
developed a professional system of codes, which only they understand and which can be
operated for only one dialect. Those who live in the frontier districts are often not only
bilingual but bi-codal, again like some telegraphists who can handle the Russian as well as
the English system of codes. The
[Page [77] ]
drum-codes of some languages have been deciphered by a few Western scholars, and it has
been shown that these coding systems are as ingenious as they are complicated.
A great chief must have many wives. The time-honoured way for a man to amass wealth in
Africa is to marry and have more offspring, which means more hands for work, more warriors
to conquer more land, and more daughters who will bring in more bride-prices. Since the first
wife has the right to command the junior wives, it gives her prestige, too, if her husband is a
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
66 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 68 ---
polygynist. This is why senior wives, far from demanding that their husbands be
monogamous, will encourage them to marry other wives and so acquire relations and respect
in society: a tree with more branches gives more shade.
The fire-basket is one of the many motifs in this myth that is not explained further. All we
learn is that fire sparks out of it, and that it produces any number of blades: knife-blades,
axe-blades, arrowheads, spear-blades, hoe-blades, etc. These products of the Smithy are in
such high demand in the Congo that they are used as currency, and for the acquisition of
brides. Any man who can produce them in large numbers will be affluent.
Every married woman in equatorial Africa wants her own 
shamba or plantation, where she
can grow exactly what she likes. Land is still available in Africa for anyone who can buy it or
conquer it. On their fields the women grow bananas, groundnuts, cassava, beans, peppers and
other vegetables, all for their own kitchens, and to have a little pocket money.
Many years ago there lived a man called Lonkundo, who was superior to all men. He lived in
a region called Middleland. His wife's name was Nsombe, and they had a son called Yonjwa.
One night, Lonkundo dreamed a dream in which his father, Elombo, appeared to him, to give
him instructions:
'Go to the well, early in the morning and examine the ground. There you will find the track of
an animal. You will not miss it, for I shall be with you. Cut raffia-fibres and a springy twig,
and make a trap.'
[Page [78] ]
The next morning, Lonkundo did everything his father's ghost had told him. In those days,
snares and traps were unknown in Middleland. That evening, the women coming back from
the well with their jars on their heads, reported that an unknown animal was caught in a
contraption they had never seen before. Lonkundo said that he had made it himself. He
sounded the signal drum to call the men from the other villages, telling them they must help
him. All the men arrived and took the animal to the village square. It was a weasel: they
called it 
olia and made a song about it.
From then on, Lonkundo went to the forest every day, setting his traps and catching many
animals, so that he became a wealthy man, and was highly respected.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
67 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 69 ---
One night he dreamed that he caught the sun in one of his traps. For days afterwards he was
puzzled, wondering what the dream meant, until one day he found the answer. In the middle
of the forest, he saw a light shining between the trees. When he came nearer, he saw that one
of his traps had caught a beautiful woman, bright as fire!
The woman said: 'Come close to me, do not fear me. I am the eldest daughter of a king. I
refused every man. My father did not want me to marry anyone. Put away your weapons, and
come closer, Lonkundo!'
Lonkundo approached her, admiring her fiery beauty. Meanwhile, she told him her history.
'I met a man who persuaded me to go with him. We walked and walked through the forest
until he vanished. I think he was a spirit. I lost my way and was caught in this trap. Please
release me! We will go to my father, who will reward you richly.'
Lonkundo, still admiring her flaming features, released her from his snare, saying: 'No, I want
no reward, I only want you for my wife.'
She did not resist, she did not disagree with him. She told him: 'My name is Ilánkaka,
daughter of Esombyankaka. Remember
[Page [79] ]
that you must never tell anyone how you caught me in your trap. Never mention it, or we
shall have trouble.'
They went home together to Lonkundo's village, where he built a big hut for her. They lived
in peace for a time until Ilánkaka said: 'You are an important man, you must have more
wives.'
Lonkundo replied: 'I have no possessions that are suitable to pay for brides with.'
Ilánkaka answered: 'Don't worry, I will find you as many bride-prices as you want. Now go to
the villages and find some wives for yourself.'
Lonkundo set out. After a long while he returned.
'How many wives have you got?'
'I have four.'
'That's not enough; you will want at least a hundred brides. Remember that you are a man of
substance.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
68 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 70 ---
Lonkundo set out again, journeying from village to village. His fame ran ahead of him and the
drum was beaten before he arrived.
'Lonkundo is planning to play it big, he is collecting a big troupe of dancing girls!' So the
fathers kept their daughters away from him.
Lonkundo went home and told Ilánkaka what happened. She said: 'Tomorrow I will go with
you. Let us go to that big village.'
She took her little basket with the fire-sticks. Lonkundo ordered his drummer to announce
his arrival, and the next day, when they arrived on the market square, they found all the
fathers of nubile daughters sitting there. They said: 'So, Lonkundo, you do want to marry after
all, do you? But do you have enough bride-wealth?'
Lonkundo turned to Ilánkaka, who took from her basket several dozen blades of knives and
spears and axes. One father after another was satisfied that Lonkundo was a man of unlimited
affluence and therefore a worthy son-in-law.
[Page [80] ]
Having bought all the daughters of that big village, Lonkundo wanted to take them home, but
the elders said: 'No, do not go home yet, that would not be proper. Spend the night here, and
permit us to entertain you. We need time to collect and prepare the wedding presents for you,
and the parents' gifts that brides are given on their departure from home.'
Lonkundo agreed, and spent the night in the village with one of his new wives. He told the
people that Ilánkaka was not his wife but his mother. It was right for him to say so, for in the
absence of a father it is the mother who provides the bride-price for her son to marry.
The next morning the wedding presents were brought for the bridegroom. Two slaves for
each bride, and innumerable goats and chickens. The brides said good-bye to all their
relatives. After that, Lonkundo set off on his homeward journey with his long retinue, the
slaves carrying the chickens in baskets, and pulling along the tethered goats.
When they arrived in Lonkundo's village they were welcomed in festive fashion. Lonkundo
ordered his many slaves to build huts. They all received axes and went into the forest to cut
poles. Quickly huts were built for the wives and pens for the goats. Lonkundo indicated to
each wife where she might live. Each wife had her own hut; all were provided for.
The next morning, Lonkundo showed each of his wives where she could make her plantation
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
69 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 71 ---
and cultivate her cassava. So they all settled down together in their new village. Lonkundo
became a patriarch, for his wives bore him many, many children.
Alas, a rich man will always be envied. The people of Lonkundo's village began to feel
jealousy for their wealthy and prosperous countryman. Lonkundo heard how people talked
about him with hatred in their voices. He decided to move house and build a new village
elsewhere. He told his wives:
'Tomorrow we will leave this place and never come back. Remember that no one is allowed
to take anything---all possessions
[Page [81] ]
must be left behind. Only Ilánkaka will take her little basket with the fire-sticks. The people
of my village do not love me any more, so I have decided to leave them alone. They may
have all my possessions to satisfy their greed. We will start out early in the morning, and
Ilánkaka will find an auspicious place where we shall build a new village.'
The next morning all the wives rose early, put on their few clothes and made the children
ready for departure. They heard Lonkundo give the signal and they all began their migration
behind their mighty husband. Ilánkaka led them into the forest, along the paths of the wild
pigs into the vast jungle of the Congo Basin. In the afternoon, many of the pregnant women
began to show signs of fatigue, so Ilánkaka and Lonkundo decided to call a halt there and
make a provisional camp.
While the others were preparing for the night, Lonkundo took his bow and arrows, hoping to
shoot some game. There, in the middle of the forest, he suddenly heard his own name
repeated over and over again. He stole up to the place where the voice came from, and saw a
big man standing there, apparently looking for him. Lonkundo took an arrow, shot it, and hit
the man in the leg; with the next arrow he hit his arm. Then he came out of his hiding-place
and tied up his writhing victim with a piece of liana.
He brought him to the camp, but when Ilánkaka saw him she cried out: 'That is the man who
vanished in the forest, leaving me a prey to the wild beasts!'
Lonkundo said: 'Good, I give him to you as your slave; he will serve you for the rest of his
life.'
The next morning they went on until Ilánkaka heard chattering voices in the trees. They
belonged to the parrots, and this was the omen she had been expecting. It was around those
trees that they would be building their new village. It was too late to start building then, so
they spent that night in the open air.
