[Page [82] ]
2. THE NEW VILLAGE
A human sacrifice is necessary before a clan can begin building a village. The spirits of the
locality have to be reconciled with the disturbance of the peace, and especially with the
felling of the trees in which most of them dwell. In this part of Africa all human habitation is
carved out of the forest.
It is the first duty of every woman in Africa to bear her husband children. Even though
Ilánkaka is a fire-princess who has made her husband rich and powerful, she has no children,
and that annoys her husband more than anything else.
Before they could build their new village, they had to sacrifice a human being and bury the
body in the ground, to propitiate the spirits of the forest. Lonkundo suggested to Ilánkaka that
they take the man who deserted her in the forest for their human sacrifice. Ilánkaka agreed, so
Lonkundo killed the new slave and performed the necessary ritual. Then he ordered the
slaves to cut poles for new huts. Some of the women started pounding the floors of their
future houses, others went out fishing in the nearby river.
That night the floors were beautifully level, and there was more fish than they could eat.
They also had 
lifambu fruits in plenty. Soon the huts were going up, and Lonkundo assigned
a hut to each of his wives. All were satisfied except Bonduwa, Lonkundo's first wife, who felt
that her hut was an ugly one. She complained and Lonkundo, annoyed, told her that if she did
not care to accept it he would take it away from her. Yonjwa, her son, who was beginning to
grow up, promised his mother that he would build a hut for her himself, far away in the
forest, and that she should not wish to have any more to do with his father.
When Lonkundo came back to Ilánkaka and told her what had happened, she was delighted.
She took out of her basket a nut which, she said, her father had given her as a special present.
[Page [83] ]
'This nut,' he had said, 'is very strongly loaded with magic. You may plant it as soon as you
feel that your husband really loves you.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
71 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 73 ---
'Today,' Ilánkaka added, 'I am quite sure that you love me more than anyone else, so I have
decided to plant this nut.'
The nut grew into a tall palm. The palm grew many branches. On the branches there grew big
nuts. Ilánkaka picked some nuts and shared them with her husband. They were delicious. He
asked for more.
Then he wanted to give them to his other wives and their children, but Ilánkaka refused,
saying that those nuts were meant for herself and her husband only. Lonkundo was annoyed
with her pride.
'I am not allowed to give the nuts of a tree in my own garden to my own children? Is that
because you have none of your own? Have you forgotten that I caught you in my trap?'
It was said! He had uttered it! Ilánkaka collected her belongings and went away. She never
came back and no one knew where she had gone.
Lonkundo felt ashamed for the many things he had done wrong in his life. He thought for a
long time. Then he went personally to the hut of his first wife, deep in the forest. He stood in
front of the hut and called out:
'Mother of Yonjwa, mother of my eldest son.' They both emerged from the hut and greeted
him.
Lonkundo went on: 'I want a reconciliation. I did no justice to you. I want you to come back
and occupy the best hut in my village. You are my first wife. And you, Yonjwa, you are the
eldest son of my homestead. The others are children. You are a man, I see. Go out to the
other villages and find yourself a wife. Lonkundo is rich. Your father can pay a king's
bride-price for you.'
[Page [84] ]
3. YONJW A'S WEDDING
In this chapter, the narrative moves on to the eldest son of Lonkundo, the second hero in the
dynasty. Yonjwa conquers a bride. In some countries men like their women supple and
slender, but African men like big women, strong and with abundant flesh. Eyonga is the
Congolese ideal of a bride: muscular and motherly. Once conquered, she remains loyal to her
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
72 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 74 ---
husband.
Yonjwa set out and travelled through many villages and districts, and saw hundreds of girls.
But the son of Lonkundo was not satisfied with an ordinary girl: he wanted a real heroine, a
mighty woman.
One day on the road, he met a man who was covered with oil. His whole body was greasy and
smudged. Yonjwa persuaded him to tell his history, and the man began:
'I was foolish enough to go and woo a woman called Eyonga, who was so famous for her
strength and her fighting spirit that the people called her Ondéfetana, 'the Champion'. She has
a peculiar way of finding out whether a prospective bridegroom is worthy of marriage. She
wrestles with him, and if she throws him down, he has lost. But that is not all. She insists that
the tournament should take place in a basin filled with oil. Yes, sir, her father possesses many
plantations of oil-palms, and each time a suitor arrives, she orders her father's slaves to fill a
pit in the garden as wide as a pond with pure palm-oil, and she wants to fight her suitors in
that. So far, they have all lost. I am the last one. It is a miracle that men can still be found to
court her. I really was stupid!'
When Yonjwa heard this story an inexorable desire seized him to woo this girl, and to
measure his strength against hers. Of course, he knew his own strength. He knew that he was
the son of Lonkundo, the great huntsman, the father of a tribe. Yonjwa's veins carried the
blood of heroes.
Yonjwa travelled to the homestead of Kungoelo, father of
[Page [85] ]
Eyonga. He expressed a wish to see her. He met a group of girls, who were quarrelling
together. One of them was Eyonga the Champion. She did not hide herself---she was big and
beautiful. Yonjwa told her he wanted her as his bride. She said: 'Tomorrow.' He said: 'Today.'
They went to the village. The elders said: 'Tomorrow.' Yonjwa said: 'Today .' They said: 'Her
father is not here.' Yonjwa said: 'Fetch him.'
They went out and found Kungoelo far away in the fields. They told him: 'Here is a man
wishing to marry your daughter, the Champion.'
'Can he not wait till tomorrow? Tell him I am busy.'
'He says he cannot wait. He wants Eyonga today. He looks like a great man. He is the son of
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
73 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 75 ---
Lonkundo.'
'In that case, I will come. Tell the men to make the oil-pond ready. We shall want many
barrels of oil from the plantations.'
The slaves carried out Kungoelo's orders. The elders arrived, the slaves carried the barrels,
the pit was filled with oil. Eyonga went and stood in it, with the oil up to her navel. She was
a colossal woman. Yonjwa, too, jumped into it. He was no smaller. They began to fight,
gripping one another like gorillas. Eyonga lifted Yonjwa up above her head and threw him
down. But the agile man landed on his feet, like a bush-cat, like his father's wild weasel.
Again and again she lifted him above her head. The last time she could not throw him
down---he stayed on top of her, and they both went down in the oil. They re-emerged and
again the angry woman wanted to lift him up. She seized him, and he held her, he held her
close to him. Whatever she tried, he clung to her like a liana to a tree, like the African
fig-tree clinging to the tree stem on which it lives until the stem dies and withers away. She
tried to lift him, when she felt his grip like the grip of the blacksmith's tongs on a piece of
glowing iron, she tried to fight him off, but could not. His grip was like iron, like the heavy
liana rope that holds the foot of the elephant. Very slowly he pressed her down in
[Page [86] ]
the oil. Down, down, down---and she loved him. She knew her master and admired him. He
helped her up and she declared herself vanquished.
All the spectators cheered and clapped at the great hero. Yonjwa, son of Lonkundo, said to
the bride's father: 'Kungoelo, master of oil-palms, I sought your daughter today, and I have
conquered her.'
Kungoelo declared them husband and wife. 'But,' he said, 'there is one one more thing to do,
that is, cutting oil-nuts from the giant tree.'
That night, Yonjwa and Eyonga slept together as husband and wife. Eyonga told her husband
that he must go out early and fix some branches of the oil-palm to the giant tree before her
father was up. He did so, and when Kungoelo arrived and told his son-in-law to find a giant
tree on which there grew oil-nuts, or forfeit his bride, Yonjwa soon found the giant tree, and
cut the oil-nut branches, which he had fixed there himself. So Yonjwa had done the
impossible. He kept his wife, who had found her master.
4. YONJW A GOES HOME WITH HIS WIFE
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
74 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 76 ---
Eyonga indeed becomes the mother of the Nkundo people, because she possesses the magic
Pouch of Life that is here mentioned for the first time. It will remain the chief source of life
for the dynasty in the generations to come. We never learn the nature of the mysterious
powder in it. In some ways it acts like a frog, and it may have been made of frogskin. It has
the power to rescue the family from any emergency, even from the jaws of death. It is a
protective spirit, for it can fly over the forest with all the members of the household in its
belly, and spew them out at their destination.
That night, Eyonga had a dream. A voice warned her: 'You must
[Page [87] ]
tell your husband that if anybody asks him his name he should not reply, but ask that man's
name.' She told her husband as soon as they woke up.
The next morning they set out on their way to Lonkundo's village. Kungoelo said: 'I will give
you eight slaves who will carry the bride-price back to me.'
Yonjwa said: 'Rather give me eighty.' Kungoelo gave him forty slaves and forty slave-girls to
accompany him.
In the forest they met someone who said to Yonjwa: 'Who are you?'
'I am Yonjwa!' said the son of Lonkundo proudly. And the man pierced Yonjwa with his
spear, so that he died.
Eyonga the Champion cried bitterly for her husband, lying there in the forest. Then she
remembered the magic pouch her father had given her, took some balm out of it, rubbed it
into her dead husband's nose, and at once he was revived, and rose up.
She reminded him of her dream, and he promised to remember it. The next person who
asked his name was a dark figure. Yonjwa asked him who he was.
He said: 'I am the ghost-bat: now you see me, now you don't.' And gone he was.
When they were in the middle of the forest, Yonjwa told the slaves to cut an esio -tree and
make him a slit-drum. On it, he drummed a message to his father: 'Coming home soon. Your
son brings his bride. I married the daughter of a leopard.'
Then they realized they had lost their way. Eyonga took out her father's pouch and sang a
song for it. It began to jump in front of them, and so guided them home.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
75 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 77 ---
All the time Eyonga sang:
My father's bag,
Guide me to my husband's home.
The others joined in, but no one was allowed to speak while the magic pouch bounced. It did
its guiding work perfectly, for soon
[Page [88] ]
they were home. Old Lonkundo was delighted to see that his son had found a worthy bride,
and ordered a few dozen goats to be slaughtered for a big feast. Dancing lasted all night.
The next morning, the headman of the slaves asked for the bride-price, following the
instructions of his master, Kungoelo. Lonkundo told the slaves to take their digging sticks to
a place in the forest which he pointed out to them. They started digging, and found
treasure---more than they had ever seen. They filled their bags and carried them to
Lonkundo's village. Lonkundo asked the headman if that would satisfy Kungoelo as a bride-
price. The headman said it was too much, to which Lonkundo replied: 'From Lonkundo one
can expect only a royal bride-price.'
Eyonga and Yonjwa prepared for the journey to accompany the slaves back to her father's
homestead. When they were in the middle of the forest, they lost their way again, so Eyonga
took out her father's pouch and sang a little song for it:
Open, open, little pouch,
Swallow all my father's men.
Then she invited all the slaves with their loads to step inside it, and finally Yonjwa and she
herself jumped in. There was room enough for everyone to sit comfortably. Eyonga sang:
Fly, my father's bulgy bag,
Carry us all to my father's farm!
The bag with its living contents took off and landed in the twinkling of an eye. Eyonga
opened the bag and lo! they were at her father's village.
5. THE BIRTH OF ITONDE
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
76 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 78 ---
A pregnant woman has a whimsical appetite, and it is considered mauspicious if the husband
does not succeed in obtaining whatever she desires. It is believed that it is the spirit of the
embryo that wants
[Page [89] ]
the strange dishes, as an offering. This spirit may be an ancestor who has decided to return to
earth. If he does not get what he wishes he may be annoyed and destroy the embryo.
Rats are eaten in many parts of Africa, certain sub-species are considered a delicacy, if well
fried. The rats are the animals that are in some way related to death and decay. Itonde is the
god of death. Death is born one generation after the first man is born. He is the spirit of
darkness and fear.
Yonjwa and Eyonga travelled back through the forest. They came to a place they liked and
decided to settle there for a time. Eyonga found she was pregnant, and lost all appetite. The
only thing she wanted to eat was fried rats. So Yonjwa went and set traps for rats. He caught a
dozen, half of which he fried, the other half he dried. The next morning the dried rats were
gone. The same happened the following morning. They did not know that their son, Itonde,
emerged from his mother's womb while she was asleep, and devoured the rats. Before
daybreak he plunged back into the womb. After three days he wanted to stay outside, so he
created darkness. His element was darkness, for he was the god of death. He it is who likes to
eat rats.
Yonjwa heard the cocks crow in the middle of the night. Later, he heard the drums sound in
the distance. This is usually done at nightfall in Nkundo villages. Yonjwa realized that a day
had passed without dawn and without sunset: no light had been visible at all. All this time
Yonjwa and Eyonga were lying in bed together in the darkness, for they felt drowsy and slept
continually.
When he heard the cocks crow again in the surrounding villages, Yonjwa woke up his wife,
saying: 'This place is cursed, the sun will not shine here. We must get out of it before we start
sleeping for ever and never wake up.'
As he touched his wife's belly, he noticed that it was flat---the swelling had gone. He asked
her: 'Where is your child? Are you no longer with child?' She said she did not know. This was
a sure sign that the place was cursed. They rose and ran.
[Page [90] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
77 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 79 ---
After a long walk they saw the sun shining. They left the darkness behind like a dense forest,
and saw that it was broad daylight. They passed through the villages and the people
confirmed that dawn and dusk came and went all the time as usual. They concluded that
Eyonga had had a miscarriage, and that her baby was dead. Indeed, it was Death.
Yonjwa told all the people of the villages, that if they saw a stranger coming, he would be the
powerful sorcerer whom he suspected was wandering around.
'Treat him politely, but if you can, poison him, for he is dangerous.'
The villagers answered: 'Yes, son of Lonkundo,' and the two went on.
6. ITONDE FINDS HIS FATHER
The 
elefó is a spherical dancing bell, probably imported into the Congo from the Arabian
countries. The magical characters on it are the signs of the zodiac and other astrological
ciphers. This motif is almost certainly borrowed from Islamic mythology. The peoples of the
Congo were not literate in pre-colonial days: it is the Muslims who have this strong awe for
the power of letters.
The 'divine judgement' ceremony is very common in many parts of Africa. In it, the accused
drinks a cup of venom. If it kills him he was a liar; if he lives, he spoke the truth.
Itonde, the young god of death, dispelled the darkness, took a few palm-nuts and waited for
game. He saw a dwarf antelope and threw a palm-nut at it. It hit the animal on the shin. It
died at once. Itonde roasted it and ate it. He went back to his waiting- place, saw a big
antelope, hit it with his palm-pit, roasted it and ate it. Then he hit many other animals---
except the snake and the elephant. He roasted them all and ate them. Each time he sang his
song:
[Page [91] ]
Itonde the killer, kill it.
Itonde devourer, devour it.
Finally, he saw a humming-bird. He was going to hit it, when the humming-bird sang to him:
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
78 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 80 ---
'Why would you kill me? If the big antelopes did not satisfy you, do you think my little body
will? Listen, I will tell you something you did not know. You have parents who are expecting
you. If you wait here, I will send the caterpillar-bird to you, and he will explain the road to
you.'
The humming-bird disappeared and a moment later the caterpillar-bird arrived. Itonde asked:
'Are you the caterpillar-bird? Give me what I was promised.'
The caterpillar-bird produced an 
elefó , a small dancing-bell. 'The name of this bell is World.
Inside it you will find peace and war, wealth and poverty, strength and weakness, rain and
drought, all the animals and birds, and all the languages of the world. You will see that on it
are written the signs of all plants and snakes, of winds and storms, yes, of all things on earth.'
Itonde saw it and was satisfied. At once he began to dance, and sang:
Sign of the path, show me the road, Ching, ching!
Sign of the path, the straight high-road, Ching, ching!
Itonde travelled along the road and met a man called Lomanga. Lomanga saw a young man
with fiery eyes, walking along stark naked. He knew this must be the sorcerer of whom
Lonkundo's son had spoken, so he threw his spear at him. Lomanga's spear missed, for who
can kill death? Itonde took the spear and threw it. It pierced Lomanga. Lomanga died. Does
death ever miss?
Itonde took Lomanga's clothes and put them on. Thus properly dressed, he arrived at the
nearest village. The elders received him with great courtesy, for they feared him as they
feared the cobra. They ordered their wives to prepare a meal in secret and put
[Page [92] ]
poison in it. When the meal was served, Itonde asked the elders if they would join him, but
they declined politely, saying that the food would be just enough for him; they had eaten
already.
Itonde said: 'Even a liana has eyes.' He took his magic bell and danced round with it, saying
that such was the custom in his birthplace. He sang a song to it:
My little bell, can I eat this?
My little jingle, is it good food?
The bell answered:
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
79 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 81 ---
Itonde, spirit of death, it is poison,
It is poison, do not eat.
No one dared to speak. They were all stupefied by this magic bell that could reveal the truth.
They put their fists in their mouths, as a sign of utter embarrassment. Itonde took up his
belongings and rose to go. The people followed him like a swarm of bees. He raised his spear
and turned towards them, whereupon they all fled.
Finally, Itonde arrived where he wanted to be: in the village of Yonjwa, his father. The drums
had caused his fame to fly ahead of him, so that before he arrived on the village square, the
people had all fled into their huts and were peering at him through the cracks. Itonde sat
down near Yonjwa's royal drum and took the drumsticks. This was a dangerous breach of the
tradition: only the king himself and his eldest son were allowed to sound that big drum, and
no one knew who Itonde really was.
The elders ran out to warn Itonde that disease would strike him if he sounded Yonjwa's royal
drum, but he did not listen. He drummed the signal that called all men together to the royal
compound. Yonjwa himself was away at the time, and the local people asked him why he had
sounded the great drum, well knowing that no one else would ever have the evil courage to
touch it. Yonjwa said he had not. He hurried home and found Itonde sitting there and
drumming his own royal signal. Yonjwa was
[Page [93] ]
furious---all the more so when Itonde said: 'I have beaten the drum to announce that I have
come home to you, my father.'
When he was questioned by Yonjwa he said: 'Do you not remember that someone stole the
rats? Do you not remember the darkness? Where is the child of which your wife was
pregnant? Finally, do you not see that my toes are the same as yours?'
Yonjwa was still not satisfied and it was decided that Itonde would 'drink divine judgement'.
The experts of the villages cooked the poison together. It became the most potent venom
they had ever brewed. When it was ready it was poured into a pot called Evil Smell; gaseous
vapours rose up from it which made people cough and their eyes water. If a mortal would
have drunk it he would have died like a moth in the flame. But what can kill the spirit of
death? Itonde raised the pot to his lips and swallowed its contents, gulping it down like sweet
beer. To show that the poison had no effect on him whatsoever, Itonde took his magic bell
and started a round dance. The bell rang the dancing tune:
Itonde is the killing spirit,
The poison is not killing him.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
80 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 82 ---
Itonde was now recognized as Yonjwa's son, and he demanded a big human sacrifice to
celebrate his inauguration as the crown prince, Yonjwa decreed that the Batswa or Balumbe
(the pygmies) should be killed. It was indeed a hecatomb: the Nkundo drove their spears into
the poor pygmies and massacred them. Some pygmies succeeded in escaping into the forest,
and have lived there ever since.
7. ITONDE AND THE COPPERSMITH
Itonde has many aspects: some pertain to death, such as sacrifice, poison, hunting and
darkness; some are ambivalent, such as the fire
[Page [94] ]
and forgery; some definitely belong to the symbols of life, such as the banana.
Itonde wanted to wear copper rings on his ankles, like all the other young men. He went to
the smith and ordered them. The smith tried to operate his bellows but the fire would not
flare up. Itonde pushed him away and took over, singing his magic song to the spirits of
twilight:
Big-ears bat and crest-nose bat,
Flying dog and flying squirrel,
Come and help me.
When the song was finished, the copper was white-hot. Itonde invited the smith to fix the
copper rings on his legs, but the smith failed again in his own craft. Itonde took the copper
from the moulds and fixed the rings, still red-hot on his own legs.
Then he wanted to go, but the smith claimed his fee. 'A fee for failing?' jeered Itonde. Soon
the rings broke and Itonde went back to the smith, claiming that the copper was of inferior
quality. The smith took him to court, and the elders ruled that the smith had to deliver the
rings the next day, this time without being helped by Itonde.
Itonde went to the forest and found a banana, a fruit which in those days was unknown to the
Nkundo. He came home and planted the banana in his own garden. The next morning it had
grown into an enormous tree, with hundreds of ripe bananas hanging from it. Itonde picked
the bananas and cooked a huge pot of banana-mash to which he invited the whole village.
When they had all eaten their fill they felt so heavy that they all fell asleep, including the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
81 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 83 ---
smith.
Itonde went to the smithy and fixed his rings. As soon as he woke up the smith claimed his
payment. Itonde took him to court, saying: 'This time he did nothing at all, and as payment
for his copper I let him eat until he burst out of his clothes.'
[Page [95] ]
The council of elders ruled that the smith must pay Itonde a slave for failing to keep his
promise and for not doing his work. The smith refused, but Itonde grabbed him, saying: 'You
are going to be that slave.' The smith struggled to free himself but Itonde beat him with sticks
and tied a liana round his neck.
The poor man lamented his lot: 'I was a smith and now I am a slave.' He had to work in his
own smithy as a slave. Itonde took his house and his wives became Itonde's wives.
Who will not forfeit all to the spirit of dying?
8. INDOMBE THE DRAGON
The story of Indombe, the giant copper snake, contains several mysterious motifs. Its origin
must be Semitic, for only in Semitic languages is the word for 'copper' and 'snake' the same.
The fiery-red glow of the serpent is somehow related to the sunset. Only when Itonde has
trapped the sun can he overcome the serpent. Itonde is also 'related' to the liana which people
use for rope-making.
Of the many magic beings in the Lonkundo sagas, Indombe is one of the most mysterious.
She is a feminine snake, a goddess of fire and life, and as such even she is vanquished by
death. 
En passant , the discovery of the sugar-cane by Itonde is here mentioned: another
aspect of him as a culture hero, a giver of life as well as death.
More wives were acquired by Itonde in a simple manner: When he saw one he liked he just
took her. If her husband or father had the courage to protest at all, a fight would invariably
settle the argument in Itonde's favour. But none of his wives bore him children.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
82 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 84 ---
Itonde's brother Lofale married Wangala, who soon became pregnant. Pregnant women have
odd desires. Wangala had a craving for nothing but snakes, and so the two brothers set out
into the forest in search of snakes. Suddenly they saw a bright
[Page [96] ]
light shining in one of the trees, as fiery as the sun. Lofale ran away but he had to stop before
a river, which had not been there when they came. Itonde approached the sun-in-the-tree
although even he trembled with fear. The brilliant phenomenon turned out to be the giant
serpent Indombe, all made of shining copper, curled up and coiled around the gigantic lileko
-tree. It was more than three foot thick and several miles long. Itonde sat down under the
palaver-tree and began a conversation with the serpent:
'Great Indombe, do descend from your tree that we may talk together.'
Indombe replied: 'If you want to talk to me, come back at sunset. Wait for my friends to talk
to you.'
'No, it is you I want to converse with.' After some more fruitless discussion, Itonde intoned a
song:
Indombe of the Bakongo,
Come down that I may carry you!
Indombe was furious: 'You worm, are you trying to bewitch me? You baby! Go back to your
father! Do you think you can carry me? What will you carry me on?'
Indombe was so enraged that hot flames flared up, raging and roaring. The whole forest lit up
and the faces of the people on the river bank shone red as if it were sunset. Then Indombe
uncoiled a little and put her head on Itonde's shoulder. The shoulder was badly burnt and
Itonde fell down, fainting. When he came to, he rang his magic bell and immediately he felt
better. He looked up and saw that Indombe was back in her tree, all coiled up again. He also
saw that the sun was setting, and he feared that Indombe would kill him in the darkness, so
he decided he must catch the sun. He ran to the western horizon, cut down a number of trees
and made a gigantic bird-trap. The sun, in descending, was caught in the noose and fell into
the trap. Itonde had caught the sun.
He wanted to take the sun out of his trap, but when he touched
[Page [97] ]

83 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 85 ---
it, it burnt him badly all over his body. He fell down dead, except for the hand which held the
magic bell. The hand stirred, the bell rang, Itonde sneezed and came back to life. He rose to
his feet and went back to Indombe. Indombe put coil after coil round his neck and shoulders.
Itonde kept ringing his bell, so that he grew taller and stronger all the time, while the strength
of the serpent diminished.
Itonde's feet sank down into the ground, so heavy was the snake. He waded through the
swamps with Indombe on his shoulders until they reached the river. There, he told the
serpent to uncoil from his shoulders on to the ground so that he could find a way to cross the
river. Indombe refused and suffocated Itonde, but one of its coils accidentally touched the
bell, so that Itonde revived, and put Indombe on the ground with tremendous effort, coil after
coil. As soon as he was rid of the giant serpent, he took a liana and studied it for a long time.
Then he bewitched it with a song:
Little liana, help me, my brother-in-law,
Little liana, help me to cross the river.
The liana began to sprout a branch that grew longer and longer and thicker and thicker and
broader, until it reached the other bank. Itonde set it down, and the liana branch had become a
wide bridge. They crossed the bridge, whereupon Itonde ordered it to resume its original
shape. The bridge became a liana again.
Lofale ran to the village and called to the inhabitants to fly, 'for Itonde is coming with death'.
All the people fled except Yonjwa and his wives, and Itonde's wives, who wanted to see what
Itonde would bring. Itonde put the giant snake down at the entrance to the village. At once it
uncoiled itself and quickly encircled the village, so that no one could escape. It caught every
living being in the village and swallowed all the people. Gone!
Itonde was furious: 'You monster! You have eaten my parents, my wives, my brothers and
sisters! I am going to kill you, cut you in slices and eat you up!'
[Page [98] ]
Indombe mocked him: 'You baby with your face still full of milk, you misshapen worm, I
have lived many times longer than you, how will you kill me?'
Itonde, however, went to his store-hut and took out his magic matchet, armed with which he
approached the dragon's head, all the time singing a magic song. Indombe felt that Itonde was
stronger, became despondent, and said:
'If you have to kill me, eat me all today.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
84 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 86 ---
If you leave one little piece, you will not survive.'
Itonde laid a spell in his knife and suddenly slashed through Indombe's neck. The colossal
head fell on the ground, boom! The giant body uncoiled and died. Itonde cut it in slices, put
these in pots and fried them in oil. Then he ate the lot, including all the juices, licking the
pots out. All the time he sang his little song:
Itonde, devour it, eat it all!
Only the head could not be eaten, not even by Itonde. He put it under his bed and went to
sleep.
In the middle of the night he woke up, feeling that he was rising up, with bed and all; soon he
was being pressed against the ceiling. He rang his bell, and at once whatever pressed him up
was deflated and Itonde could continue his sleep.
The next morning he saw to his terror that the dragon Indombe was back in all her
ugliness---but now it was only a ghost, who said: 'I told you that you must eat me whole; now
I have come back as a spirit. Now I will help you, for you are my master. Call your family, I
will show you a good place to live.'
They travelled along the river together until they arrived in Indombe's village, which was
very beautifully built and free of diseases.
On the road, Itonde learned that his brother Lofale had been killed by Indombe. He could not
take revenge on the serpent, who
[Page [99] ]
was already a dead spirit. So Itonde pursued a man in the forest, who tried to escape by
turning into a sugar-cane. Itonde seized him and changed him back into a man, with his feet
firmly rooted in the ground. He then killed him and buried him, as a sacrifice for the dead
Lofale.
Indombe called Itombe and said: 'Henceforth you will be called Ilelangonda. I give you this
village because you are a strong fighter. Now, good-bye to you all.' At that, Indombe coiled
up, jumped in the river and vanished.
9. THE DEA TH OF ITONDE-ILELE
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
85 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 87 ---
It is the privilege of great heroes to know the signs that forebode death. Itonde-Ilele knows
that he must go and search for the fruit and that that will mean his end.
There are numerous stories in Central and East Africa about men who meet disaster while
devoutly trying to satisfy the foolish cravings of whimsical wives.
The leprous beggar at the foot of the tree is, as we have seen before, an evil spirit. The birds
too, are spirits. The god of Life and Death, Itonde-Ilele, walks to the scene of his death, and
knows it. He has no option: it is better for him to die than for his child to be still-born. A wife
has the power of life and death over her husband, for she carries his child, which is his own
life in the next generation. Nature mourns Itonde.
Ilelangonda, or Ilele, as Itonde is now called for short, lived happily with his wives in
Indombe's village. One day they saw a long-boat full of fishermen rowing past on the river.
Ilele called them, but they shouted back insults. Ilele seized the boat and tied the men up,
until they begged him to let them free, and apologized saying: 'We did not know the great
Ilele lived here, it will not happen again.'
They bartered their fish and Mbombe, Ilele's favourite wife,
[Page [100] ]
bought a likoko -fish, which she roasted and ate. The bone stuck in her throat and was
removed with great difficulty. From that day Mbombe was pregnant, and like all pregnant
women, she had her whims.
One evening Ilele told his wives: 'This night my death will come flying over the house, it
will be dropped in the yard. Nobody is allowed out at any time.'
They all obeyed except the pregnant Mbombe, who got it into her head that she 
must have
some fresh air, so she went out of her hut and walked about in the yard in the middle of the
night. At that moment a horn-bill came flying over with a sau -fruit in his bill. Mbombe fell
in love with the bird and called it. The hornbill dropped the sau , which fell on the yard.
Mbombe picked it up and tasted it. It was delicious---sour-sweet---and she ate it all. From
that moment she craved sau -fruits and refused any other food. She cried day and night until
Ilele gave in and started out in search of the sau -tree.
Ilele took his bell, raised it towards the east and towards the west. When it was pointed at the
western sky the bell began to ring. Ilele started out in a westerly direction until he found the
sau -tree. At its foot he found a beggar sitting, with scabby sores all over his body; his name
was Fetefete. The beggar refused to go away unless Ilele promised to throw him a fruit. Ilele

climbed the tree and threw a fruit down which hit Fetefete on his sores. Fetefete was angry
and began to yell:
'Master of the fruits, someone is stealing, Sausau, someone knocked my sores open.'
Sausau, the owner of the tree, heard it, and sent his servants, who were all birds, to go and
chase the thief away. They arrived near the tree in great swarms and attacked Ilele, but he
succeeded in hitting each of them with a 
sau -fruit. He jumped out of the tree with two
baskets full of fruit, which he brought to his wife. He gave her one of the baskets, and she ate
all the fruit at one go.
[Page [101] ]
Then she started crying until he gave her the other basket. When that was finished they went
to bed.
The next morning she began again, crying on and on. Ilele told his wife: 'Do you realize that
if I go in search of those fruits today, I shall die there and never come back?'
She retorted: 'And if I do not get more of those fruits, I will have a miscarriage!' Ilele gave in,
for she had his first-born in her womb. He told her: 'When my horn that hangs on the wall
there begins to bleed, I shall be dead. My snare-string will turn into a snake, the monkeys
will come and cry in the yard, the elephants will come out of the forest into the open, and the
sky will rain.'
Ilele left his house knowing he would never come back. He arrived at the tree, climbed it,
and threw down the fruit which the beggar demanded. Fetefete yelled when it hit his sores
and began singing his magic evocation of the Master of the fruits. Sausau called together all
his birds and asked them if anyone knew a device to catch Ilele.
The humming-bird said: 'What will you give me if I tell you?' It got three rings as a present
and said: 'Ask the caterpillar-bird.'
The caterpillar-bird was called, received a thousand rings and said: 'If Ilele loses his magic
bell he will die.'
The birds flew together to the 
sau -tree, with their nets. The pheasant climbed the tree,
slowly, slowly. Suddenly it struck Ilele in the eyes with its wings. Ilele dropped his bell and
fell out of the tree. The tortoise caught him in his net. All the birds beat Ilele until he died.

--- PAGE 89 ---
In his hut, blood leaked from his horn. Monkeys appeared in the trees and cried loudly.
Elephants emerged from the forest and walked up to the village. Many wild animals called at
the house of the deceased king and wailed loudly. The sky started pouring sheets of rain and
it grew pitch-dark. The women understood, unbound their hair and began to lament.
Mbombe, alone of all living creatures, refused to cry, for at that moment her labour began.
[Page [102] ]
10. THE BIRTH OF LIANJA
The body of the god of death is full of the creatures of death: worms, maggots, flies and ants.
When he dies the birds peck holes in his body and insects swarm out of it: that is the origin
of vermin. Itonde's horn, like Roland's, announces the disaster.
Like Ichakejana, the Zulu hero, Lianja is born through his mother's shin. Lianja is in
possession of his father's bell, the secret of Itonde's life. Lianja is born at the moment of his
father's death: the father is reborn in the son. At once he wants to know why his father died,
and the mother is afraid, for her own foolish wish caused her husband's death.
There lay the body of Ilele the great chief, caught in the net of the Tortoise. The birds were
crowding round it, wishing to get a portion of the meat. They pecked it open with their
bills---and it was full of worms. Maggots swarmed, ants crept in and out, and flies buzzed.
The body was rapidly decaying. Water oozed out of it and and collected around it, making the
ground marshy. This was how the swamps were formed. The leaking body-water of Ilele
became a stream---the origin of the marsh rivers.
In Mbombe's hut, where the horn was bleeding, Ilele's wife lay in labour. Out of her lap came
ants and flies, and insects of every type: this was how insects came into the world. After that
she gave birth to the six ancestors of the Mongo-speaking peoples: Balúmbe, Bafotó,
Bielángi, Mbóle, Nkundó and Elíngá. Thirdly, Mbombe gave birth to a girl named Nsongo,
bright and beautiful.
Finally the deep bass of a male voice was heard from Mbombe's womb: 'Along which path
shall I go out?'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
88 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 90 ---
His mother answered: 'Do you not see the path along which your fellows came out?'
The son replied haughtily: 'Can the son of a king walk along the road of slaves? I want a path
of my own. Take some of my father's white clay and smear it on your shin.'
[Page [103] ]
The princeling's first order was quickly obeyed: the slave women hurried for him before he
was even born. They applied his father's white clay, used normally for festive occasions, to
his mother's shin-bone. At once it began to swell up, split open, and there appeared Lianja,
the great hero, fully armed with all his father's weapons: the chief's staff, the royal necklace,
bow and arrow, the royal spear, the magic knife, Itonde's bell and his blowing horn. Lianja
was born a grown-up man, ready for battle to avenge his father who had died that day.
At once he demanded from his mother an explanation of why his father had died. Mother
Mbombe, fearful lest her son would meet the same enemies that destroyed his formidable
father, lied and said: 'I wanted some palm-nuts, and your father fell from a palm tree when
picking them for me.'
Lianja went out at once, found a palm-nut tree, climbed it, jumped down from the top, and
landed on his feet, unhurt. He returned in his mother's hut and started a row: 'Mother, you
lied to me, you concealed the true cause of my father's death and you know I must avenge
him. Tell me the truth.'
His mother, dreading the moment that her son would encounter the terrible birds, invented a
half-lie: 'I wanted some fish, your father went to catch it for me and was drowned by the
river.'
Lianja rushed out, jumped in the river, dived to the bottom, surfaced and swam to the shore.
He rushed back to his mother's hut, shouting: 'You lied again, the river did not kill him, it did
not kill me! You are hiding the truth. Perhaps you killed him yourself. If you did I will kill
you, and if you don't tell me the truth I will kill you also, so you might as well tell me!'
He took his knife and wounded his mother. She said: 'All right, do not kill me, I will tell you
the truth, I craved the fruits of the 
sau -tree, so your father went to the land of Sausau to pick
them for me, and the Master of the fruits killed him.'
[Page [104] ]

11. LIANJA A VENGES HIS FATHER
Nsongo, Lianja's twin sister, leads a mysterious double life of man and woman. She
accompanies him with whom she shared the womb, on all his expeditions. Nsongo was also
predestined to become Lianja's wife, although this is not explicitly stated by the narrators.
The idea is that: 'After all they have been sleeping together in the womb already.'
Their grandmother's magic pouch reappears in this story. It is the first time we learn that it
contains snuff. Sneezing is associated with waking up to a new life.
Lianja went out, called up all the men of the village and told them to prepare for war. When
they were ready they set out, while Nsongo sang a song:
Lianja, son of my mother, go out to battle,
If you don't fight, may your shield be torn.
The army crossed three rivers by means of plaited mats. When they arrived near the 
sau -fruit
tree they sat down and made camp there. Lianja climbed the tree and began cutting down
fruits and branches.
The little beggar ran to his father and said: 'The son of Ilele has come to take revenge!'
Sausau collected his army and rushed to the sau -tree. All his men and all Lianja's men were
killed in the ensuing battle. Finally, the two heroes approached each other and engaged in a
terrific fight. The forest echoed with the blows, until at last Lianja killed Sausau.
Then Lianja took some of his own magic snuff from his pouch. He pushed a little pinch of it
into the nostrils of each of his dead men. Every man sneezed noisily and woke up, alive.
[Page [105] ]
12. LIANJA AND THE SUN
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
90 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 92 ---
The sparrow-hawk receives the sun in heaven and fixes it in its orbit. This tale may perhaps
echo that of the Egyptian sun-god Horus, the sparrow-hawk. Yemekonji is the name for God.
Lianja gave the dead Sausau the same treatment. Sausau woke up and said: 'If you make my
people live again, I will agree to serve you, together with them.' Lianja assented, on condition
that they would become human beings. This was agreed. Lianja gave all the dead bird-spirits
his magic snuff and they all woke up as normal human men. They all acclaimed Lianja as
their king and followed him.
They marched away until they reached a village, and Lianja told Nsongo to go and ask the
villagers who they were. They answered: 'We are Balúmbe, we are King Lianja's people.'
Their men joined Lianja's army.
At the next village they received the answer: 'We are Bafotó, we are Lianja's people,' and
their men too joined the army. Nsongo accompanied her brother's army and acted as
messenger. Lianja observed that the sun neither rose nor set and this caused him worry. The
sun must be suspended somewhere, but where? Lianja decided to send the sparrow-hawk as
his messenger to Yemekonji high in heaven.
The fly volunteered to go on the same mission, but as an independent observer. The fly said
to the sparrow-hawk: 'Take no notice of me, I will go and eavesdrop on the meeting of the
celestials.'
The councillors in heaven decided: 'We will make three parcels, one containing soil, one
containing leaves, and one containing the sun, and we will make the sparrow-hawk choose.'
They made three parcels. Two of them were painted in bright colours, but the one that
contained the sun was left unpainted. They
[Page [106] ]
said: 'If he chooses the earth, he may go back to earth, but if he chooses the leaves, he will go
on wandering for ever like falling leaves.'
The fly heard all this and flew quickly to the sparrow-hawk to tell the story, urging him:
'Remember, choose the parcel with no colours at all. Those that are brightly painted contain
only worthless things. The sun is in the uncoloured parcel.'
The sparrow-hawk flew to Yemekonji. Yemekonji said:
'In the past, you people of the earth have spoilt things. Ilele sent a man who did not know
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
91 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 93 ---
what the sun was. He chose the morning star. Later a man came who left the sun lying here
and took the moon. I showed him how to place the moon in the sky. So now they have the
moon and the evening star, with their companions. Now open your wings and show me if you
have any spies there with you.'
The sparrow-hawk answered: 'Yemekonji, you who know everything, I am only a speck of
dust in your eyes, I hid nobody in my wings.'
Yemekonji, however, knew everything in advance. He knew that Lontsingo, the fly, had
eavesdropped on the deliberations. He knew that the sun would be carried away by the
mortals. Had he not revealed all this in parables?
The sparrow-hawk was then invited to make his choice. After inspecting each parcel closely
and carefully, he finally picked out the sun-parcel and bowed to Yemekonji, who told him:
'As soon as you reach the clouds, open this parcel and pull your feathers together.' The
sparrow-hawk left heaven with the sun-parcel in his claws. Down, down, down he circled,
until he reached the silver clouds. There he undid the parcel, and the golden sun rolled out
and shot its rays through the sky. The sparrow-hawk fixed the sun in its course, and its light
descended on earth. The people on earth sing hymns in praise of the sparrow-hawk:
[Page [107] ]
The sparrow-hawk flew to the limits of the sky
and brought a message from Yemekonji.
The sparrow-hawk is like a father, he gave us light.
The sparrow-hawk flew through the clouds
and unwrapped the radiant sun.
He placed the sun-disc on its path
and brought its rays to men and beasts.
When the sparrow-hawk came down to earth and perched on a branch, there were all the
creatures of the earth waiting to greet him. The wild beasts of the jungle were there, the
fishes, the birds and even the insects, to ask him the news from heaven. The sparrow-hawk
related what Yemekonji had told him:
'The sun will descend and then disappear with the number twelve. Afterwards, it will
reappear and rise again with the number twelve. Thus it will be for all time, by God's will.'
Lianja decreed that as a reward the sparrow-hawk would be allowed to eat chickens.

--- PAGE 94 ---
13. THE ORIGIN OF THE WHITE PEOPLE
In this story we find the first mention of the great diviner Bongenge, who is to Lianja what
Merlin was to King Arthur: a protector as well as a magician.
This notion of the origin of the white people is not uncommon in the region: i.e. that white
men are the sons of spirits, and have learned their technical tricks from ghosts in the forest.
White is the colour of apparitions. Obviously, whites are not normal people. Ordinary healthy
children are born chocolate brown and grow up to be as black as bilberries.
Yendembe, one of Lianja's daughters, appeared to be pregnant without being married. The
girl maintained and swore that she had known no man. Lianja consulted the famous diviner
Bongenge,
[Page [108] ]
who pronounced:
'Lianja's daughter is pregnant of twins, a boy and a girl. Their father is a god; therefore they
will be white-coloured children instead of black, as human babies ought to be. Because they
are not the children of a man they will want to live separately as if they were superior beings,
and they will make things we have never seen and engage in activities we have never heard
of. They will be married together.'
When Lianja's daughter's time had come, she gave birth to a boy first, and then a girl, both
quite white. The two kept apart from the black children and began to speak a different
language. At fifteen, they asked to have a separate house built for them, a sign that they
wished to sever the bonds with the clan. They began to write with pens on flat surfaces. To
the amazement of the black people they started forging iron, saying they were building a boat.
Who had ever heard of making a boat out of iron, which sinks like a stone? But the iron ship
did not sink, and when they had added a propeller they could travel together along the rivers
without engaging rowers. They built their own house downstream, with a roof made of iron,
and also with keys made of iron instead of leather thongs to fasten the doors. The white
woman became pregnant and gave birth to twins, a boy and a girl, who also married together.
14. NSONGO'S DEATH
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
93 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 95 ---
Like all the heroes of this cycle, Likinda, Lianja's son, has a magic object. Lonkundo has his
snare, Ilánkaka her fire-basket, Eyonga her pouch, Itonde has his bell, Lianja his snuff.
Likinda has a mysterious pot that predicts the future and diagnoses diseases: a divining jar.
Nsongo, Lianja's wife, gave birth to a son, Likinda. Likinda grew up quickly and became a
great warrior. After many adventures, such as the wars with the Ngombe people, he visited
the country
[Page [109] ]
of Sausau and fell in love with Boale, the daughter of Sausau. For the bride-price Lianja paid
sixty copper rings, and other valuables such as tools. Sausau gave a hundred chickens and
thirty goats to the newly-weds, to start them off.
Boale became pregnant. One day her husband went to the swamps to set fish-traps. Boale
followed her husband, suddenly she heard a fish moving in a hollow tree-stem in the swamp.
She put her hand into a hole and caught the fish but could not withdraw her hand. She called
her husband to help her, and Likinda killed it with a fish-spear. It was a tough species, a bulgy
type of fish. They went home together, and the fish was cooked and eaten by the family.
Suddenly Nsongo, Lianja's twin sister, fainted and no medicine could cure her. She was
gravely ill. Likinda consulted his magic pot and the voice of the pot answered: 'Nsongo is
going to die.'
Lianja was desolate. In deep despair he called the famous diviners of the land, Boyoyo,
Lofonde the frog who jumps and prances, and Bongenge, the greatest soothsayer of them all,
who had given so many correct predictions already. Bongenge of the Bells began to sing:
Lianja, stop consulting the diviners,
None can help your sister's ailment.
Nsongo will not recover.
Nsongo never woke up. She died the same day. Lianja cried like a small boy for his twin
sister, bright, beautiful Nsongo.
15. THE BIRTH OF LIKINDA'S FIRST CHILD
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
94 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 96 ---
Like Mbombe, her husband's grandmother, Boale, Likinda's wife, feels that weeping over a
death in the family would bring ill luck over the baby she is just giving birth to. There is a
more mysterious
[Page [110] ]
reason, one that only mothers know: the child, in the process of appearing, receives the spirit
of the person who has just died, and the old personality is born in a new body. Why should a
mother weep if the deceased is being born out of her own body? Nsongo is reborn as her dear
brother's first granddaughter. There remains a warm relationship between the old hero and
this little girl who bears his twin sister's name.
Boale was singing a monotonous song to herself, in order to overcome the pangs of labour.
The women of the household rebuked her, saying: 'You young woman from the forest, we are
all wailing, we mourn the famous Nsongo, your husband's dearest mother. And you merely
sing, on the very day of her death!'
Boale merely replied: 'I cannot mourn, I must not wail, as the spears of pain are drilling in my
belly!'
The women fell silent, and suddenly they heard the child crying inside Boale's womb. The
tiny voice called: 'Mother Boale, I am Nsongo, I am coming to my brother! Lianja, son of my
mother, come and give me the clothes of Nsongo, give them to mother Boale!'
Lianja was called at once and informed of the event: 'Lianja, grieve no longer, Nsongo is
coming today!'
The girl was born and gladly welcomed by her father and grandfather. But however much joy
she gave her grandfather Lianja, he could not forget his twin-sister, and mourned her for
seven years. One day, Lianja stooped and felt a severe pang of headache. Nsongo, who was
now a little girl of seven, cried for him, and suddenly the pain was over.
She said: 'Grandfather, I am here to care for you, always. Please do not say, as you so often
do, that you will soon have to follow your sister Nsongo.'
Lianja answered his beloved granddaughter: 'It is not I who wish to go, it is Yemekonji who
has written that I must follow Nsongo and our father Ilelangonda. Like him, I shall die in
battle.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
95 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 97 ---
[Page [111] ]
Nsongo answered: 'If you die, I will disappear too, and no one will know where I have gone.'
16. LIANJA SEES HIS WHITE GRANDCHILDREN
It is not clear why this story is out of its chronological order. It may be a modern addition,
woven into the fabric of the narrative. Lianja is the ancestor not only of the black people but
also of the white people, such seems to be the message of this episode.
Yendembe was Lianja's third daughter. He had spoilt her by announcing that no one could
give her orders, except for 'come and eat'. The little girl's maternal grandmother had once
asked her to fetch water but the child refused. When grannie asked her to pick some
peppercorns the child just started crying, and refused to eat anything. As a result she soon fell
ill and faded away. Old grandmother took her knife and made a small incision in the child's
skin, so that people would think a snake had bitten her. The child fainted and died.
Lianja was inconsolable. He invited Bongenge, the famous diviner, to come and smell out the
cause of his daughter's death. 'Is it a cobra or a viper?' Bongenge sat down with his
paraphernalia and his magic bell, and began to narrate what had happened in his own
sing-song manner. Without having consulted anyone, he knew about many things he had
never even seen.
Grandmother is the evil one, the mother's mother,
I see her looking at the child, 
ngelinge ,
I see her carving the child's skin, ngelinge ,
To make it look like snake bite, ngelinge ,
She is a dangerous witch, casting spells.
This was the origin of witchcraft. Lianja's mother-in-law was the first woman to practise it.
She was banned from the village.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
96 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 98 ---
[Page [112] ]
Lianja found his magic snuff, after threatening to kill his mother-in-law, who had hidden it.
He pushed some of the powerful powder into the little girl's nostrils. She sneezed, woke up,
and rose, hale and hearty. It was this girl, Yendembe, who later bore the white twins without
a father.
For many years, Lianja had been searching in vain to see his freak grandchildren, the
intelligent white twins. One day he started out again, leaving his big blowing-horn behind,
and told his wife Nsongo: 'If the horn leaks water, I am tired and sweating on my journey, but
if you see blood oozing from it, I am dying.'
He went on his way saying: 'No profit without journey.' After nightfall he heard a thunderous
noise like men fighting a battle. 'If only my father Ilelangonda were here,' exclaimed Lianja
aloud. Suddenly he saw a white apparition in the dark forest, speaking to him in a hollow
voice:
'Know that what you think is a war, is not. It is the ship of the children of Yendembe,
travelling along the river. If you want to meet them, go and wait on the river-bank. You will
see them when they come back.'
Lianja asked the spirit: 'Are you God?'
'I am not God.'
'Are you my sister Nsongo?'
'I am not.'
'Are you the spirit of my father?'
'I am Ilelangonda, but do not fear, I will go with you. Together we will go to the river. It is
your father who is speaking.'
After arriving on the river-bank, Lianja built a temporary hut for himself, and started hunting
pigs. When he heard the noise coming back he ran to the bank and saw the ship. He waved
and shouted, but the people could not hear him. At last they saw him and stopped their ship.
They invited him to join them on their voyage and took him to their house, where they
received him hospitably. He stayed with them for two weeks.

17. THE DEA TH OF KING LIANJA I
Yendembe, Lianja's favourite daughter, the one who was embraced by a god, is the one
surviving member who has the most intimate relations with him. This means also that she
knows the language of the birds. She can force the leopards and even the spirits to pay their
respect to the great king.
One day, Lianja found a stream in which a man called Ngimokeli had made barriers, and
caught so many fish that the nets were bulging. Lianja took some of the fish for himself, since
the nets would soon be torn; also, he considered that stream as part of his property.
Ngimokeli appeared and started uttering insults against Lianja's mother. This angered Lianja,
and the two men started a fight and killed each other.
Yendembe saw the magic horn bleeding and knew her father was dead. She went out and
stood on the road to the forest. There she called a weaver-bird:
'Weaver-bird, weaver-bird, if father Lianja is dead, start weaving!' The weaver-bird began to
weave at once.
'Weaver-bird, if my father is dead, destroy your nest!' At once, the weaver-bird tore down
what it had just woven.
'Monkeys, climbers of trees, if father Lianja is dead, why are you not wailing?' At once the
monkeys started wailing loudly in their trees.
'If father Lianja is dead, may the leopard roar!' Deep in the forest the leopard roared,
mourning the dead king.
Yendembe spoke her final incantation: 'If my father Lianja died, let the dead come here!' At
once the misty spirits of the dead appeared from all sides, crowding the deceased king's
compound, to pay homage at the home of the great ruler.
Intrepid, Yendembe went on: 'Now let the dead return.' At once, the ghostly apparitions
withdrew, dispersed and vanished in the forest.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do

--- PAGE 100 ---
[Page [114] ]
Not long afterwards, the girl Nsongo disappeared and was never seen again.
18. THE BIRTH OF LIANJA II, THE SMITH
The king is dead: long live the king. The Congolese philosophy of metempsychosis creates
an atmosphere of gay expectation. When a great chief is dying there is bound to be one
woman in labour in the household. The spirit of the deceased is no sooner released from one
body than it plunges into the body of a baby who is just being born, and into a new life. As a
result, the characters of the story have an un-individualistic hazy existence, protracted over
several generations.
In the story of the birth of Lianja II, Ilánkaka has suddenly reappeared. She was the
fire-woman who got caught in Lonkundo's trap in the forest. She helped him create a nation
but she never bore him children. Now she appears as the wife of his great- grandson, Lianja,
whose other wife, who is called Nsongo like his twin sister and his granddaughter, dies in the
same episode of the narrative.
All the women were wailing and mourning, except Ilánkaka, wife of Lianja, the king who had
just died.
'Why don't you mourn?'
'I cannot mourn, for the pains of labour are drilling through my body.'
She gave birth to a daughter and the women said: 'Now, mourn and weep, for your child is
born.'
She said: 'I cannot weep, for there is still pain in my womb.'
A moment later a son was born; in a short while he grew up into a big man. The women were
terrified and stammered: 'Look this is the king! He looks like Lianja!'
His brother Likinda asked him: 'What is your name?' The boy answered: 'My sister is Nsongo
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
99 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 101 ---
and I am Lianja, the Smith. We
[Page [115] ]
both have the magic bell. Now will you tell me who killed our father Lianja?'
As soon as Likinda had told him what happened at the brook, young Lianja suggested they go
and take their revenge on the people of Ngimokeli. So the two brothers set out. Of course
they won the battle, but only after fierce fighting and with generous use of the magic snuff
that brings people back to life.
After the conquest of Ngimokeli's village they captured all the nets, hooks, lines, harpoons
and other fishing gear for which Ngimokeli's men were so famous. They decided to go on a
fishing expedition and spend the night on the river-bank. That night, young Lianja had a
dream. A voice told him: 'You are comfortable here in the wilderness, but at home there is
bitter sorrow.' When they arrived home they found that their sister Yendembe had died; she
had simply cried herself to death. They consulted Bongenge who confirmed that no witch had
killed her this time; sorrow alone had done it. It took a long time before the magic powder
made her sneeze.
When she became fully conscious she said: 'I am Yendembe, Lianja's daughter, no one could
give me orders except "come and eat". I have seen my grandfather Ilelangonda, who died
many years ago. He told me that we were sinning and that the world would therefore exist
only a few more years. Grandfather told me: "Return to life in your human body, and grieve
no more. Wait patiently until God calls you. Tell Likinda that his mother must come now; she
is expected in the world of spirits." That is what my grandfather said. I could see him as
bright as the moon at night. And now I have come back to you, happily.'
The next day Lianja II Boketsu was hunting in the forest, when he suddenly heard his magic
bell ring. He thought: Lianja my father told me that if ever I heard this bell ring some
misfortune would have fallen on the family. He went home and found Yendembe crying near
her mother Nsongo's bed. Lianja entered
[Page [116] ]
his father's widow's hut. Yendembe said: 'I am not crying, it is just the smoke that hurts my
eyes.'
Lianja Boketsu said: 'I want the magic pouch of our father, Lianja.'Likinda agreed and went to
fetch it. They applied the magic powder and sang the magic song:
Oh powder of the Sneeze of Life,
Kangili, kangili ,

Oh pouch of Lianja,
Oh pouch of the crying Mbeo -beast.

'He who knows everything does not know the tortoise yet.'

Do not say it was Bongenge, ngelinge ,

Her sorrow was too large for her, ngelinge ,

'Am I not master of all the villages? This I say to Likinda: I have made Yendembe my
witness. She warned him that Nsongo would die. To apply the magic powder is trying God. If
