Lianja touches it again he will die. Yendembe, do not grieve. I will be with you.'
19. ELOKO THE BOGYMAN
The Lianja cycle of stories is something of a novel, a family chronicle. Each chapter fits in its
proper place, though very often the student has to search in the earlier chapters to find the
first hidden roots of the drama that later develops.
Nevertheless, the myths of the Nkundo people will always remain a subject of discussion
among mythologists. There are so many mysterious characters, whose reappearance at a later
stage in the narrative makes their significance only more puzzling.
The word 
Eloko (pl. Biloko ) denotes a race of semi-real forest- dwellers, who can kill and
be killed but are not wholly human. 'Bogymen' seems the best rendering in English, although
they also resemble the Scandinavian trolls.
Yendembe is the most human of all the characters in the great Lianja romance. One of her
very human aspects is that she always seems to attract ill luck. Being spoilt by her father is
clearly considered the origin of all misfortune. Being bewitched by her grandmother is a
blemish that never wears off. Becoming an unmarried mother is a terrible disgrace in Africa.
Giving birth to a pair of albinos is even worse.
Likinda appears to be an experienced magician. He can be transformed into a delicious fruit
and let himself be eaten by his enemy, while still preserving his spiritual identity. To eat one's
enemy does not mean his end, as one might suppose. If the enemy possesses
[Page [118] ]
magical powers he will kill his eater from inside. This is why extreme precaution should be
taken by everyone not to eat dangerous fruits. A man can eat his death. To open the bowels is
one way of getting rid of the evil spirit, but a clever spirit will manage to stay inside and eat
his victim from within. Thus does Likinda, the grandson of Death.
We have already seen how Yendembe, who is born as one of the many daughters of Lianja I,
became the first human being to suffer the evil effects of witchcraft, and all because her royal
father spoilt her. Her maternal grandmother was banned from court for her sake and was
never heard of again. Later, Yendembe was mysteriously made pregnant by an unknown god
or spirit and gave birth to the first white people.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
102 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 104 ---
When this first white couple moved out of Nkundoland in their iron craft, their grandfather
Lianja went out in search of them. He did not find them that first time, for he was confused,
and wandered aimlessly in the forest. He had a vision of his father Ilelangonda, and of his
mother Mbombe (Nsombe).
He heard a voice calling him, but searched for the caller in vain. He consulted the great
war-wizard Bongenge who, as usual, knew the answer:
The one who gave you visions, ngelinge ,
Do not say it is Bongenge, ngelinge ,
It is the owner of the bedsteads, ngelinge ,
And of the hidden sleeping-places, ngelinge ,
It is Eloko and his servants, ngelinge ,
The master of voices and visions, ngelinge .
This was the first apparition of Eloko the Bogyman, and old king Lianja was the first to suffer
from his visitations. Later in the history the bogy spirits return.
After the birth of the albinos Yendembe was married off to a prince of the Ngombe, a rather
hostile people, but her father Lianja I probably considered this a wise political move. After
six
[Page [119] ]
years of life with that foreign people she returned home for good, not having borne her
husband any children, and never having seen her two albino children since they had left her.
After the death of her father, Lianja I, the elephants greatly increased in numbers and began
to destroy the fields. This was also blamed on Yendembe, because it was revealed in a dream
to the wife of one of the diviners that the elephants were bewitched and wanted to kill
Yendembe, whom no one could give orders to.
This happened in the same night in which Yendembe had died grieving for her father and was
subsequently brought back to life by the magic powder. After that time, her brothers Likinda,
Lianja II, Boketsu, Itonde II and Lontsinga 'the Fly' were increasingly engaged in hunting
elephants. One day Likinda and Itonde killed an enormous elephant. They went to find
Lianja and his men to help them carve the body up and divide it.
After Likinda and Itonde had left the body of the elephant, all the elephants of the forest
gathered on that spot in order to revive their brother with magic means: they took large
branches and beat the body with them, but without success. When Lianja's men arrived, they
simply chased away the big animals. When they had carved up the body of the dead elephant,
Lianja decided to make a thanksgiving to the forest god for this successful hunt, at the
ceremonial place where their forefathers had previously sacrificed to the god.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
103 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 105 ---
On their way back they ran into a gang of Eloko's servants, the bogymen. They started
fighting and killed all Lianja's men. Lianja, left alone, met Itoli, the grass-starling, who
helped him with useful advice. Itoli told him to collect mbole -fruits, make a mash of them
with salt, and place them in the path of the bogymen, Lianja first revived his comrades with
his magic powder, then told them to collect the mbole -fruits. As soon as the eloko - spirits
found their favourite dish waiting for them on the path, they fell on it and forgot the war with
Lianja. The latter surrounded them with his men, and killed all the bogymen---except one.
[Page [120] ]
Itoli, perched on a branch, warned Likinda: 'You did not kill all the Biloko , you omitted the
little Inkankanga, the wizard, and his wife. They ran away in time.'
Likinda asked: 'Give me a charm to have them too.'
Itoli said: 'You have it and you know it.'
Likinda went and found the well in the forest where Inkankanga, the little wizard and his
wife, usually came to drink. Likinda sat down and changed himself into a baby. The wizard's
wife arrived at the well and picked up the poor babe, but her husband, being himself familiar
with magical practices, said sharply: 'Put that back where you found it! That is not a baby at
all, but Likinda who killed our people!'
The woman said: 'Nonsense! This is not a warrior! Anybody can see that this is only a small
child! I want to keep him and bring him up as my own child!'
'Be it on your own head, you will see what happens.'
They travelled home together, and each time the wife saw a fruit-tree she said to her
husband: 'Later when our baby is a big boy, he will climb the tree for us old people and pick
the fruits, and throw them down.'
When they came to a 
senja fruit-tree, the wizard's wife began again: 'If only our boy were big
...'
Suddenly the baby (Likinda) began to talk, and said: 'Mother! Put me on a tree so that I can
climb to the top and pick you some fruits!'
She placed him with his chest against a tree and the Likinda- baby climbed up and began to
throw down senja -fruits for his foster-mother. She began to eat at once, and enjoyed the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
104 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 106 ---
fruits so much that her husband was tempted and called to the child in the tree: 'Throw one
for me too!'
That, of course, was sealing his own doom. Likinda had been waiting for just that. He
selected an extra juicy and well-rounded fruit and crept into it himself. The fruit came down,
and Inkankanga caught it. He ate it up whole and it sank down into his belly.
[Page [121] ]
When they had enough fruit, the pair called the little baby back: 'Come down from up there.'
Likinda replied gleefully from inside Inkankanga's body: 'I am already here, I am sitting in
the bogyman's belly!'
The wizard was terrified, for to eat a spirit means certain death. And Likinda, the grandson of
Ilele, was a very powerful spirit.
The wizard said to his wife: 'If I do not get rid of him, you will die. You silly old woman, I
told you it was not a baby but the mighty Likinda. Now I have eaten him! He is inside me!'
Suddenly, he jumped up and ran as quickly as he could. Then he stopped and asked: 'Mr
Likinda, did you perchance stay behind?'
But Likinda replied from the bogyman's bowels: 'Oh no! I am still with you, I want to see if
you cannot invent more clever tricks! Did you think I could not keep up with you? Come, let
us go on travelling together!'
The bogyman knew that Likinda was going to kill him, so he called his wife and stabbed her,
shouting: 'You die first! You caused my death!' She died---and he ran away.
As soon as he saw a river he threw himself into the water. He swam round, saying: 'Mr
Likinda, you stay here in the water, I will come back later and meet you here.'
Likinda replied from inside his stomach: 'Oh no! You go on and carry both of us!' The
bogyman-wizard crept back on to the shore, found a pit and began to empty his bowels into it,
hoping in this way to shed Likinda.
He said: 'Mr Likinda, why don't you stay in this pit, it's perfect for catching wild animals!'
Likinda answered: 'Not on your life! Come on, carry us along!' The bogyman walked and
walked until he was tired. He said: 'Mr Likinda, you didn't want to kill me, did you? You just
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
105 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 107 ---
want to torture me, don't you? The sun is setting, and I feel sleepy. Just let me lie down here,
and you can do as you please.'
He sat down, sadness filling his heart because he knew death was
[Page [122] ]
approaching. Likinda jeered at him: 'Have you nothing better to do than catch lice on your
empty head? Have you no more magic tricks? Do you think the fisherman who has shipped a
live crocodile can sleep? Don't you know that I am a grandson of Ilelangonda, the spirit of
death? Don't you remember that your people killed the men of Lianja, my brother? Come on,
carry me!'
But Inkankanga had given up. He decided that life was not worth suffering for in this way. He
just sat there and waited for the inevitable end. Likinda grew impatient. He crept into the
heart and tore it to pieces so that the bogyman died. Then he drew his hunting knife and
slashed through the skin of his prison. He stepped out and left the corpse lying there, after
having taken the little bag of 
yenyi -skin, the wizard's most cherished charm.
Now Likinda was lost in the dark forest, but God had pity on him and showed him the path to
his village. There, he was received with cheers of joy; the people spread out a path of mats
for him to tread on, and his brother Lianja gave a big beer party. They improvised songs:
I love my brother, the measure is full!
Likinda told them how how he prayed to God that he might become a little baby, how he had
been adopted by the bogyman's wife, and how he had travelled about in the bogyman's belly.
They all roared with laughter.
20. THE FAMINE
Manioc (or Cassava) was brought to tropical Africa by the Portuguese from South America in
the sixteenth century. It has become the staple diet of many peoples of equatorial Africa,
from the east coast to the west coast. It has saved millions from starvation and enabled many
regions of Africa to become densely populated.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
106 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 108 ---
The day arrived when it pleased Yemekonji to punish the people
[Page [123] ]
for their sins. They had feasted too much and prayed too little. Blight struck the plants, the
sugar-canes mouldered, the bananas rotted, the fish died in the ponds, no game could be
found in the forest. The people lamented:
'Oh father Lianja, successor of Ilelangonda of the lianas, we wish to go and find a better place
to live.'
Lianja answered: 'You may go; I stay. I do not want to relinquish the shrines of my ancestors.'
Bongenge stayed with Lianja. Lofonde departed with the people, saying: 'No gain without
journey.' When they had gone, the famine in Lianja's village grew worse every day.
Lianja and Nsongo wept bitterly and prayed fervently to God, and He relented. He,
Yemekonji, sent them the answer to their prayers from heaven. It was a new plant, 
toko , the
roots of which can be eaten as cassava, or manioc. God revealed the name of the new food to
Bongenge, who announced that the period of punishment was over. Yemekonji had taken pity
on the people and given them a new crop from above. From that time cassava has been their
staple food.
Lianja decided to recall the people who had emigrated. As they had now suffered enough,
there was no more reason for them to search for better land, for there was plenty at home. He
started out on his search, and in the middle of the forest he found a forked stick standing in
the ground with long white hair hanging over it. A little farther on, he found another forked
stick supporting the same long white hairs which, he decided, was a beard. Lianja followed
the beard, which was supported by a long trail of forked sticks, along the path, through the
forest, until he came out into an open village square, where the beard disappeared into a hut.
Lianja entered the hut and met its owner, old Banjolo the sage.
Lianja's people had arrived there on their search for a new homeland, and Banjolo had told
them: 'I will receive you as my people but I do not wish to live with idle men, so go and
cultivate
[Page [124] ]
my fields.' They went out with their hoes, and worked obediently. When they had produced
their crops, the sly Banjolo sent his own men to collect the harvest.
When Lianja arrived and claimed his people back, old Banjolo refused to let them go unless
Lianja would pay him all the food he had given them during the period of their stay with
them, not mentioning, of course, that they had worked for their food. Lianja refused, and
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
107 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 109 ---
started calling his people together from the fields with his magic bell, which they all heard
and recognized no matter how far away they were. Banjolo, too, collected his men,
threatening to kill Lianja with all his people. A terrible battle ensued in which Lianja burned
all the houses, singing:
I lit the beard, the beard is burning,
I set the beard alight, the beard is flaming.
Banjolo and his men all perished. They had underestimated the power of Lianja, who did not
lose a single man. They returned home all together, singing happily.
The people in Likinda's village heard the bell ringing from afar, so they beat the drum and
started a dance.
21. THE ORIGIN OF JEALOUSY
A wise husband leaves the choice of a second wife to his first wife. She will usually select a
close relative, a younger sister or cousin, so that the two women may live in harmony, sharing
one husband. The senior wife then has the feeling that it was really her idea that her husband
should marry again.
The woodpecker is an important member of the bird family. He is a magician and a breaker of
rocks.
Ilelangonda-Itonde reappears to his great-granddaughter as the spirit of death, but also as a
saviour. The tortoise was the animal that caught him in its nets. It is the most intelligent of all
animals.
[Page [125] ]
Likinda's sister Botoma was married to a man named Bolonga. After some years, when it
became appropriate for Bolonga to take a second wife, Botoma decided that the best
candidate was Bolumbu, her niece, a daughter of Likinda. When Bolumbu entered Bolonga's
household, it soon became clear that he loved her much more than his first wife, her aunt.
She was an obedient wife and a good cook, so that her husband spent much more time in the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
108 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 110 ---
hut of his second wife than with her aunt Botoma.
This was the origin of envy: Botoma began to hate her young niece, and to hatch a plan to get
rid of her. The opportunity presented itself one day when they went out fishing together.
Bolonga had dammed a stream and told his wives to collect all the fish in the dry bed. There
were dozens of fish squirming in the mud. As soon as Aunt Botoma had her basket full, she
told her niece and co-wife to carry on picking up the fish; she would go home to do the
cooking, she said. Bolumbu stayed on working, stooping over the river bed under an intricate
maze of tree roots. Aunt Botoma climbed out on to the bank, went to the dam, which was
built of wooden poles, took one of the poles and used it as a lever to break the dam. The
water rushed into the empty bed and Bolumbu was washed away.
When Botoma came home her husband asked her where Bolumbu was, but she only said:
'Oh, she went by another path.' When it was found that the river dam was broken, and
Bolumbu did not come home, it was assumed that she was drowned. But she was not. God
had decided that she should not yet die and had given her strength to survive the ordeal. The
current carried her into a hollow rock where she could stand up. She could not get out, but
she could breathe and so she lived.
It was at that place that the people of Likinda's village used to fetch their water; it was a
waterfall in a rocky part of the land. One day, Lianja and Nsongo sent some children with
earthenware jars to fetch water. As soon as the children arrived near the water they heard a
voice singing:
[Page [126] ]
Kelenge, kelenge ,
Did you bring my father?
Did you bring my mother?
Do not bring the fool who broke the dam!
Terrified, the children dropped the jars so that they broke, ran home and stammered out a
story about the frightening thing they had heard. The adults decided to go and see for
themselves. When they came near the river they heard the voice singing inside the rock.
They recognized it as Bolumbu's voice and concluded that her spirit lingered there in the
rock. They went and consulted Bongenge, who was even more cautious than usual:
Do not say it was Bongenge, 
ngelinge ,
When I speak my magic words, ngelinge ,
I shall have to mention names, ngelinge ,

You have called me for divining, ngelinge ,
For you want to know who murdered, ngelinge ,
Do not say it was Bongenge, ngelinge ,
Blame the leopard, not the monkey , ngelinge ,
Not the squirrel but the lion, ngelinge ,
Do not kill me if I mention, ngelinge ,
Names of men you want to honour, ngelinge ,
Or of women leopardesses, ngelinge .
Lianja promised him and swore that he would go unhurt. Bongenge then revealed the

God decided her survival, ngelinge ,
And concealed her in a boulder, ngelinge ,
There she lives like normal mortals, ngelinge ,
Not a spirit but a woman, ngelinge ,
Singing all the time kelenge, ngelinge ,
With her own voice of Bolumbu, ngelinge ,
She is fed by the Creator, ngelinge ,
Kept by Yemekonji's power, ngelinge ,
She may now be liberated, ngelinge ,

If we break the boulder open, ngelinge ,
Let us take the song-birds' counsel, ngelinge .
With characteristic resolution, Lianja took his iron tools and began to hack away at the stone,

from a tree:
Father Lianja, tsen tsen tsen tsen ,
Listen to the song-bird's counsel, tsen tsen tsen ,
Call the birds tsen tsen tselenge ,
Don't forget the woodpecker tsen, tselenge .
The men ignored the honey-bird, so it flew away.

--- PAGE 112 ---
At last, when they were tired, they said to each other: 'Who said that about the birds? Wasn't
it the honeybird? Call him back!'
Lianja asked the honey-bird to go and call all the birds. When they were all assembled,
Lianja asked the honey-bird to break the rock, but it said: 'I break hives, not stones.' The
crested starling tried, but broke its bill. So did all the other birds, one after the other. Finally,
the eagle said haughtily that he of course should have been approached first about this
problem. He was invited to test his strength; he hacked away at the rock, but he too broke his
beautiful bill. At last they noticed that the woodpecker was not present at the roll-call. Lianja
sent the honey-bird to look for him, and when at last he arrived, he sang this song:
Tsa tsa tsa , I am the woodpecker,
I am the father of magicians.
He pecked away at the stone so that an opening was formed, through which a face became
visible. Lianja praised and encouraged him:
Bravo, Woodpecker,
Father of magicians,
Open the stone!
[Page [128] ]
The woodpecker, Yondoko, tapped on and on until the opening was wide enough for
Bolumbu to be pulled through. Lianja gave the woodpecker four baskets full of maize, and
ten to all the other birds for having willingly tried and broken their bills.
Bolumbu was carried to her father's house, alive but unconscious. Lianja sent for Bongenge.
The great diviner arrived and announced that he could only succeed this time if Bolumbu's
Aunt Botoma was present. But no one could find her. At last her husband saw her in the
fields and brought her to the meeting- place. There she was accused not only of attempted
murder but also of witchcraft, because it was evident to an expert like Bongenge that a curse
still weighed on the young woman. So he told her to remove the spell. She replied by
admitting the attempted murder but denying the allegations of witchcraft.
Bongenge answered enigmatically: 'He who knows everything, does not know the tortoise
yet. Take the plant of life, scrape it and put it on the young woman's head.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
111 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 113 ---
Botoma, on his instructions, found the stalks of the bosaako and the bosaanga plants,
chewed them, and so made the Water of Life. But it did not work, no matter how often
Likinda called his daughter back to life. At last Lianja fetched his father's magic snuff and
blew it into her nose. Bolumbu sneezed and woke up. Then she told her story, which she
ended thus:
'The water found me on the bottom under the net of roots, and I knew I could not escape. I
thought: shall I be going to grandfather Ilelangonda without reason? Suddenly I felt as if
someone was with me, and I arrived in a place where the water stopped pushing me. At first I
could not speak, but suddenly grandfather Ilelangonda ordered me to speak to the people so
that they might hear me and know where I was. Will you now ask Aunt Botoma why I had to
die?'
Bolonga went home with his first wife, and they kept Bolumbu with them for a while.
Bolonga invited the elders of his father's village and told them the whole story. Their verdict
was: 'If a rat
[Page [129] ]
has eaten the seam, it will soon eat the whole garment,' which meant in this case that it was
too dangerous to keep a proven witch in the house, and that it would be safer to take her back
to her powerful brothers. This he did, and in this way their marriage came to an end. Botoma
stayed with her brothers, deeply ashamed. Bolumbu said she loved her husband Bolonga very
deeply , but she feared her aunt. So she stayed with Bolonga. They lived together in peace.
22. THE LAST W ARS
I. The War of the Bogymen
The tale of the end of the epic Lonkundo dynasty is told in six episodes. At first, the great
warriors are successful: Lianja, Likinda and their comrades-in-arms exterminate their
hereditary enemies the Biloko or Bogymen.
What, then, went wrong? Where lay the seed of ruin? Bongenge the seer is ageing. He eats a
fruit that is taboo for diviners. His young ambitious son dreams that he is the sole survivor of
the clan. Like Joseph's brothers, they perceive that this young dreamer is secretly proud. Like
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
112 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 114 ---
Joseph, he is right. Unlike Joseph, he is dishonest. He steals the most treasured family
possession: the magic pouch. It had once belonged to Kungoelo, who gave it to his daughter,
Eyonga, who gave it to her son, Itonde, who gave it to his son, Lianja I. With the Pouch of
Life goes the good fortune of the family. Bongenge loses his power of prophecy, and dies.
The Ngombe people live to the north-west of the Nkundo, along the banks of the Congo.
They arrived in the region when the Nkundo were already settled and became their
arch-enemies. Young Bongenge walks over to them and becomes the king's son-in-law; but
blood is thicker than water---he walks back across the battle lines with the Snuff of Life.
One day Nsongo said she wanted to eat meat, and at once the brothers Likinda and Lianja
went out to hunt. On a path in the
[Page [130] ]
forest they heard voices and hid in the bushes. A long line of Biloko bogymen, went by. They
were saying to each other: 'Did you smell that human meat?' Lianja seized the hindmost
Biloko and killed him. The others fled into a hollow tree.
Lianja made a strong snare around the opening in the tree while Likinda put fire to the other
side. The first Biloko who tried to get out was caught in the snare and could not move
backwards or forwards. So they were all burnt alive. The two brothers picked up all the sau
-fruits which the Biloko had dropped in their flight, and went home. Bongenge said that there
must be many more of those elusive Biloko men, and that they should go out together and
kill the lot. 'But not now, for my wife Itutsi is about to give birth, and at such a time hunting
should be forbidden.'
When the child was born, the great diviner called him Bongenge, after himself. He, too, was
a prodigy, for he grew up in a few days and became a strong youth, able to follow his father in
the forest on a hunting expedition. They went out all together and young Bongenge sang:
If you hear 
fululu , it is the wood-pigeon.
Y eeloya , the wind is blowing.
Murmur, murmur, says the brook.
Nsongo wanted a pigeon, so they snared one for her. Then she wanted the wind, which no
one could grasp. Then she wanted to have the murmurs of the brook. Young Bongenge gave
her water to drink, and she said: 'The water of murmuring is the best there is.'
They arrived at the frontier of bogyman country. It was surrounded by water and there was no
bridge. They camped on an ant-hill and started forging. Linaja forged the knife-that-kills-
chiefs.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
113 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 115 ---
The bogeymen arrived, attracted by curiosity on hearing the ke! ke! of the hammer. They
threw the spear-that-kills-chiefs at Bongenge but missed. He threw the knife-that-kills-chiefs
at them and killed two. Likinda did the same, until all the Biloko
[Page [131] ]
who attacked them were dead. Then Lianja rang his magic bell, which dried the lake that
surrounded the Island of the Bogymen. They walked to the village of the bogymen and killed
all the bogywomen and bogychildren. Finally, they sent a man called Corpse as messenger to
Likinda's village to announce their victory.
They travelled through the forest and found rubber-fruits. Old Bongenge forgot they were
taboo for diviners and ate some.
His son said: 'The diviner does not see the omens of his own misfortune.'
The old man said: 'You, child, are you trying to do your father's work?'
II. Y oung Bongenge is Banished
That night, young Bongenge dreamt that a war arose in which all the men were killed, and
that he alone stayed alive. The next morning he told this dream to the village council and
they were all annoyed. They wanted to kill him, but his mother's brother said: 'Do not kill
him, just banish him.' This was accepted unanimously. Lianja sent young Bongenge to the
Ngombe people, but he did not know that Bongenge had stolen the magic pouch kangili .
Bombuna, paramount chief of the Ngombe, had a dearly-loved wife who was one day bitten
by a viper so that she died. They called young Bongenge, who was already known as diviner.
He asked: 'Do you want me to bring her back to life?'
Bombuna said: 'If you succeed, I will make you chief.'
Bongenge took the magic pouch, pronounced all the ritual formulae:
Kangili, kangili , magic pouch,
Spirit of the shouting Mbeo !
He took some powder, put it in the dead woman's nose and--- hachoo! She woke up, talked,
and was restored to King Bombuna.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
114 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 116 ---
[Page [132] ]
The king gave Bongenge his own daughter in marriage and made him chief minister.
III. Old Bongenge dies
One day, one of Likinda's children caught an eye-disease and died. Lianja went to his secret
hut to find his magic pouch, but no matter where he searched, he could not find it. Old
Bongenge was called, and sang:
Do not say it is Bongenge! ngelinge .
The magic bag has somehow vanished.
It vanished with the dying spirits.
When beauty dies, paleness is only a corpse.
Darkness descends, the eyes are failing.
The child is panting for the bird of death.
The rats sleep on the floor, I want to weep.
My magic disappeared. The Ngombe have it.
It was the first time in his long career that Bongenge had failed to find the answer to a
problem. When the young leaf grows, the old leaf dies. Old Bongenge felt ill and lay down
for the last time. He called his old friends and comrades-in-arms, Lianja and Likinda, to his
bedside and said:
'I, the diviner of war and peace, am dying. You must find my son and keep him with you. He
is a greater diviner than I ever was. I have lost my prophetic voice. After my death things will
change rapidly. Do not cut a tree if you want a mushroom.'
And then he died.
IV. 
The Great War
They all called: 'Bongenge is departing, fare thee well! Departing as a victor. Go in peace!'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
115 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 117 ---
They sent a messenger to the land of the Ngombe to inform young Bongenge that his father
had died. They performed the funeral rites, after which all the men were called up. Together
they travelled to Ngombeland. Nothing can be gained without a
[Page [133] ]
journey! Lianja told King Bombuna upon arrival that he wanted Bongenge, to which the king
replied: 'A man is not a yam that can be hidden in a barn.' He refused to let Bongenge go.
Then Lianja pronounced the dreadful curse:
Oh the killing, oh the slaughter,
Between Nkundo and Ngombe!
Bombuna called his men and the war was on. Man killed man, and was killed in his turn.
Bongenge took the side of Lianja's men. The Ngombe wanted to kill him for his treachery,
but they never found him. In the end they were all killed, all the Ngombe and all the
Nkundo: only Lianja and Bongenge remained alive.
Bongenge applied the magic powder to the noses of Lianja's men so that they all woke up and
went home. The people of Lianja's village spread mats for the victors. They said: 'Rest first,
tell tales tomorrow. Too many questions kill the answers.'
Bongenge went to sleep in his father's house and the old diviner visited him in a dream,
saying: 'My son! dance for all you have lost! Sing and recover what I left behind! He who
knows everything does not know the tortoise yet! Many proverbs tire even the elders. The
forest is a snake with eggs. Do not approach the owner without oil. If the eye dies the heart is
guilty. The slave can become a chief. The king's right hand can kill him.'
V.  
Likinda's Death
One night Bongenge dreamt that Likinda would be killed in a fight in the forest. When he
told Likinda his dream, the latter replied: 'You wish me death. We shall see.'
Thirty-two days later, Likinda went out to the forest to find the sau -fruit tree of the
bogymen, near the place where they had been burnt. Suddenly the bogymen reappeared,
surrounded Likinda and killed him. There he lay, the great hero, motionless, on the path of
the pigs.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
116 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 118 ---
[Page [134] ]
When he did not return, Lianja called the diviners. Lofonde, the son of old Lofonde, sang:
'We will all return to our mother.'
Bongenge divined: 'Likinda is lying on the same path where you burnt the bogymen.'
Oh Likinda, the great hunter of the striped antelopes,
Oh Likinda, the judge of his people should never sleep,
Oh Likinda, sleeping like a child where he fell on the floor.
Nsongo sang: 'I am not crying, it is the smoke that calls my tears.'
Bongenge went on:
Oh Lianja of the hanging lianas, standing upright,
You know the path of the 
sau -fruit tree,
Where you grilled the people of the clubs,
The terrors of the night-time,
The people of the feathery birds,
Hooting to frighten children at night,
They who possess the 
sau tree in the forest.
Oh Lianja, rock-breaker, liana standing straight.
Lianja called his men and they departed at once. They arrived at the sau tree where Likinda
died, but they did not find his body. The people of Sausau arrived as soon as they heard
someone picking fruits. Everyone was killed except Bongenge and Lianja. They used their
magic powder on their own men, who rose up, alive and healthy.
23. THE END
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
117 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 119 ---
The tragic end of the famous family comes suddenly, as an epidemic. Only Death survives.
After this they lived in peace for many years. One night, Bongenge dreamt that Lianja,
Nsongo and he himself died. He saw and heard
[Page [135] ]
Ilelangonda, who told him: 'People die like a mockery. The world is decaying because people
do as they like. In my time, I lived in God's world, I did His bidding. Now the great tree is
falling, for the ants are eating it from within.'
That morning, when Bongenge told Lianja his dream, the great chief was so infuriated that he
threatened to kill his diviner. Bongenge fled away---but it does not help to chase away the
mosquito after it has stung you.
Twenty-seven days later a storm arose and a sudden gale blew down a tree, which crashed
down on Nsongo. Who can avert Yemekonji's decision? There she lay, the twin sister of great
Lianja the smith. He applied his magic powder but it did not work. It only worked when God
willed it. Robust Nsongo, always full of song, Lianja's life-companion, was dead.
Lianja went in search of Bongenge. He found the people dying in all the villages. He arrived
in Ngombeland, but there, too, the people lay dying in their huts. Lianja came home, lay
down on his couch and said: 'The world is decaying.' He felt ill. At last Bongenge arrived.
Lianja demanded that he should drink poison to divine judgement, for he suspected that
Bongenge was guilty of killing Likinda and Nsongo.
Bongenge drank but he did not die. He sang:
It is not me,
It is the Master.
Later that day, Lianja called his people and told them to bury all his weapons with him.
Boyoyo was appointed his successor. The same day Lianja died.
That night, Bongenge had a dream in which God spoke to him: 'For the anger of Lianja and
Likinda will I make the world die. You too will die, for you have contradicted me. All things
will be as they were before.'
After that, all the people died, even Bongenge and Boyoyo. All the villages were empty. Only
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
118 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 120 ---
God remained, and Ilelangonda.
[Page [137] ]
4 The Woyo
[Page [138] ]
The mythical history of the Woyo takes us back to the lower reaches of the Congo river,
where the turbulent movements of the water betray the presence of powerful spirits who rule
fertility.
THE W ATER SPIRITS
These serpents, or water-snakes, assume every colour of the rainbow, and so it is believed
that they are identical with the rainbow, the rain-giver.
Bunzi is worshipped by the people in nocturnal dances, in which the dancers are naked.
Four spirits resided in the water beneath the rapids in the Congo River, in the form of four
serpents, Kuitikuiti the Waving one, his wife Mboze the Fertile one, and their children
Makanga and Mbatilanda. They lived in the Infernal Cauldron, as the white men call it, the
maelstrom where the powerful current of the Congo meets the rising tide at every noon. The
people say that Kuitikuiti has been seen in many other parts of the river as well.
Long ago there was only the earth with the bushes on it. Then Kuitikuiti rose out of the water
and created all the tail-less animals, and Mbatilanda created all the animals with tails. When
they came home they found that Mboze was pregnant. She had committed incest with her
son, Makanga. Furious, Kuitikuiti seized a club and beat her to death. Dying, she gave birth
to a serpent daughter, called Bunzi. Bunzi is the goddess of rain and
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
119 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 121 ---
[Page [139] ]
fertility. She gave birth to another water spirit called Lusunzi, who comes to visit his mother
regularly , and whenever he does, there is kalema , springtide, in the vast estuary of the
Congo.
Kuitikuiti resuscitated his wife Mboze, but now her skin was white instead of black, so he
also exchanged his own black skin for a white one. Kuitikuiti also lay with his granddaughter,
Bunzi, and the issue of this union was a daughter, Kambizi the Storm, who floods the low
lands of the delta and drags the sailors and bathers down so that they drown. On the bottom of
the sea she makes love with them, like the princesses of the old days who had the right to
pick any man they fancied to satisfy their desires.
[Page [141] ]
5 The Makere
[Page [142] ]
The Makere live near the Bima river north of the Congo. The first narrative is an account of
oral traditions about the constant southward migrations of the Makere and the deadly pursuit
of the Azande conquerors.
1. THE MIGRATIONS OF THE MAKERE
The Makere are a branch of the Mangbetu group of peoples in the north-east Congo. Among
these peoples, the mothers wind strips of cloth around the tender heads of their newly born
daughters, so stiffly that the skull is deformed and becomes elongated in the shape that has
become typical for women in that part of Africa.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
120 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 122 ---
Those among the Makere who were old men in the thirties could remember vividly how they
defended their people against the Arabs ( Tuku-Tuku ) who arrived from Kisangani and
Banalia. The Arabs had built a boma (fortress) on the left bank of the Rubi, from where they
penetrated farther, crossing the Bima, and built another fortress near Zobia. They wanted
ivory, and the easiest way to get it was simply to raid the villages, take women and children
prisoner and transport them to their fortress, where they were held as hostages. The men of
such a village knew what they had to do: they went into the bush and hunted elephants until
they could offer enough ivory to the Arabs to induce them to release their families.
The Makere elders narrate that their ancestor, Duli, who lived
[Page [143] ]
about ten generations ago, crossed the Bomokandi river from the north and settled with his
people on the banks of the Nemboma. His descendants migrated further in a south-westerly
direction, and settled between the Bima and Makongo rivers. Two clans penetrated as far as
the Rubi river: they are the Mangiri and the Baetoy.
The Makere people faced national disaster with the invasion of the Azande, a numerous and
powerful people who came from the north. They moved south along the left bank of the
Makongo river as far as the Bima. There they conquered three of the Makere clans, the
Babuy, the Babruenge and the Bapele, who are still under Azande rule. This is the story of
how it happened.
Many years before the white men arrived, long before even the Tuku-Tuku arrived in the
region, the Azande arrived from the sunless lands. They are mighty warriors, skilled at
warfare with lances; in addition, they had spinning knives with four to five cutting edges,
which they could throw with incredible dexterity. The Makere defended themselves with
bitter tenacity but they had to withdraw from village after village and watch their wives and
daughters being carried away as slaves.
Finally, after many deliberations, and after the diviners had consulted the benevolent spirits,
it was decided they would migrate to the south-west. They would search for a fertile region in
the dense forest, where they would be left in peace to build their new villages. When this was
agreed they all went home, packed their meagre belongings, what little they could take in a
hurry, and drove their goats out of their pens. With hearts full of sadness they left their once
peaceful and prosperous villages with their ancestral shrines. Even the sacred graves of the
ancestors would now be desecrated by the invaders.
Alas, their migration was blocked by a big barrier: the wide Makongo river. If only they could
reach the other bank in time, they would be safe from the raids of the Azande. They decided
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
121 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 123 ---
to build a bridge, and all the men and women were called upon to
[Page [144] ]
work on this primitive device. Mats were plaited first, and these were sewn together so that a
long carpet was formed which ultimately reached the other shore.
Carefully balancing on this bridge the Makere people crossed the broad Makongo, in correct
hierarchical order: first the paramount chief with his wives, children, aunts and uncles,
cousins, grandchildren, nephews and nieces and their kin. Then the elders, the notables and
sub-chiefs. Then the Mambuli family, that is, the clan whose totem was the leopard, to which
the other families paid homage. After them came the second family, the Balasa, with their
retainers.
This first, orderly crossing took several days. In the meantime, ever more refugees arrived:
women bent under baskets of food and the few possessions they had managed to save; others
with children on the back and on the hip; men carrying poles from which hung large jars of
masanga (banana-beer), young children, some dragging heavy hunting nets, others rolling
dancing drums. The first families had already penetrated deeply into the new land, and all the
time new caravans arrived at the bridge to safety. Finally, only the Babuy, the Babruenge and
the Bapele had not arrived at the shore of liberty.
In the meantime, the waves of Azande invasions surged onwards. Village after empty village
of the Makere was occupied, and the meagre belongings which the owners had not been able
to carry away were stolen. As there was no more armed resistance, the Azande marched on
rapidly and soon overtook the refugees. One day at nightfall they were near the river.
The last village of the Makere on the 'old' side of the river Makongo was that of the Bapele.
That last and fateful night there was deadly silence. No more were heard the cheerful drums
of yore, no voices chattered or laughed, no singing was heard, nor the rapid rhythm of dancing
feet. Makereland was dead. Only the 
gudugudu  , the drum that was reserved for announcing
disasters, floods, wars or famines, could be heard above the silent forest. It
[Page [145] ]
told of the imminent approach of the Azande, whose vanguard was expected any moment. It
begged the people near the shore and those on the bridge, would they please hurry so that the
Bapele could reach the Bank of Peace in time?
They never made it. Those for whom the message was intended were camping for the night
between the Bapele village and the river. Hearing the disaster-drum's alarming message, they
panicked. They left their camp and, armed with flaming torches, squeezed their way through
the thick undergrowth of the hostile forest. On the shore there was already a great throng of
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
122 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 124 ---
people jostling one another to reach the bridge. Since many had already crossed it, the bridge
hung low in the water, waves washing over it. Women shrieked in fear and children cried
hysterically as the bridge sank deeper and deeper. At that, everybody panicked, and they all
crowded on to the bridge, terrified of the Azande.
Inevitably , the lianas that held the mats together snapped. A loud wail arose. In the glow of
the torches men, women and children could be seen struggling in the strong current. None of
the Makere could swim. They were not familiar with the water- spirit. So, the water-spirit
swallowed all the bodies, it took all the souls. Not one escaped his deadly embrace.
Those on the banks had to look on helplessly as their friends and relatives disappeared one
after another. The river flowed for ever between life and freedom on the one side, death and
slavery on the other.
The next morning, at daybreak, the first Azande warriors arrived on the north bank. All the
survivors were taken prisoner, and left the place as slaves. And so, because the water-spirit
was so greedy, the Bapele, the Babuy and the Babruenge are now the captive servants of the
Azande. They have no land, no houses, they own nothing. They are owned.
[Page [146] ]
2. THE CHIMPANZEE-ANCESTOR
The neighbours of the Makere are the Babwa, a very different race. They speak a different
language, and although the two peoples are separated from each other only by a narrow strip
of woodland, or a hill, or just a small stream, they cannot understand one another. The Babwa
are forceful characters, great hunters and proud warriors. When their turn came to be attacked
by the Azande, they took a bloody revenge for the disastrous defeat of the Makere. In one
raid, they penetrated deep into Azande territory and even destroyed Zobia. They were very
fond of Azande meat and this was an additional incentive to kill as many Azande as they
could, which they did in a long series of battles. As a result, the Azande gave up every
attempt to penetrate farther. When the white men arrived and defeated even the Arabs, the
Azande told them with awe in their voices: 'But beware of the Babwa, they are terrible
fighters.'
The Mambese are a sub-clan of the Makere who also speak Babwa: they occupy the only
bilingual village along the border. They are mainly cultivators and fishers: game is a treat for
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
123 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 125 ---
them.
When the Belgians set up their administrative offices in the region, they looked for clerks
who could speak more than one language. So all the Mambese boys found employment in the
government offices and earned much money. It was evident that a benign spirit was still
guarding them. Who else but the kind Kumbusu, the chimpanzee-spirit! To this day it is
strictly forbidden for the Mambese people to hunt or eat chimpanzees.
The Mambese explain their bilingualism in the following story.
Many years ago an elephant-hunter of the Babwa lost his way in the forest. He walked for
days but found no way out. At last, in despair, he decided to climb a tree, hoping that he
would see a fire or the curling smoke of a village. He made himself a sling from lianas, of the
type used by tree-climbers. Slowly , and with a terrific effort, he managed to climb up till he
could just reach the
[Page [147] ]
lowest branch. As soon as he had grasped it, his sling slipped, and there he hung, swinging in
the evening wind. He could not pull himself up, as he was too exhausted from many days
without proper food. He uttered a cry for help, hoping that someone would rescue him before
his hand became too weak to hold the branch and he fell to his death.
No 
man heard him, but an ape did, a chimpanzee, who came at once to rescue him. He
climbed the tree, took the man by his wrist, pulled him up, and brought him gently down to
the ground. Carefully he laid him to rest at the foot of the tree, and put leaves under his head
as a pillow. Then he went and picked some sweet fruits which he squeezed into the sick
man's mouth. When the hunter had recovered a little, the chimpanzee took him in his strong
arms and carried him to the nearest village, that of the Mambese.
Their chief had no son. He ordered his only daughter to care for the hunter until he was
cured. Gratefully, he started to hunt elephants for the Mambese, and each time he killed an
elephant, he would bring the tail to the chief, as a token of homage. A whole elephant meant
weeks of food for the people, so each elephant was an occasion for a feast. The chief of the
Mambese village, realizing that the hunter who had been brought to him by the mysterious
monkey was a gift from heaven for the villagers, gave him his only daughter in marriage. The
hunter settled for good in that village, where fate had sent him.
The kind chimpanzee was never again seen in the village, although it is believed that he must
still be watching over its prosperity. No doubt he is the incarnate spirit of an ancestor, for
which creature, human or animal, would ever show so much care and solicitude for a
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
124 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 126 ---
stranger? Surely, the chimpanzee knew that the hunter was one of his own grandsons! May
the spirits of the ancestors watch peacefully over the welfare of their children!
The hunter and the chief's daughter lived together for many years in peace and prosperity.
They had many children, so that
[Page [148] ]
they came to be regarded as the father and mother of the village. The hunter taught all his
children the language of the Babwa. Their mother taught them the Makere language. This
explains why the Mambese people today speak two languages.
3. THE SLAVE-GIRLS OF THE SPIRITS
A young man called Madiango asked his father to buy him a wife, but the father said: 'Are
you out of your mind? Did you ever buy a wife for me? Go and find your own wives!' So the
son left his father's home in pursuit of brides. He travelled through the bush until suddenly he
arrived at a large compound. He could not know that he had hit upon the place where the
spirits kept the women they had captured from the villages of men.
As all the women were at the riverside fishing that day, young Madiango entered through the
gate unseen. He hid behind a banana tree, but he was discovered there by two slave-girls who
were sweeping the compound. They stalked up to him from behind and overpowered him,
until he promised them he would not try to escape. They took him to their hut, and there they
made love to him.
When the other women came back and found out that the slave-girls had captured a man to
make love with, they all wanted their share of him, and he had to make the round of the
whole compound, which was inhabited by hundreds of women, all prisoners of the spirits.
The spirits were male but invisible, and very wicked. They wanted the women to cook great
quantities of food to assuage their insatiable appetites, rather than to serve them as female
companions. Each of the women, the morning after she had spent the night with Madiango,
described the pleasures she had enjoyed to her best friend, who would of course claim him
for the following
[Page [149] ]
night, and so on, until he had slept with every woman, old or young, black or white.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
125 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 127 ---
Then one ominous day one of the spirits arrived and challenged him to a fight. Immediately,
it grasped him and threw him high up in the air, but Madiango landed on his feet, like a wild
cat. Then it was his turn to seize the spirit. He took it by its belt, dragged it to the river and
held it down under water, until it had flown away. He later did the same with all the other
spirits, as soon as they appeared, until the women told him that every spirit had been
drowned and carried away by the river. The women rejoiced and praised Madiango as a great
hero, as the Conqueror of Evil Spirits.
When he had rested, Madiango had the drum beaten and announced that all the men who had
been robbed of their wives by the spirits could come and fetch them back. He made each of
them pay a cow to redeem his wife, which they all did without hesitation in recognition of his
heroic battles. Madiango was left with forty wives and four hundred cows. He was a rich man
now, and a famous chief.
4. MODESTY PA YS, OR NEVER INSULT YOUR MOTHER
A mother had two sons. One day the younger asked her to tell him how he could get to his
maternal uncle's homestead. She pointed out the road to her brother's compound and he set
out on his way.
His uncle embraced him affectionately and had a chicken prepared for him, as well as a dish
of millipedes, a special delicacy. At nightfall, his uncle told him to go and sleep in the
chicken-run. He did so, and the next morning, before he returned to his mother he was shown
the house in which the hens laid their eggs. His uncle told him he could select an egg for
himself. He chose a small egg. His uncle said:
[Page [150] ]
'On your way home, you will pass a place where there are the ruins of an old settlement.
There, you must throw the egg on the ground, and see what happens.'
The young man said good-bye, and when he arrived at the ruins of the old village he threw
down the egg. At once, neat new houses rose out of the earth: out of each house there
emerged a beautiful woman, and slaves came running towards him. The slaves bowed down
before him and acclaimed him as their master. Each woman approached him with a dish full
of delicious food, calling him 'my dear husband'. So the young man became a great chief.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
126 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 128 ---
The elder brother was in the habit of insulting his mother. One day he came along to his
younger brother's village and asked him how he had got all his wealth. The latter pointed out
their uncle's compound to him and wished him good luck.
The good uncle received his nephew with fond embraces and had a chicken prepared for him,
as well as a delicious dish of millipedes. The elder brother ate the chicken but spurned the
millipedes. Next, the uncle told him to go and spend the night in the chicken-run, but his
nephew replied arrogantly that he was used to sleeping in a proper house and on a proper bed.
So his uncle complied with his wishes. The next morning his uncle told him that, before
leaving, he could pick an egg from the hen-house. The young man selected the biggest egg he
could find. His uncle told him to go out in the bush where no one could see him and break
the egg there. Alas! Inside the egg there were armed men. They grew up into giants in a few
moments, and killed him.
[Page [151] ]
6 The Ngbandi
[Page [152] ]
1. THE SNAKE GOD
The Ngbandi live in the northern Congo, along the banks of the Oubangui river. Myth and
history are closely interlinked in their oral tradition. Their mythology is one of the most
remarkable in all Africa.
The following amazing anecdotes, which were written down by Father Tanghe in the first
years of Christian missions in northern Congo, are all historical.
The scene is in Banziville, on the Ubangi river. Father Basil Tanghe built a hut for himself in
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
127 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 129 ---
the country of the Ngbandi in 1912. As he had nowhere else to sleep he made his bed in it
the first night he spent in the village. In the middle of the night he woke up because of a
noise under his bed. He rose, lit his candle and looked---straight into the gleaming eyes of a
long snake, coiled up under the bed. He went to find his pistol, but when he came back the
snake had vanished.
The following night he was aroused by screams of terror from under his bed. He rose up, lit
his candle and found that the snake was back, eating one of the kittens. The mother cat had
defended her young, the snake had bitten her, and now she lay dying. The unperturbed
Capuchin father took his pistol, fired, but missed. The snake raised its head as if to attack
him, then slid majestically out of the room. It was five yards long. Father Tanghe fired four
times, but the snake went on coiling and recoiling.
At last the cook came in with a rifle. He was called Ginga, and
[Page [153] ]
had been newly engaged for the mission station. He finally managed to hit the snake in the
head so that it died. At that moment Ginga began to weep and cry like a wailing woman.
Father Tanghe thought the man was succumbing to a nervous breakdown, a disorder which,
he reports, is common in that part of Africa. He wanted to comfort the man as he felt that
such is the task of a priest, but one of the villagers stopped him, saying: 'Leave him, he is a
snake.'
The cook stopped crying as abruptly as he had started. When the missionary asked him why
he had made all that noise, Ginga said: 'I am one of twins and so I am a snake. I have just
killed my brother. So I have to mourn for him. If I had not wept for him, he would have made
me ill. Now I have wept, I can be quiet.'
The next morning Kumba arrived, Ginga's twin sister. She lived with her husband in a village
an hour's journey away, but she had come to purify her brother of the stain of killing his
brother. They streaked each other with a red powder made of the pounded wood of the 
mbio
tree, on the arms and shoulders. The rest of the red powder was strewn on the skin of the
snake, which was lying in the sun to dry. The skin can still be seen in the monastery of the
Capuchin fathers in Antwerp.
This was the beginning of Father Tanghe's many years of research into the myth of the Snake
God: the belief of the Ngbandi people that twins are snakes and are therefore identical with
the god of the Ngbandi people. He recorded his experiences in four books, written in his
inimitable Flemish, which make fascinating reading for anyone interested in anthropology
and mythology.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
128 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 130 ---
2. THE FIRST -BORN
The toro , 'supreme god', or 'tribal spirit' of the Ngbandi is the snake, just as the neighbouring
Banziri have their hippopotamus.
[Page [154] ]
The mother of the snake is Kangalogba, the dragon-fly, probably 0because she bears a tiny
little white worm that coils like a snake. The snake is the first-born of all the animals, and as
such he has seniority even over the royal leopard. The soul of a village chief settles, after his
death, in the body of a leopard, so that no chief may eat the flesh of the leopard lest his
ancestor kill him in revenge. Yet, the snake is the first-born.
The first-born of every clan is in charge of the toro -hut or 'spirit-house'. It is his function to
pray the spirits for blessing on behalf of the clan. This priestly function is the only one of his
many tasks that he cannot delegate to a younger member. He can even sell his chieftainship,
but as founder of the clan he remains for ever the mediator between the living and the spirits
of the dead.
An exception to the rule of primogeniture is provided by the following story.
Chief Lango-Gbau had four sons. One day his eldest son was killed in a battle against the
Mbondo. Chief Lango-Gbau swore that the Mbondo would suffer for this, but he was too old
to go out to battle himself. So he called his second son Zengusi, but Zengusi said he did not
wish to go. The third son, Gaso, also refused.
Finally, the youngest son was prepared to take revenge for the death of his eldest brother. He
was already famous for his gallantry. His father promised him that he would inherit the right
of primogeniture in preference to his lazy brothers. Wando, as this youngest brother was
called, took a good spear and a 
gorombe , an oblong box made of bark, and set out in the
middle of the night, heading for the village of the Mbondo. At dawn, he came back with his
box full of heads.
Wando got his reward: he was given the right to live in his father's enclosure, and he inherited
all his dead brother's wives. It is the custom that all children born to a dead man's wives, even
a year after his death, are his.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
129 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 131 ---
[Page [155] ]
3 THE LI-MEN
The Li is an evil spirit who comes to live in the stomach of certain people, and as a result
these people have an urge to wander about and create mischief wherever they go. A li -man
may knock at any door in the middle of the night and the owner of the house will wake up
with a start, feeling as if someone is trampling on his chest, as if someone is squeezing his
throat. Alas, he must call his children together, for he has only a few days left to live. Disease
will come, and slowly the victim will die.
A li -man may secure a lock of hair or a nail-paring, or a piece torn from the clothes of the
doomed man, so that by means of it he can bring about his victim's death. Some li -men steal
children and carry them away to a place deep in the forest, where they can kill their victims
and consume them undisturbed.
The li -men come together from time to time to have a feast, with drumming and dancing.
They may be near a village where ordinary people live, but no one can see them or hear
them. Li -men love to dig up corpses from the graves and eat the dead flesh. For this reason
the custom is to place guards on the cemetery when a corpse has been freshly buried.
Whenever someone dies who has been suspected of being a li -man his own relatives will
insist on an autopsy, because they wish to rid themselves of the evil reputation. The li is
hereditary and can be passed on to one's children. Whole villages are for this reason known as
li -clans. The professional 'belly-cutter' will be called in to open the body of the deceased.
The entire intestine will be taken out and carefully examined. If the belly-cutter finds a
tumour, he knows that it contains the li -spirit. He will take it to the forest and hang it in a
tree. No one will dare to come close to it. The relatives of the proven li -man are for ever
disgraced and feared. If they should hide the li , rain would pour down and never stop. The li
may also cause the failure of hunting expeditions.
[Page [156] ]
It is said that all animals have a kundu ('stomach'), which is another word for li . Some
animals, however, have a particularly powerful li : the bat, the owl and the cat, especially the
wild cat.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
130 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 132 ---
If a wild cat is heard meowing in the village during the night, the next morning all the people
will be told to come out of their houses, and those who cannot eat are the li -men. The bones
of the rock 'rabbit', secretly placed near the well, are an infallible device to catch the li -men:
those who smell it have a li .
If you find bats hanging on a tree, you can bring them down by starting a fire. The smoke will
stupefy them so that they fall on the ground. Take them to a place deep in the forest and eat
them there, for if you take them to your house in the village, the li -men will smell them and
take revenge.
A li -man may carry his murderous habits too far so that one day, if there have been several
sudden deaths, the whole clan will kill him. If the li -man's identity has to be detected, sacred
ashes will be strewn on the path, or a smith's hammer will be placed there, and the li will
make itself known as a light in the distance. When the li -man comes near, a small flame will
be seen coming out of his mouth.
Diseases caused by a li -man can be cured by a kokoro -man. In Banziville a kokoro -man
cured a patient who suffered from a pain in the eye by pulling a long fishbone from the ailing
eyeball. A woman who suffered from a sore in the side was cured when the kokoro -man
pulled out the bone of a young deer which a li -man had hidden in her body.
4. THE BENDO-SPIRITS
The Bendo are a special species of spirits which tell their favourites the facts about the past,
the present and the future. Those who claim to have control over a bendo -spirit are called
diviners. They do considerable harm, because when they are called in to explain the
[Page [157] ]
cause of somebody's illness they may point at anyone and accuse him of having caused the
disease.
In many cases the spirit himself is in control and falls upon his victim unexpectedly, in the
middle of the forest. The possessed person begins to talk about unknown things such as
hidden crimes; the best remedy is to consult a fellow victim about how to appease the spirit.
A person who has a 
bendo must worship his spirit with offerings of eggs and palm-oil. He
will plant two young trees near his door and hang a pombo plant above it, to show that he is a
diviner, and wait for business.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
131 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 133 ---
One morning a customer came along to a house with the pombo sign. He told his wife, who
was walking respectfully behind him, to put down his stool, which she was carrying. She did
so and he sat down on it. Soon the diviner emerged, a horn full of water in his hand. The
customer, who had been summoned by the magistrate, asked if the sentence would be severe.
Reassured on this point, he proceeded on his journey, after paying a small fee.
A man in the village of Mbondo suffered from repeated attacks of over-aggressiveness. He
himself knew he had a bendo -spirit, which he worshipped regularly. From time to time the
spirit induced him to fight, either in his own village or in other places. In the end he was
always beaten by the men he challenged, and the bendo-doctor was called. This doctor knew
the disease of this patient, and that scrapings from the bark of certain trees would cure the
attack.
He administered these powders in the form of sacred ashes; meanwhile, the drum was
beaten. The doctor threw pebbles and pips at the sick man. Finally the patient became
exhausted and was laid to rest in his own bed. The doctor called him, 'to wake him up'. He
had to pay the doctor with his last possessions.
A rich man had been ill for a long time. Finally he was placed, in his bed, near the spirit-hut,
and the bendo -man was called. He
[Page [158] ]
came and performed his magic dances, all decked out in plumes and beating the drum
ferociously, for this was an important customer. Finally, he gazed into his water-horn and saw
a face. He called a name, collected his paraphernalia and his fee, and went home. The
relatives of the ailing man were now left with the problem of dealing with the wicked
bewitcher. When they had found him, he was beaten to the ground and tied with a rope. He
must give up the thing he had captured from his enemy. In this case it was the rich man's
excrement, which the sorcerer had secured and bewitched. He handed it over, it was thrown
in the fire, and the sick man was cured.
A village headman who had nine wives was very ill. The 
bendo -man was called, who was
known to be a scourge of li -spirits. He came and performed his dances, rubbed all over with
red powder and dressed in a grass skirt. Finally, he indicated three of the man's wives as li
-women. The three women had to drink a poison which they would survive if they were
innocent. But all three of them died.
Many other diseases are in reality caused by animals or natural phenomena.
Wounds are probably spirits, eating away the patient's flesh. If someone is wounded in battle
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
132 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 134 ---
or during the hunt, no one will come near him.
The heartbeat ( ndoko ) is a water-bird (a type of loon) which constantly bobs in and out of
the water. The skin of a dwarf- antelope is used to cure heart diseases, especially in children.
5. LIGHTNING AND W ATER SPIRITS
Sciatica is caused by lightning. Therefore anyone who has survived being struck by lightning
is called in to cure the disease, because he is a friend of the lightning.
Lightning is pictured as a big brown cock which swoops down
[Page [159] ]
and touches a person's head with its bill. It makes a hole there, picks up the soul or 'life
spirit', and flies away with it.
Others say that lightning is a small animal that hits you unexpectedly, then runs away and
hides in the water of a well. together with the rainbow ( kongo ).
The great water spirit is called Mbomba or Sa-ngu ('beast in the water'). He is a colossal
monster and the king of all the fish, frogs and water reptiles. He lives in the large Ubangi
river, usually near the rapids. All boatmen who pass such places will throw food in the water
and ask the protection of the Mbomba.
A man who caught a big fish heard the water bubbling and gurgling where he had just been
fishing. He realized that the water spirit was getting angry, and threw the fish back into the
river to appease it; otherwise it would have dried up---because if Sangu is annoyed it will
stop the river and the land will be thirsty. If the Sangu catches you, cry out the name of your
dead grandfather. His spirit will protect you.
A boy was playing in his uncle's village when he suddenly heard someone calling his name.
He looked up but saw no one. He heard his name again and followed the voice to where it
came from. He was later found, drowned in the river, with one earlobe bitten off. Evidently
he had been caught by Nda-Were, the spirit that rules the mouth of the Uele river.
A girl from the same village was caught in 1913 by the Sangu spirit. She disappeared in the
Ubangi river, and the whole family mourned for her. Invitations were sent to many villages in
the surrounding districts to attend the funeral meal, when suddenly word came back that she
had been found, washed ashore three days' boat-journey downstream, in the country of the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
133 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 135 ---
Banziri. These people took her to their village and tried to wake her up by rubbing her with
sacred ashes, but she stayed unconscious. Finally they decided to beat the drums near her, and
at last she came to.
She told a miraculous story! The Mbomba had seized her by
[Page [160] ]
the hand and taken her deep down into the lap of the river. There she had walked over the
mountains, and she had seen the meal of the Sangu, which was nothing but fishbones. 2
The water spirit can make a person mad, but it can also show him the future. Every pool and
stream has a spirit of its own. The spirit of the Bogo river is called Bogo, and his help is
implored for abundant crops. The Limalo is a narrow stream which nevertheless does not
permit people to jump across it. The Limalo-spirit is prayed to when the people go out
fishing and wish for a good catch. A dwarf-antelope may be offered to it, or the liver of a big
animal, when the hunt has been successful.
The Bogo-spirit once killed a group of girls who were dancing in the shallows, by dropping a
broad flagstone on them from a rock on its bank. It is therefore advisable to pray to the river
regularly and to throw offerings into it.
There is a legend which tells that the god Ketua had seven children: Morning, Noon,
Evening, Night, Moon, Sun, and Water. In the season of the ants Ketua went round to visit all
his children, asking them for ants. 
3 The six eldest replied that they had none. Finally, Ketua
visited the homestead of his youngest son, Water. Water spread a mat for his father to sit on,
and told his wives to stew a good portion of ants for his father. Ketua was moved by this and
decided to make Water the chief of all his descendants, his first-born.
If anyone drowns in a river, that part of the water becomes his property and his son will
inherit it. The son will go there from time to time with an offering for his father's spirit; the
water has
[Page [161] ]
become part of his ancestor-hut or spirit-house. When the people of his village go fishing in
that part of the river, they will always take him in their boat. He does not have to work, but
his father's spirit will protect the boat.
