6. THE FOUR CHILDREN OF NZAPA
Nzapa is the Creator, the Supreme God, the Father of mankind. He has ordained the laws, the
rules of behaviour. He rules our destinies. He decides the fate of man. Whether you will die
young, or live to see your grandchildren, it is the will of Nzapa. He rules man and their
ancestors, and all the other spirits, animal and vegetable.
Seven gods are invoked every morning in a special prayer: Nzapa, Yayu (sky), Sese (earth),
Banga (white water), Mbongo (black water), Ketua (Fortune), and Lomo (peace). Mbongo is
the dark brown water along the left (southern) bank of the Ubangi river. Banga is the clear
water along the right (northern) bank; it is also the name of a powerful magic powder. Banga
is also the name of the ancestor of the light-coloured peoples of the north. The god Banga
causes a type of epilepsy for which he (in the form of magic powder) is also the medicine.
Mbongo is the ancestor of the black peoples of the south, in particular the Ngbandi.
In addition to these gods and the snake god whom we have already met, the Ngbandi also
worship the moon (Nze), the father of the stars, at the beginning of every new cycle, when
the new moon appears, preferably accompanied by his wife, the planet Venus. Every month
the moon 'cuts' a woman so that she has her menses. When a girl has her first menstruation,
people say that she has seen the moon. When the moon remains dark for a woman, she is
pregnant, or as the Ngbandi say: 'Her husband has forged a fruit with her.' The word fruit (
ngo ) also means 'pregnancy' and 'drum'; a big drum is called tá ngo 'mother-drum'. The word
for 'moon' ( nze ) also means 'food basket'. The Ngbandi language is full of such allusions.
[Page [162] ]
Palm-oil ( môn ) is used for a great variety of purposes, not only for the preparation of many
dishes but also as a medicine, against scabies. Many proverbs refer to oil, such as: 'Palm-oil
strokes the stomach', i.e. it makes all food delicious. 'The palm-nut dies, the palm-oil does
not.' This is symbolic of the continuation of life itself, for it is quoted when someone has lost
his parents or is himself dying; it is a consolation: 'Do not worry, your sons will honour your
spirit!'
Nzapa had four children, all palm-trees: 
Ndimba (Raffia-palm), Ngara (Liana), Pe
(Wine-palm) and Kpmon (Oil-palm). After many days, Nzapa grew old and felt hungry. The
wives of the three eldest sons prepared food for their husbands, who ate it and took only the
left-overs to their old father Nzapa. The wife of Kpmon, too, cooked food for her husband,
but he took the whole dish to his old father.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
135 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 137 ---
One day, Nzapa called his sons together. Kpmon was late and the other three urged their
father to begin and tell them what he had to say, but he waited.
When Kpmon arrived, father Nzapa began: 'You three have given me only little bits of your
food, but Kpmon treated me as a father ought to be treated. I have therefore decided that your
fate will be as follows: You, Pe, will be cut and carved by men who want your sap. You,
Ngara, will be twisted and plaited by women, who will shape you into a basket. You,
Ndimba, will be woven and become a bed for Man. You, Kpmon, my child, will be honoured
by the people of the earth. Your oil-nuts will feed them and will be highly appreciated by
them.'
And thus it happened.
7. SOULS AND SPIRITS
The importance of water is expressed thus in the Ngbandi language: the rain is called 'Water
from Above', the river: 'Mother of Water'; ink is 'letter-water'; tears are 'face-water'; the sea is
'big water'; a
[Page [163] ]
stream is 'arm of the water'. Sap is 'tree-water'; and 'stem of the water' means 'salvation'.
'Body-water' is the word for semen. If the sky is clouded, people say: 'Water has taken away
the face of the sun.' 'Water has conquered the land,' means a flood. A proverb says: 'Death
does not thunder, it arrives imperceptibly like rain.'
A person has a soul that can be seen if you look in the mirror or in the water. This is why the
older people do not wish to be photographed: it means trapping souls, and the soul-trapper
will make you ill.
Another proof of the existence of the soul is dreaming. At night your soul may travel around
and see strange places, while your body lies visibly on your bed. Or you may receive a visit
from your husband who is far away in the army, or from your father who died years ago.
Once, in a dream, a man saw his brother, who was in the forest on a hunting expedition.
Three days later his companions returned and told the dreamer that his brother had had an
accident. That was what he had come to tell his brother in the dream.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
136 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 138 ---
Animals and plants too have their souls, some weak, some strong. Roots and fruits which kill
or cure you if you eat them have in them a fragment of the powerful spirit of the tree to which
they belong. The elements, too, have their spirits, for instance the fire, which is a voracious
spirit. The water spirit makes you love it so much that you go and bathe in it; then you
become drowsy--- the water pulls you towards her bosom. If it has bewitched you, you feel a
compulsion to go to it.
8. DEATH AND THE SOUL
The Ngbandi honour the leopard as the spirit that kills. It is the spirit of the chief, and if a
leopard is killed in the forest it is brought to the chief's compound and dedicated to the
ancestors. Here it is
[Page [164] ]
flayed; the skin may only be worn by chiefs, the meat may only be eaten by foreigners.
Cases of eating human flesh were repeatedly reported in the early years of the century.
Enemies slain in battle were cooked by the women and eaten by the men. Women cooked
soup of human heads; men made necklaces of human bones. Adulterers were killed in public
and eaten by the villagers.
If a stranger appeared in a village he might be welcomed by a villager who was particularly
interested in human meat. He would say: 'Just wait here, I will bring you some food.' The
food would soon arrive, together with a group of friends of the villager, who would say:
'Sorry, I've forgotten my knife; help me to cut this food for you.' The stranger would hand
over his knife, and that was the end of him. He would be assaulted, killed with his own knife,
cooked and eaten. His skeleton would be placed near the spirit- house of the ancestors, as an
offering. Madmen were buried alive, or alternatively bound, put in an old canoe and left to
float downstream. Slaves who died were not buried---they were either thrown into the river,
or dragged into the forest and left there to be eaten by the hyenas. A sick man without either a
mother or a wife would die miserably.
Here are some Ngbandi proverbs about Death: Death has no friends. Death makes no noise.
Death never regrets having killed. There is no appeal against Death. Death cannot be bribed.
Death is never far and never tired.
Death and the Soul were enemies.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
137 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 139 ---
Death said: 'I will kill you.'
The soul said: 'I will not be killed.'
Death collected his helpers: Lightning, Ulcers, Syphilis, Smallpox, Leprosy and Yaws.
The soul collected his helpers: Bat, Ant-eater, Owl, and Hog.
The soul and his helpers arrived in Death's village for a visit. Soul sent Bat out first to go and
spy. Bat went and hung in Death's hut, in a corner of the roof, and no one noticed him. Death
was just saying to his soldiers:
'I will raise a cloud in the sky; from it you, Lightning, will jump
[Page [165] ]
down on to my house, which I will give to Soul, and you will destroy it and everything in it.'
The soldiers of Death agreed, and dispersed.
Soul arrived in the village and was received with honour. Death vacated his own house to
make sure that his guest would have every comfort. But Bat kept an eye on the sky, and
suddenly he saw a cloud approaching. He shouted: 'We must go now! Run!'
Soul departed hastily, with his soldiers. They had not gone far when Lightning struck Death's
house and destroyed it totally.
Death exulted: 'I have killed the soul!' He assembled his soldiers and had the drums beaten.
'Ha! He said that he could not be killed!'
At that moment they heard the drums of triumph being beaten in the village of the Soul.
Death at once went out with his army, but Soul had ordered Hog and Ant-eater to dig deep
pits and trenches on the way. Death and his party all fell into the pits and gave up the attack.
Since that day the Soul has been immortal.
9. THE ORIGIN OF MANKIND
The name Tondo-lindo is full of double entendre , for, as Ngbandi is a tonal language, each
written word-form can be pronounced on four or five different tones. On the face of it, the
name seems to mean 'Tondo fruit of the region', but if taken as single syllables and assembled
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
138 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 140 ---
with different tones, there are allusions in it to to (father), ndo (forest), toli (testicle), linda
(clitoris), li (seed, fruit, produce), and li (to enter).
It is conceivable that the Tondo-lindo legend belongs to the end of the story, and that the
moral is that men who belong to earth should find wives on earth. It is stated, though, that
before she went back to heaven, the first woman bore many children. For the Ngbandi, this
story explains the cause of the dry season: no rain falls
[Page [166] ]
because there is a total drought in heaven itself, and, like certain trees, the celestial people
are lying as if dead until the rains return.
Many years ago the elephant came down to earth from heaven, where he was born. He met
Lightning, who had also come down. They agreed to hold a competition in noise-making.
Elephant started, and he trumpeted so loud that the trees trembled: 'Haaah! Hoooh!' But
Lightning just sat there quietly. Then it was his turn. He thundered so loudly that the earth
herself shook, the trees were uprooted, their branches broken, and the rivers flooded the
country.
The elephant was so frightened that he died on the spot. His body just lay there, and his
bowels started fermenting. His stomach began to swell up until it burst, and out of it came all
the seeds of all the good plants that Elephant had been eating in Heaven. That is how the
vegetables came to earth.
A girl came along one day and found a 
tondo fruit (which is used to cure yaws). It was nicely
ripe and red. She took it home and put it in a box. Later, she opened the box again and found
a complete man inside. She asked his name, and he said it was Tondo-lindo.
The girl fell in love with him and married him. She gratified all his whims, and they lived in
peace a long time.
One day the man was in the forest and found many seeds and vegetables. It was the place of
the dead elephant. He collected them and put them in a box. He came home with it, but no
one could open the box. At last an old woman came along and opened it. Lo! Innumerable
little children emerged, who flew away in different directions, like young ants in the rainy
season.
One day the bird Gbaima made a pit in the forest, to trap animals in. He put the tortoise on
guard and said: 'If you catch an animal, you may eat of its meat, but not if it is Lightning, for
that is the meat I want to eat.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
139 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 141 ---
He went away, and shortly afterwards Lightning swooped down and fell in the pit. The
tortoise could not resist the temptation to
[Page [167] ]
taste a piece of Lightning, and so he took a knife and started cutting its body. Lightning began
to thunder so terrifically that the tortoise was frightened and hid under the dry leaves.
Lightning escaped and flew back to heaven.
Gbaima returned and found Tortoise trembling under the leaves. He began to rebuke him,
saying: 'Now you have let my dinner escape, go and fetch it!'
Of course, the tortoise could not climb, so he delegated the spider to go in his stead. The
spider climbed up to heaven, but he met Lightning, who thundered so loudly that he
frightened Spider away. Spider would have crashed to his death on earth if it had not been for
his thread. He descended all the way to earth and walked away .
In another version of the story the spider is really a man, the first man to descend from
heaven. So it was that man came to earth---but his wife's parents still lived in heaven. His
father-in- law told him that he was not allowed to come and visit his in-laws in heaven during
the dry season. Of course, one day he could not resist the urge to go and visit his wife in the
middle of the dry season.
You know how he found them? They were just lying there like dead branches, lean like
sticks. Out of his wife's hair the husband pulled a hairpin, which he took with him when he
went down to earth.
When the rainy season began the wife descended to earth to rejoin her husband. They
quarrelled, and he showed her the hairpin. She was annoyed and decided to go back to
heaven. The husband followed her, but got a frosty reception from his father- in-law, who
gave him a drum and said: 'When you are almost down, beat the drum, so that we may know.'
He did so, but when the in-laws heard the drum, they cut the rope so that the man crashed
down on earth. Nzapa then decided that man should never again be allowed to climb up to
heaven.
[Page [168] ]

10. MAN AND HIS DOG
A man went into the forest with his dog to look for honey. At last they found a tree, in and out
of which the bees were buzzing. The man said: 'If only I had a companion to help me!'
The dog opened his mouth and spoke in human tongue: 'I can help you, on condition that you
never mention anything I tell you to your fellow humans. If you do, you will die.'
The man agreed, and the dog taught him the language of the animals, so that he could
understand not only the dogs but also the black ants and the cockroaches.
The man took his axe and cut the tree open. The dog helped him take out the honey, which
they collected together and brought home.
On their way they passed through the village of the man's in-laws, and as it was beginning to
rain, he decided to call in. His mother-in-law wanted to fry a potful of ants for him as a
garnish for the porridge. The pot of ants was on a shelf under the roof, so the woman had to
reach above her head to get it.
At that moment the man heard a cockroach on the shelf, warning his fellows: 'Watch out, the
mistress is coming! Take care, the lady of the house is approaching!'
The man laughed at this, but his mother-in-law was furious. She cried: 'You laughed at me
because you saw my nakedness! You saw my loin-cloth slip!'
Her son-in-law denied it, but he would not say what the real reason for his laughing had been.
The whole village of his in-laws became involved in the dispute, and they all took the old
lady's part. They demanded an explanation, because they believed he had laughed at seeing
the private parts of his mother-in-law. The man said: 'If I tell you, I shall die.' But they would
not believe him. So the man secretly sent word to his father's brother, to say that his in-laws
were killing him.
Then he told his in-laws what the cockroach had been saying.
[Page [169] ]
At once he heard his dog howl in dismay, and a few moments later the animal died. Shortly
afterwards the man, too, dropped on the floor and died.
Meanwhile, his paternal uncle assembled all his friends and relatives; they came with spears,
and together they marched on the village of his nephew's in-laws, surrounded it and attacked.
It was a terrible war. Many people were killed.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
141 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 143 ---
Never tell a secret to anyone.
11. HOW THE NGBANDI LEARNED TO TRA VEL ON W ATER
The sia is a striped mouse who loads all her children on to a large leaf that floats in the water,
and pushed it from behind, swimming after it.
The ndoko is a water-bird that shouts: ' Tí húruru! ' ('fall often'), and perches on a stick which
floats in the water. The bird often disappears into the water but surfaces immediately
afterwards, as if he possessed a secret medicine to keep alive. His name is also the sound of
the heart-beat: listen to it---'Ndoko, ndoko ...'
When they saw him perching on his stick, the people decided they could use a floating tree in
the same manner, while doing their fishing.
That is what gave them the idea that led to the invention of boats.
12. ANIMAL FABLES
I. The Spider and the Mason-fly
The spider and the mason-fly went to the forest to hunt. The mason-fly discovered a dead
elephant and told the spider. Spider said: 'Remember, I found it, not you, I want all that meat,
not you!'
[Page [170] ]
The mason-fly was sad and went to his uncle: 'Uncle, what shall I do to get some of that
elephant's meat?' His uncle told him, and Mason-fly went back to the elephant, crept into the
body through the hole the arrow had made, and waited.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
142 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 144 ---
Soon, Spider arrived and started carving up the elephant, wanting to carry all the meat home
that day. Then the mason-fly started making noises from inside: 'Hooo, Hooo!' Spider was
terrified, for he thought it was the spirit of the mighty elephant. So Spider shouted: 'All right,
all right, Mason-fly found it, he may have it all.' And he went away.
II. 
Spider tries his wives
Spider had two wives, Dale and Kondo. He loved Dale more than Kondo. Dale always got
what she wanted. One day Spider decided to try his wives to see who loved him most. He
pretended to be terribly ill, thrashing his hands and feet like an animal in the throes of death.
Then he lay quite still. As soon as Kondo realized that her husband was dead, she wept
bitterly. Dale, however, did not cry at all, she only wailed a little, because it was the custom,
but Spider could hear that she felt no grief. So, Spider got up, and Kondo was overjoyed:
'You are cured!' Dale was chased away for good. Spider now gave all his favours to Kondo.
III. The Dog and the Cock
Long ago, the dog and the cock lived together in the forest, for in those days they were still
wild animals, and did not belong to Man.
One day they decided to go and steal Woman, so they went and abducted her. Man went to
his uncle and asked him what to do. Uncle told him, for he was very wise. Man took four
bags of maize and pounded them. Then he went near the village of the cock and scattered the
maize on the road. Then he went to the village of the dog, took the bitch and all the puppies
and put them in a bag.
[Page [171] ]
The dog came back from hunting just at that moment, and tried to stop him, but Man
wounded him with his spear. The dog howled with pain, and the cock, on hearing this,
decided to go and help his ally. On the way from his village, however, he found the maize and
could not resist the temptation. He ate it all up, and by the time he arrived at the dog's village,
Man had gone with his bag.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
143 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 145 ---
Next day, Man went to the forest and shot a deer; then he went to the dog's village and put
the dead animal down on the road. That done, he went to the village of the cock, took the hen
and the chicks and put them all in his bag. The cock came flying home and attacked the man,
but the spear wounded him. The dog in his village heard the cock crow and came running to
help him, but on his path he found the carcass of the deer and he could not resist the
temptation, so he ate it all. By the time he arrived at the cock's village, the man was gone
with his bag. The cock and the dog then decided to go to the village of Man to beg for their
families to be returned. They understood they had to bring Woman, so they took her out of
the dog's house where they had hidden her, and went with her to the house of Man.
Having received his wife back, Man said: 'You may stay here, for I will keep your wives and
children with me, just in case you deceive me again.'
This is how the dog and the cock became domesticated.
IV. 
The Elephant and the Dudu- bird
The Elephant and the Dudu -bird decided to go on a hunting expedition together. When they
had finished hunting they wanted their wives to know they would be coming home soon, so
that their meals would be ready. The Elephant trumpeted so that the forest shook. 'Haaa,
hooo!' Then the Dudu -bird called: ' Dudu, dudu! '
Some distance away there was another dudu -bird sitting in a tree, repeating the words,
singing: ' Dudu, dudu! ' This was heard by a third dudu -bird farther away, who passed on the
word, and
[Page [172] ]
so on, until the song reached the edge of the forest where the wife of the first dudu -bird
lived.
When she heard ' dudu ', she thought: 'My husband is calling me, he must be wanting his
meal.' So she started cooking his dinner, a true dudu -dinner. When the Elephant and the
Dudu arrived, the meal was ready.
The Dudu invited his friend, but Elephant declined politely, saying that there would of course
be a real elephant-dinner waiting for him. But when he came home, there was not. His wife
had never heard his trumpeting.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
144 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 146 ---
V.  The Elephant and the Gazelle
The Gazelle was afraid that the elephant would trample on him, for Elephant was in an
aggressive mood. So, Gazelle asked Locust to go and jump on Elephant's back and gnaw
through the string of Elephant's loin-cloth, leaving only a thin thread. Locust took a big jump
and did as he was asked.
As soon as Elephant saw Gazelle he jumped forward to trample on him, but at that moment
the string of his loin-cloth broke on his back, so he had to hold it with his big hand lest his
shame be uncovered. So, Gazelle had time to escape.
VI. 
Kwan the Boa and Longo the Viper
Kwan suggested to Longo: 'Let us both cut off our tails!'
Longo agreed, but he said: 'How shall I know that you have done it?'
Kwan said: 'I will cut off my tail under water so that the coolness will relieve the pain. If the
water is coloured red, that is the sign that I have cut off my tail.'
Longo said: 'All right, as soon as I see that, I will cut off my own tail too.'
Kwan, who is a very cunning animal, went and picked a zamba - fruit, the juice of which is
blood-red. He squeezed it under water and pulled faces, as if he were feeling awful pains.
[Page [173] ]
Longo, on seeing this, took his knife and cut off his own tail. Then Kwan came out of the
water and showed his tail. Did he laugh!
Longo was furious and went for him, but without his tail he was too slow and could not
overtake Kwan. There came a bush- fire that advanced more rapidly through the dry grass
than Longo, so the viper was burnt.
Still today, if someone is trying to cheat you, you say: 'Are you trying to do a Kwan on me?'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
145 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 147 ---
VII. The Spider and the Crocodile
Spider went to visit Crocodile on the other bank of the river. Crocodile, as a good host,
offered his house to Spider. Spider said: 'For firewood, I use only koso -wood.' This wood,
contains much resin.
Crocodile obligingly went out, found some koso -wood, came back, built a fire for his guest
in his own house, and left Spider alone for the night. Spider searched in all the corners until
he found Crocodile's eggs under the bedstead. Crocodiles are very fertile. Spider took the
eggs and fried them over the fire. They spluttered and crackled, but so did the resin in the
koso -wood, so that Crocodile, who lay outside, was not perturbed by it. Spider ate all his
host's eggs.
The next morning he demanded a canoe and two rowers to take him across the river. While
the boat was being made ready , Spider locked the door of Crocodile's house with a well-tied
string.
When they were in the middle of the river, Crocodile began to shout from the bank: 'Bring
him back, he has stolen my e-e-e-e-eggs!'
The rowers asked Spider: 'What did he say?'
Spider answered: 'He says, row quickly, for a storm is gathering and this canoe capsizes
easily.'
The rowers rowed as quickly as they could, and as soon as the boat hit the sand of the bank,
Spider jumped ashore and said:
[Page [174] ]
'What Crocodile really said was that I had stolen his eggs. That is quite true. I ate them all. It
was a good meal. Good-bye!'
At that, Spider laughed heartily, and before the rowers could catch him, he had vanished into
the forest.
VIII. The Spider and the Spirit of Makune
The Kulu-ngu is a bird which calls: ' Kulu! Kulu! Ngú!' Ngú means 'rain', so that the bird
calls for rain, and if someone brings misfortune on his own head by mentioning it, it is
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
146 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 148 ---
proverbial among the Ngbandi to say: 'You are like a kulungu , now you've got what you
asked for!' As we say: 'Talk of the devil ...' The word kpman denotes a certain fruit; it also
means 'to be angered, to be furious, to sulk'. The word makune means 'climb alone'; it is also
the name of a very large variety of the kpman -fruit which, the people believe, is bewitched,
so that no one will pick it. To be alone is in itself to arouse suspicion; only greedy people sit
alone, because they do not wish to share their meals, and sorcerers, because they hatch evil
plans against their neighbours. The Ngbandi know that the Spider is a greedy egoist, as is
shown by the story of the Mason-fly, and that of the Crocodile. The kpman -fruit is sucked,
not eaten whole, and so one 'sucks anger'. The Spider in sucking the fruit, sucks in an evil
spirit called Makune which is, as follows from the above, a type of 'fury'. Ngusu means
'jiggers' or 'carrion worms, maggots'. These insects are lethal: they eat the body from the
inside. Sugar-cane ( ngan ) is used by the Ngbandi to get rid of catarrh.
Spider was walking in the forest when he saw the Kulungu -birds picking kpman -fruits. He
climbed the tree in which they were sitting, picked a makune , and ate it.
Spider said: 'I am going to the village of the Ngusu .'
The spirit Makune, sitting in Spider's stomach, echoed: 'I am going to the Ngusu!'
Spider was terrified when he heard this and ran to his wife: 'I am dying, I am practically dead!
Give me some sugar-cane!'
The Makune inside him called out loudly so that everyone could hear it: 'Did you say you
were dying! Really! You only want to
[Page [175] ]
eat sugar-cane because you want to kill me! If you do, I will stretch your intestines so that
you will scream!'
Spider said to his wife: 'Wife, leave that sugar-cane, I will not have it after all. Something is
alive in my belly, which is eating my inside.'
Makune burst out from inside Spider's body: 'Am I eating you? Really! Yo u have eaten me !'
Word spread in the village that Spider was not well, and his neighbours came to see him.
Spider told his story: 'There is an evil spirit in my bowels who is torturing me!'
The Makune shouted at the people from inside Spider's bowels: 'I was sitting peacefully in a
fruit, but he had to eat me, so I will stay here and torture him!'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
147 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 149 ---
Spider saw his wife making soup and said: 'Wife, give me some of that soup. I want to eat it
boiling hot: if I am going to die, at least that evil spirit will die with me.'
The Makune shouted: 'He is going to pour hot soup on my face! I am getting out of this
wretched place!' He left Spider's bowels from behind, and said: 'I am gone!' He sat down at
the back of the house.
Spider sighed and exclaimed angrily: 'That ugly spirit! I could not even breathe! I hope
someone cuts his eyes out with a knife! That would be a good thing!' The Makune, on hearing
this, returned and crept back into Spider's bowels. He clutched Spider's intestines and
pinched them, shaking them so that Spider cowered and screamed.
Spider's wife said: 'Why didn't you wait a little longer before cursing that spirit? Now he has
heard you and is coming back.'
Makune shouted from inside the bowels: 'I said I was gone: but you cursed me. Now I have
come back, so I will stay in your belly five full days and make you suffer: after that I will
depart.'
Spider said: 'If you wish, you may have my wife. Take her, but please leave me in peace that
I may eat and enjoy my food.'
[Page [176] ]
Makune answered from his victim's belly: 'I want to eat and enjoy you , and I will repeat
whatever you say. I am your death!'
Spider replied: 'All right, if I have to die anyway, you will die with me. I am going to pour hot
soup on your eyes. Wife! Bring me that soup when it is boiling!'
The faithful wife, whom Spider had just offered to the evil spirit in exchange for his own
health, brought the soup with which her husband was going to kill himself as well as his
torturer. But the Makune-spirit did not wait for it to fall on his head. He left Spider's body,
fled into the forest and hid himself again in a large makune .
Is it any wonder that people never touch that fruit?

IX. Man and Ape
A certain man always walked naked. This was in the days when there was as yet no difference
between apes and men, except that certain people did not like wearing clothes and others did.
Of course, the well-dressed people laughed at the naked people and called them 'monkeys'.
So the naked people were ashamed and lived in the forest most of the time.
The naked man had taken a wife. Whenever he visited his in-laws he arrived only after
sunset, so that no one could see that he walked stark naked. He used to take a little boy with
him, whose job was to wake him before dawn was visible in the sky. He usually gave some of
the food he got to the little boy . But one night his brother-in-law prepared a chicken for him,
and he found this so delicious that he ate it all. The next morning the little boy had gone
without waking him. So the naked man had to go home with his wife in full daylight.
Of course all the people laughed at him on seeing his private parts. From that time he always
stayed in the forest and never showed himself in the village again. That is the origin of the
chimpanzees.
One day a man went into the forest and lost his way. He
[Page [177] ]
heard the leopards spitting, so he climbed a tree. There he sat for days on end, terrified,
getting leaner and leaner, and not knowing what to do. An ape came along who took pity on
him. He helped the man climb down and showed him the way to his village.
The man said: 'Come and visit me one day!' The ape accepted this invitation eagerly, and not
long afterwards he showed up at the man's house. He thought the man had meant it! The man
had a potful of good juicy meat simmering on the fire and was reluctant to share it with such a
primitive guest, although both gratitude and hospitality commanded it. So the man gave the
ape a basket and said:
'Will you get me some water from the well first?'
The ape went to the pond, filled the basket and walked back with it, but of course it was
empty before he reached the house. So he went back and filled it again, with the same result.
At last he came back to the man and said: 'I can't do it.'
In the meantime, the man had eaten all the meat. Aren't men clever!
Never expect gratitude from people; they are greedier than apes.

X. The Dog and the Leopard
One day , the Leopardess crept into the hole of Kabondo, the ant-eater. There, she gave birth
to a lovely little leopard. After that she went out hunting, for she needed strong nourishment.
Then Kabondo the ant-eater came back, and found a baby leopard in his dwelling place. Of
course he ate it up, for ant-eaters like eating lots of things, and leopards are everybody's
enemy .
When the leopardess came back she found her baby gone. The ant-eater was wisely away at
that time, so the leopardess did not know whom to accuse. So she called a meeting of all the
animals and it was agreed that all should undergo the water test. The leopardess would swim
across the river first, and all the animals would follow. The guilty one, it was believed, would
be seized by
[Page [178] ]
the River Spirit, so that on the other bank he would be shivering all over.
When the animals were all standing on the other bank, it was discovered that the dog was
shivering all over his body, for dogs always shiver when they have been swimming. The
leopardess, without further ado, killed the dog for having murdered her baby, and ate him.
When the wife of the dog heard this, she took all her puppies and went to the village of Man.
She said to Man: 'I want to come and live with you from now on. The animals have accused
my husband of eating a young leopard. But he didn't do it; someone else did. Now he has
been sentenced and executed. I will take my revenge on all the animals. I will help you to
hunt them.'
Since then, the dog helps man to hunt and kill all the other animals. It is for revenge.
Never accuse anyone falsely.
XI. 
The Leopard and the Tortoise
The leopardess was pregnant again and appointed the tortoise as nurse to look after her
babies. She gave birth to a nice big litter. Then she went out to hunt for her food, and left the
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
150 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 152 ---
tortoise in charge.
The tortoise, being no friend of leopards, killed all the baby leopards and ate them up. After
that, the tortoise thought it would be sensible to disappear, and asked the bat to carry her up
to the top of a palm-tree. The bat, not being a friend of the leopard, did so.
The leopardess, on coming back, saw that all her cubs had disappeared, together with the
tortoise. Furious, she went out in search of the tortoise, and saw him at the top of the
palm-tree. Good climber that she was, she walked up the stem of the palm- tree, just like a
palm-wine tapper. When she was almost at the top the tortoise got nervous and fell out, thud!
on to the ground. The leopardess had to go all the way down again.
[Page [179] ]
In the meantime, the tortoise found shelter in the ant-eater's hole. The ant-eater, being no
friend of leopards, pretended to know nothing. The leopardess, furious, commanded the
bush- pigs to go and search for the tortoise. The bush-pigs, being no friends of the leopards,
saw no reason why they should give away the tortoise, although they knew where he was
hiding.
The leopardess started abusing the bush-pigs: 'You dirty pigs, you know very well what the
tortoise has done! You are her accomplices! She has killed all my children, and you refuse to
help me! I will never leave you in peace!'
Children! Remember this story, for it contains many lessons. Girls! Never leave your baby in
charge of someone who does not like you. If you cannot find anyone you can trust, carry your
baby on your back when you go out to the fields. Furthermore, it is useless to pursue
someone who has many friends, for he will always find someone to help him. Thirdly, take
care in life that you do not make enemies unnecessarily, for the day may come when you will
need other people's help and on that day they will all turn against you. Boys! You must not try
to order people around if you cannot be sure of their loyalty. The people will not obey you,
but make common cause against you, as soon as their hatred is greater than their fear. If you
try to eat them, they will eat your children.
XII. 
The Black Ants and the Termites
Pipi is the name of the big black ants who trek through the bush in long caravans. Their
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
151 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 153 ---
greatest enemies are the kangi , the termites, and whenever they meet any they will slaughter
them. The reason is the ferocious blood-thirst of the termites, and their aggressiveness.
Listen! One day a man had cut grass and had spread it out in the sun to dry. The next day he
came back to bind it, but a termite bit his hand, for he wanted the straw too. The man
returned home with a bleeding hand.
[Page [180] ]
A butterfly fluttered by and saw the blood. It exclaimed: 'Who has killed a man?' The snake
Longo was basking in the sun, but on hearing this it crept away into a mole-hole, for fear of
being accused of killing a man. The mole was terrified on seeing the snake enter his hole, so
he climbed a tree. In the tree there was a monkey, who now saw a thing he had never seen
before: a mole climbing a tree. The monkey fled, believing some serious thing had happened.
In his haste, the monkey broke off a dead branch, which fell heavily on the elephant.
The elephant was deeply disturbed and exclaimed: 'This has never happened before! Dead
branches falling on my back!' The elephant stampeded angrily through the forest and trampled
on a baby ant, the son of a 
pipi .
The pipi called a big meeting of all the animals, in which he accused the elephant of killing
his son. The elephant disclaimed all responsibility and accused the monkey of dropping dead
wood on his royal back. The monkey blamed the mole. The mole accused the snake Longo
of housebreaking. The snake explained that he had heard the butterfly cry murder, and that he
did not want to be accused of it, so he had hidden himself in the nearest hole he could find.
The butterfly described the man's bleeding hand.
By this time the 
kangi had realized that he would be accused of starting it all, so he said
contemptuously: 'Why does the pipi want to stir up all that old story again?'
Of course, all the animals understood that he was trying to divert attention from himself,
because he was guilty of manslaughter, or at least assault. It was the termite's aggressive
behaviour that caused the elephant to trample on the black baby ant.
This story explains why the black ants always fight the termites as soon as they see them.
Children! Always be careful what you do, for every action sets in motion a chain of
consequences that you cannot control.

[Page [181] ]
7 The Azande
[Page [182] ]
The Azande live along the north-eastern frontier, where the Congo borders on the Sudan and
Oubangui-Chari. The Azande are late arrivals in the Congo. They invaded and settled its
north-eastern corner during the nineteenth century (see Chapter 5).
The Azande brought with them their mysterious religion, which they may have acquired from
the peoples in the Sudan. In North Africa in the time of St Augustine there lived the
adherents of Mani, the Persian prophet of light. These Manichaeans were persecuted by both
Christians and Muslims. But the identity in name with Mani, the Rainbow-god of the
Azande, may be no more than a coincidence.
I. THE GOD MANI
Mani is one of the most mysterious gods of the Congo. Like the great god of the ancient
Persians, Ahura-Mazda-Zurvan, 
4 Mani of the Azande has a double nature, one dark and one
light. The Azande have added a sexual distinction: the light-coloured bundle on top is the
male god Yende, the dark bundle underneath is the goddess Ndasu.
Mani manifests himself in the rainbow, kelema , which is believed to be a double snake. Two
snakes, one light, one dark-coloured, are lying with one another in the sky. When they are not
visible they live in the water, and the grand masters of the secret sect of Mani
[Page [183] ]
possess the power to kill people by means of the water. If a Mani sorcerer wishes to kill a
man, that man will feel an irresistible urge to go and bathe in the river. The water will make
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
153 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 155 ---
him drowsy and drag him down to the bottom. There are many indescribable creatures living
on the bottom of the river who will kill the victim, or torture him and then push him back on
shore. Some members of the cult, it is said, control the power of lightning ( koki ), and can
direct it against those who arouse their wrath.
Those members of the sect who control the leopard ( ekopi ), can send him out to go and kill
people for them.
Those who control the boa snake ( nioka ) can call him by means of a magic whistle. The
snake arrives at once when whistled by a grand master of the order, and hurries to pay him
homage by coiling around the master's middle and caressing him with its head. The snake
will listen attentively to its master's instructions and obey him blindly. It will penetrate the
bedroom of its master's most dreaded enemy in the middle of the night, and bite or strangle
him so that he dies. It can also be employed to steal for its master. It can penetrate where
people cannot get in, it can steal a money-box and get away with it, or, more important still, it
can take a fetish so that its owner becomes vulnerable to the magic practices of the snake's
master. A fetish is worth more than gold. Part of the initiation ceremony is to make the
novice blow his new whistle and actually see the snakes slide towards him from all sides,
creep up to him and kiss him on the lips, coiling around him.
The fifth and most important power of the grand master of the Mani order is embedded in the
image of the god 
Y ende , which enables its owner to forecast the future.
If any member of the order should tell any of the Mani secrets to an uninitiated person, the
grand master will take him to the forest, whistle up the snakes, and leave him to their mercy.
The power of the Mani cult is illustrated in the following stories.
In Boma, the story was told in 1920 that a sergeant of the army went to bathe in the river and
drowned, through the infernal power of his sergeant-major, who was then grand master of the
order of Mani.
[Page [184] ]
It has been confirmed by the inhabitants of the district that in Mungwa, one of the villages
near Boma, on 18 November 1917, a man who was living near a marsh had a large wound
which would not heal. One of the masters of the Mani order caused lightning to fall on the
wound. At once the wound was cured--- fresh skin had grown over it.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
154 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 156 ---
The wives of the Congolese soldiers who fought against the German troops in Tanganyika
used to go to Nangai, grand mistress of the sect, priestess of Ndasu, Yende's wife; the women
wished to consult her regarding the fate of their husbands, to know whether they were alive
and faithful to their wives, and if they would survive. Nangai would go at dawn and stand
before the statue of Yende, kneel down and take the god up with both hands, and ask him the
question. She would convey the god's answer to her client against payment often francs. She
herself was constantly in direct contact with her own husband, himself a grand master of the
order, who was also at the front line.
2. THE GOD TULE
Tule is almost as mysterious as Mani. The name means 'spider'. We have seen that the 'spider'
can also be the name of the first man who descended from heaven (see Chapter 6).
In the same chapter we saw how the spider's body is entered by makune, the spirit associated
with diarrhoea. In this chapter it seems that the spider is himself the diarrhoea spirit, for he
causes the death of the man-eating bird in that manner, and in the previous story he appears to
be familiar with the 'path of water and what has left the belly'.
Magic plumes, a man's head-dress, in a mystic way preserve the presence of his spirit after
his death. Of course this spirit will help his son on earth to find food for himself and his
family, as a good ancestral spirit should. The son has a mystic relationship
[Page [185] ]
with his father's head-plumes, for if the ancestral spirit suffers, he suffers.
At the beginning of time there were no plants or trees, and nothing to eat; just bare earth.
Then Tule arrived, and in his hand he had the seeds of all the edible plants and all the cereals
and all the fruit trees. He sowed these seeds, scattering them over all the lands. Then he took
his drum and flew to the middle of the sky. There he began to play his drum, and all the seeds
germinated: stalks and leaves sprouted out from them.
There lived an old woman in a little hut. She planted yams which grew up abundantly. They
were so delicious that people came from far away to taste them. She would scrape them and
cook them properly, and serve her visitors one after the other. Alas, having eaten these good
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
155 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 157 ---
yams, the guest would get a throat- splitting thirst and ask for water. The old woman would
say she had no eater. The visitor would insist, upon which the woman invariably took a knife
and slit her visitor's throat.
When Tule heard about this murderous old woman, he decided to investigate the matter
personally. As a precaution, he took a calabash, filled it with water and put it in his bag. Then
he cut a reed and put it in the bag as well. He set out, and finally arrived at the old woman's
hut. He told her that he had heard about her cookery and that he would like to taste her
famous yam dish.
She went and cooked a meal for him, which he ate with relish. Having finished, he felt
thirsty, so he took out the reed, put one end in the calabash and began to suck up the water.
After that he felt hungry again, and asked the woman to cook more food. The woman did so,
but Tule was still not satisfied. The woman was annoyed, took her knife, and approached
Tule with the intention of cutting him to pieces.
Tule fled towards the gutter, but the old woman said: 'You cannot go there, it is the
dung-path.'
Tule, however, knew where he was going: he carried on and
[Page [186] ]
landed in a large jar, which was full of water. Tule plunged in and broke the jar. The water
spread out all over the earth.
This is how people came to know about water.
3. TULE AND THE MAN-EATING BIRD
A giant bird, Nzanginzanginzi, used to sit near the road, and all the people who passed there
were eaten by it. When Tule heard about this he decided to investigate the matter. He forged
a large number of small knives, and tied them on his back and round his body. Then he set
out, and spied the giant bird from behind a rock.
The bird shouted: 'Where are you going? Come here, I will devour you!'
Tule abused him with insults. Finally the bird was so vexed that he took Tule, folded him up
and swallowed him.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
156 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 158 ---
Inside the bird's stomach, Tule met all its previous victims and handed out his knives to
them, telling them to cut up the wall of the stomach. The men did so, singing merrily.
Nzanginzanginzi moaned loudly and sang: 'Having swallowed people, I swallowed Tule. But
he is evil: he is like diarrhoea, he cuts my inside out.'
The bird died, and Tule stepped out with his people.
4. TULE AND THE GUINEA-FOWL WOMAN
A bachelor was hunting guinea-fowl. He arrived at a place where there had been a plantation,
which is where guinea-fowl can be expected. He spotted one that was strutting about there.
He shot an arrow at it, but it escaped. He pursued it until he came upon
[Page [187] ]
an old woman who was hoeing her field. He asked her if she had seen his guinea-fowl: he had
hit it but it had escaped.
The old lady had indeed seen the bird, hit it with the shaft of her hoe, and hidden it in her
rubbish heap. Again the young man claimed his guinea-fowl, threatening the old woman that
she would hear more of it if she did not tell him where it was. This frightened her, and she
told him where to find his bird. He found the bird and handed it over to the old woman,
asking her to prepare it for him. In return he promised to help her with her hoeing.
The woman took the bird to her hut. First she prepared cassava porridge and vegetables. The
young man ate this without waiting for the meat, because he was very hungry. Finally she
brought the meat, but he refused it.
She told him to go and find a 
zambha lindi , a ripe red fruit, which he must throw on the
ground at the crossroads. Then he must go on, and look behind. The young man went and
found the red fruit, threw it down at the crossroads, walked on a little, and then looked
behind him. Lo! The fruit had changed into a beautiful girl dressed in red. He turned to her
and asked her: 'Where are you going?'
She answered: 'Do you not know that I am your wife?'
He said: 'Good! Let us go to my house.'
The God Tule met them on the road, and asked the young man how he had obtained such a
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
157 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 159 ---
beautiful wife, whereupon the young husband told him the story.
At once Tule set out on a guinea-fowl hunt. He saw a bird, hit it with his arrow, it escaped, he
pursued it, found the old woman and demanded his bird. When she refused, he threatened
her, and when she got frightened and told him where to look he found the bird on the
dirt-heap. He told her to prepare it and promised to help her hoe the fields. Soon, she brought
him the cooked cassava, then the vegetables and the meat. Tule ate it all, leaving the woman
only some vegetables. This greed would cost him dear.
[Page [188] ]
The old woman told him to go and pick a red fruit and throw it down behind him at the
crossroads. He did so, but instead of a beautiful girl, he saw behind him an ugly woman with
a long knife, coming rapidly closer. Once again Tule was obliged to flee on account of an old
woman.
He saw a man who was busy carving a drum. Tule asked the man to hide him. The man said:
'All right, I will hide you in the midst of my people, but don't be afraid.'
He led Tule into his enclosure, which was full of lions and leopards. The woman with the
knife arrived at the place where the man was once again sitting, carving his drum. She asked
him if he had seen the man she was running after, but he said 'No'.
She said: 'If I do not find him at once, I will slash you to pieces.'
He said: 'Go and look in the enclosure.'
She opened the gate, and at once the wild beasts ran out and killed her. The drum-carver took
her body and carved it up into small pieces. He asked Tule if he wanted a piece of it, and
Tule said he wanted only the head. This was duly prepared for him: he ate all the flesh so that
only the skull was left. Tule was sucking the brains when a bone from the skull found its way
down his throat and got stuck in his bowels so that he could not empty them. The spirit of the
woman had entered him and given him constipation.
Tule assembled the people of the land and told them to pull the old woman's skull-bone out
of his bowel. They all tried hard, but failed. An orphan boy appeared and offered to try, but
Tule refused. He told the people to make another attempt, but they failed. The orphan boy
offered his help again, and this time it was accepted. He adjusted the position of the
skull-bone in the god's bowel, and then pulled it out. With it came the contents of the bowel,
which were as heavy as those of all mankind put together.

5. TULE STEALS THE MAGIC PLUMES
A man called Yangaima lost his father. He took his father's ceremonial plumes, which he used
to wear when dancing, and departed. He arrived at a favourable spot and built his village
there.
One day Tule arrived at the gate, saw all the people, and decided to visit the place. He was
well received by Yangaima, who told his wives to prepare a meal. The meal was brought to
the veranda, where the men were conversing. Yangaima took his plumes, composed a song
about them, and began to dance. Soon his people gathered in a circle and joined in the
singing, clapping their hands.
Suddenly, the plumes lifted Yangaima up, and flew away with him into the sky. There he
hunted the birds so that a large number came tumbling down. His shed was full of birds when
they had all been collected and piled up in it. A large number of birds were cooked by the
women. Yangaima descended and began to eat, handing out pieces of meat to all his people
until everyone was satisfied, including the gluttonous Tule.
Tule went home, already wondering how he could get hold of those magic plumes. He waited
for two days. Then, while Yangaima was absent, he crept into his house and stole the plumes.
These were guarded by mason-flies who stung Tule brutally, making him run for his life. In
his flight, one little feather fell on the ground.
Yangaima went home and found that his father's magic plumes had disappeared. He felt as if
his heart was broken. He saw Tule's traces and found the one small feather that was left. He
tied it on to his headdress with a string. He went to Tule's house and found Tule already
dancing his magic dance, composing a song of his own in which he pretended that the plumes
were given to him. The plumes rose in the air with Tule, who began to chase the birds.
[Page [190] ]
Yangaima too performed his magic with his single feather, which contained enough power to
lift him up in the air. Soaring to where Tule was flying, Yangaima greeted him politely,
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
159 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 161 ---
saying:
'My friend, let us dance together in the sky: I will dance higher, you will dance lower.'
Tule agreed. They began to dance close to each other. Suddenly Yangaima tore away the
plumes from Tule's head. Tule fell down to his death.
