8 The Mayombe
[Page [192] ]
HOW THE MA YOMBE CAME TO THEIR PRESENT HOME
The Mayombe live in the west, in that corner of the Congo which lies to the north of the
great river and borders on the Kabinda enclave.
Nzondo, a man with one limp leg, was a chief in the Bakongo country; he lived south of the
great river. One day he assembled all his people and announced that he had decided they
would all leave those lands, and go in search of a new country to settle. So they packed all
their belongings and started trekking. On the bank of the Nzadi, which is the local name of
the Congo river, 
5 he ordered a halt. He had decided to cross the river first, together with his
wife.
Near the bank there was a rock. In the middle of the river the current was much stronger, so
Nzondo told his wife to sit down on the rock for a while, so that he could explore the river
first. Suddenly, his belt was torn from his waist and changed into a tree, standing there in the
middle of the river. At the same moment, his wife began to pass blood, and her fingers and
toes grew fixed into the rock. She, too, changed into a tree, standing there in the middle of
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
160 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 162 ---
the river on top of the rock.
Nzondo realized that it was not the will of the great spirit Nkisi that he should cross the river.
So he called the chiefs of the
[Page [193] ]
nine tribes, telling them that they must cross one after the other: Makaba, Nanga, Manianga,
Pudi, Makulu, Ngimbi, Numbu, Mbenza and Mave ma Nzinga or Mboma Nakongo.
Nzondo stayed on the southern bank of the river; he had no more children.
Nzondo's tree, the Nsanda, is still there, guarding the crossing of the river. Mabaka went on
and settled in Kimongo. His magician could change his stick into a tree.
[Page [195] ]
9 The Bwaka
[Page [196] ]
THE LEGEND OF LAKE MOGALO IN UBANGI
The Bwaka live in the north, between the Loea and Oubangui rivers, near Libenge.
It is not unusual for boys to die during the circumcision operation that takes place at the time
of initiation, as no disinfectants are used.
Women are forbidden to go near the initiands, and it is forbidden to reveal the death of one of
these before the end of the year set aside for the bush school.
In this region it is quite normal for a bereaved mother to commit suicide; some have thrown
themselves on the ground, head first, hoping their necks would break. Others just let
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
161 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 163 ---
themselves die in the bush, like decaying trees, of hunger and exposure. The old woman in
this story had other plans, however.
Before the white men arrived, innumerable years ago, there lived a lonely old woman.
Incredibly, she gave birth to a son. When it was time for his circumcision, there was no
age-mate to be circumcised with him. Finally, he was taken to the forest by some men to be
initiated. During the operation he died. His mother was not informed, and she did not worry,
since the 'School of the Bush' may last a year, and the participants stay in the forest all the
time. She carried pots of porridge to the path near the forest entrance every day . The novice-
master would receive the food and say: 'He is well.'
At the end of the year, when the initiates returned from the
[Page [197] ]
forest, one of them, a friend of the dead boy, went to the old woman's hut, took a bedstead
out, put it upright against the wall, climbed on the roof, and removed the tuft of
elephant-grass from the peak of the roof: all this without a word. At that moment the dead
boy's mother understood, and burst into tears. She knew that her son had been buried in the
forest, in secret.
She decided she did not want to stay where her son's image would haunt her, so she left,
saying she was going to the forest. She went and collected poisonous leaves, of which she
knew many types. With these she concocted a gourdful of poison. Then she lay down on the
path to the dance-place, covered with dust.
All the boys and girls of the villages who had been successfully initiated went along there for
a 
dua , a mixed dance which marks the end of the initiation ceremonies. The old woman
asked each of them to clean her with an ear of millet, but they all refused. At last, a small girl
came along who had a kind heart. Actually, she had been beaten by her sister, who did not
want her to go to the dance at all. But she had gone all the same, although she was still very
young. She was the only one who stopped.
Taking water from the stream she washed the old woman's body with her own hands, because
she found the ear of millet too hard and sharp. After that, she rubbed the old woman with
perfume. The woman was grateful and told her:
'You will be dancing all night and all morning. But by noon you must be away from the
dance-floor, for there will be trouble. You may warn your parents and those of your family
you wish to, but do not tell the villagers.'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
162 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 164 ---
The girl went on to the dance, and the next morning she warned her parents and her youngest
sister that they must leave, but tell nobody. So they did, saying to the other dancers that they
were going for a rest in the afternoon; this was nothing unusual.
Then the old woman rose and went round the dance-place, sprinkling her gourdful of magic
poison on the ground. The
[Page [198] ]
people thought she was doing this to prevent rain from falling, as this was the customary task
of old women. But the effect was different---and disastrous. Suddenly, the whole dance-floor
sank into the ground with all the dancers. Masses of boiling water spouted up from the
ground with thundering jets. The dancers all died and turned into fishes. The dance-place is
now a lake. The villages are now a desert.
[Page [199] ]
10 The Baluba of Kasai
[Page [200] ]
The Baluba of kasai live in the province of Kasai along the river of that name. They are one
of the prominent peoples of the Congo. Much valuable information about them has been
brought together by the Fathers of Scheut, an order of able missionaries who had competent
linguists and anthropologists among their ranks. The selection offered here is mainly culled
from the works of the Rev. R. V. Caeneghem, one of the prominent authorities on the Luba
peoples of the old days.
The stories of witches belong to the most fascinating of our present collection, especially
because for the Baluba of the not-so- very-old days these were no fairy tales, but true
accounts of real events. Even, today the majority of the people in Africa believe in spirits.
Witches are forced by the fellow-members of their covens to contribute human flesh to the
group for a common meal. They often have to kill a child of their own as no other persons are
available. Killing is done by means of a special type of secret poison. Poison is in 'reality' a
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
163 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 165 ---
spirit. By swallowing it, the victim gets the spirit inside his body, where it begins to eat away
the vital powers of its host. For this reason no one can trust his own relatives, let alone his
neighbours, and no one dares to be found outside his hut during the night.
Very often the one person least likely to have wished a man evil, his own wife, is suspected
of being the cause of his death. This has been repeatedly observed by missionaries in the
Congo, and it is not a matter of the past.
Becoming a widow is the worst that can happen to a woman of the Baluba. She is shaved, and
has to pay for this in cash. Most of her
[Page [201] ]
possessions are taken away from her, often even the hut in which she has lived. She has to
undergo intolerable 'purification' ceremonies, such as being smeared with a muddy mixture of
dung and urine. She will be held liable for her husband's debts, so that often anyone can come
along and claim money from a widow. The widows themselves appear to be willing and even
eager to undergo these humiliations because this 'purifies' them from the odious but
inevitable suspicion of having killed their husbands.
Father R. V. Coppenolle reports that in 1922 an accident happened in Kinshasa. A Muluba
fell off a moving tram and was killed by one of the coaches. The same day his fellow
tribesmen arrived in great numbers at his house, where his dearly loved wife lived. She had
been informed of the accident and she was in tears. The men, insensitive to her sorrow,
accused her of having bewitched the tram so that it would 'eat' her husband. They helped
themselves to the furniture by way of 'damages' and destroyed the garden. They dragged her
to the home of one of them and made her undergo unspeakable tortures, beating her with
leather thongs and then rubbing chilli-powder into the wounds: all this because they accused
her of killing their relative, saying that she owed them damages. The missionary intervened
and gave the woman shelter in the mission, but even there they came and molested her.
Father Van Caeneghem reports 
6 that in 1934 a certain man named Nkongolo had a brother
who fell ill. Nkongolo consulted a diviner, who said: 'One of your wives is a witch and has
laid a spell on your brother.' Nkongolo went home, took a knife and killed one of his wives.
His brother's disease persisted, so Nkongolo went to another diviner, who said: 'Your second
wife does not stay in bed at night; she goes out to practise witchcraft.' Nkongolo believed
this, went home and stabbed the poor woman until she died. But his brother was not cured.
Nkongolo went to see two more diviners, and the result was that he killed his two surviving
wives. By that time he was completely out of his mind.
When his brother's illness continued, he reproached him: 'I killed all my wives to remove the
witchcraft from your body but you refuse to get better!' He dragged the poor patient from his
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
164 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 166 ---
bed
[Page [202] ]
and killed him, with the same knife that had killed all his wives. After that he just stood
there, biting his nails.
In January 1951, two brothers, whose names were Lukwanga and Buta, lived together.
Lukwanga's wife had a baby who fell ill. A diviner was consulted, who hinted that Buta's wife
was a witch; she had learned her evil craft from an old aunt. Both women were seized,
maltreated, beaten and burnt. The old woman had to be taken to hospital with very severe
burns.
Then, Lukwanga's baby died. In his despair he was convinced that the two women of his
brother's household were responsible. He took a bottle, filled it with water, and killed his
brother Buta's two young sons with it in an attack of rage.
This loss of the sense of reality is in itself a sign of witchcraft. If a witch gives you food it
may look like beef or mutton whereas in reality it is human meat. The guest who thought he
was being given a treat was in reality the victim of delusion. Once having eaten human flesh,
he is for ever in the power of the witch. He will have visions, illusions of being transported
through the air to the meetings of witches. He will be told: 'Now you must surrender one of
your relatives, your father or your brother, or your sister's eldest son. If you don't, you will
die, or else your own son will. You may choose ...'
A father was told by a diviner that his own child was a witch. He beat him until the boy
confessed. A white chaplain passed and saved the boy. In 1953 the father was still in prison.
Father Van Caeneghem was himself accused of witchcraft by one of his own pupils with
whom he had had a slight difference. For years the student kept writing and begged to be
forgiven by the powerful white priest, who had 'persecuted him with magic'.
One seminarist who was accusing three girls of witchcraft was told by his bishop that it was
all nonsense, to which he replied: 'Monseigneur, it is impossible for me not to believe in
witchcraft.'
[Page [203] ]
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
165 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 167 ---
1. THE WOMAN WHO LOST THREE CHILDREN
In the old days, the poison test was applied whenever more than one person was accused of
witchcraft. The Belgian administration introduced severe penalties against this test, making it
equivalent to manslaughter. The poison, tshipapa , had to be drunk by each accused person.
If she refused it was a confession of witchcraft. If she drank it and it had no effect, she was
acquitted. If the accused vomited it out, there was also no case against her. But if the poison
killed the accused, this was considered as proof of guilt. The poison itself contained an evil
spirit which killed others.
A woman had three children and lived happily with her husband. One day one of the children
fell ill and died; then the second, then the third. Of course her husband accused her of being a
witch, because everybody knows that witches kill their own children. So her husband wanted
to have nothing to do with her any more. The night after her last child died, he shut her out of
his house for fear of being the next victim.
Having nowhere else to go, the woman went and sat down on the front porch of her mother-
in-law's hut. In the middle of the night she heard a noise on the roof. She looked up and heard
the voices of six witches: one of them was the mother-in-law herself. They had human meat
with them which they offered to the tutelary spirits of the house. The witches became
suspicious, saying there were 'revealers' present, by which they meant human eyes. They
looked for these eyes but could not find them. Yet the young bereaved mother was sitting
there all the time, and it was her presence that gave the tutelary spirits the power to refuse the
meat.
The mother-in-law produced the head of the last child who had died, saying to the other
witches: 'See, what a good hostess I am, providing my own flesh and blood for you to share.
What are you contributing?'
[Page [204] ]
The other witches then discussed their plans. One mentioned the 'big man who lived in her
house', meaning her husband, whom she intended to kill. Another one spoke about her
husband's brother, as she had already killed her husband.
Finally, the mother-in-law saw her invisible guests off. When she came back (this time in a
normal fashion, along the path for human beings), she found her daughter-in-law, who
pretended to be asleep. She asked her: 'Were you here while we were talking?'
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
166 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 168 ---
The young woman said: 'Talking? I heard nothing. Who were you talking to?'
'Oh, just my friends!'
The young woman went home and told her husband everything. Two months later, the
husband's uncle began to languish. The woman remembered what the witches had said: 'After
two months we will kill him.' Then the husband believed his wife's story that his own mother
was a witch and had killed his children, her own grandchildren. The husband warned his
relatives, and at a meeting of the elders of the village the six witches were summoned,
because the diviner had confirmed the young wife's allegations. The poison-mixer was called
upon to provide a suitable poison for the poison test.
Tshipapa was prepared. The six witches were all killed by the poison.
After that the young woman had many more children who lived and grew up, and so did other
women in the village, so that the population began to increase again. No more lights were
seen travelling through the district during the night.
2. THE WITCH WHO LEFT HER CHILD BEHIND
In this part of Africa, husbands sleep alone in separate huts, and go to visit the huts of their
wives at night. This husband had several
[Page [205] ]
wives, each wife having a hut to herself. It is not customary for a man to sleep with his wife
as long as she is suckling her baby. So he will just call her---the thin clay walls of African
huts permit conversation ---without entering.
The elders' method of torture in cases of suspected witchcraft was to put fire near the genitals
of the woman, burning that part of the body completely. It was hoped in this way to annihilate
the evil spirit, as the genitals are supposed to give direct access to a woman's most vital parts.
A woman who had a young baby wanted to go out at night to do some bewitching. She cut off
one of her buttocks and left it in her bed, together with the baby. Whenever the baby cried,
the buttock would soothe him and comfort him.
The woman now left her hut through the roof, naked. When she came back, she would glue
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
167 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 169 ---
the buttock back on her body, on to the place where it belonged, and no one could see where
it had been severed.
Many nights passed in this manner. One night her husband called his wife through the closed
door of the hut. He was disturbed by the crying of the child, and was beginning to wonder if
his wife was there at all.
The man asked through the wall: 'Why is the child crying? Are you looking after him
properly? The noise disturbs my sleep.'
He heard his wife's voice answering from inside the hut: 'Perhaps the baby is being bitten by
the bugs that lodge in the sleeping mat.'
It was the buttock speaking, but of course the husband could not see that. His wife was such a
cunning witch that she could make part of her body speak for herself. The man went back to
his hut thinking that his wife was in hers.
The next night the child was again crying its eyes out. The father was annoyed. He went to
the hut and called the wife. The buttock answered. The man said: 'Come outside with the
baby.' No one came.
[Page [206] ]
The man got impatient and entered the hut. He lit the fire and looked on the bed. There he
saw the baby in tears, and next to him the buttock. The man was stupefied.
After some moments he recovered from the shock and devised a plan. He went and found
some salt, and pepper, and a small knife of the type that is used for tattooing. He carved
tattoo designs around the edges of the buttock and strewed salt and pepper in the wounds, in
the manner of professional tattooers. In this manner he made sure that there would be clear
evidence that the buttock had been separated from the body. Then he took the child on his
knees and sat down on the bed.
After several hours he heard something fall on the floor from the roof. It might have been a
bat---but in fact it was his wife who had come back. At once she took the buttock to re-attach
it to her body, but it would not fit.
She mumbled: 'Something is wrong with this buttock, someone has smeared dirt on it!'
The man said: 'Indeed, so you left your buttock here! Tell me, where have you been? Doesn't
this prove that you are a witch? We'll wait until the morning, and then we shall know for
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
168 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 170 ---
certain.'
Slowly the night passed; very gradually came the dawn. The man sat there with his wife.
Early in the morning he called all the villagers together and told them the whole story.
The wicked woman was called out. She limped, for she had only one leg; she had had to
leave the other one on the bed, as it could not be fitted in its place without the buttock. She
looked like a dismembered goat.
The woman was tortured and forced to speak. The elders know how to do that, with fire and
daggers. She confessed that she was a witch and that she had had meetings with other
witches. She told who they were. She confessed how she used to fly around in the middle of
the night in the shape of an owl; how she penetrated in people's huts while they were
sleeping; how she pointed a brush at an innocent sleeper whom she had selected as her
victim; how
[Page [207] ]
she cut off a tuft of hair from his forehead, over which she would pronounce secret formulae
to make him ill; how the victim would languish away and die, as his vital strength had been
robbed.
In the end, she was taken to the forest and killed. Her head was cut off lest the spirit return to
the body.
3. THE WOMAN WHO ESCAPED THE WITCHES
A woman was beaten by her husband. She was aggrieved, feeling humiliated and rebellious.
She ran away in the middle of the night. Suddenly, at the crossroads, she heard voices but she
saw nothing.
One voice said: 'Take that foot, pull it this way , so that I can cut it off. Make your light shine
more strongly so that I can see.'
There was no light visible, but the woman knew that those were the voices of witches who
were busy cutting up a human being.
Suddenly one of the voices said: 'I smell a live human, where is it?' When the woman heard
this, she closed her armpits by drawing her upper arms closer to her body, trying to put the
witches off the scent. She withdrew quietly, and returned to her husband, to whom she told
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
169 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 171 ---
everything. He said:
'It was the powerful buanga 7 I gave you that protected you from the evil power of those
witches. It made you invisible for them; but you should never have gone to the crossroads in
the middle of the night, for you know that the witches are active then. And you should never
have quarrelled with me, for that gave them some power over you so that they could at least
smell you. Next time you run away from me, you may not be so fortunate. You may get
caught in their invisible nets.'
[Page [208] ]
4. THE THIEF AND THE SORCERER
A thief and a sorcerer lived near each other. One day the sorcerer decided to bewitch the
thief's child. This was easy for him as he was a neighbour. Children are so careless. They
leave their excrement scattered all over the compound, and if the mother is not careful, the
sorcerer may come in the middle of the night with his little box and gather some of it. If he
knows the proper magic ritual, he will succeed in getting power over the person of whom that
waste matter was once a part. In this way, he will sap the life- force of the person, who will
contract an incurable disease and slowly die.
The thief would not be a thief if he were not an expert in bewitching people himself, and so
he guessed that his own neighbour was the culprit. His own magic power, however, was not
strong enough to make his neighbour's witchcraft ineffective. He went to a farmer who gave
him a buanga , which would help him to penetrate the sorcerer's house unnoticed and even
steal parts of the latter's body.
So the thief went and stole the sorcerer's eyes. Then he woke him, shouting: 'My friend
sorcerer, they have stolen your goats!'
The sorcerer answered: 'Not my goats, but my eyes have been pinched! Follow that thief, my
friend neighbour; if you get my eyes back, your child will not die!'
The thief pretended to be running away, but came back soon after. He did this three times.
Finally the sorcerer's wife said to her husband: 'If we return the child to his father first,
perhaps your eyes will be returned.' The sorcerer hesitated, but he knew very well that if he
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
170 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 172 ---
did not cure his neighbour's child he would stay blind for life. So he gave up his power over
the child.
The thief went home and inspected his child. He saw that the boy's health had returned, and
that he looked strong again. So he
[Page [209] ]
went back to his neighbours and restored his eyes by means of his buanga .
The sorcerer woke up and could see. He called out through the wall: 'Neighbour, my two
goats have come back!'
There is a proverb: 'Two leopards do not bite one another.'
The two powerful evil-doers are indeed as careful as two lions meeting. The sorcerer
confesses implicitly to his evil deed when he says: 'Get my eyes [goats] back, and your child
will be cured.' He does not think it prudent to accuse the thief outright, nor does the thief
accuse him. He merely shows that he has equal power and equal ruthlessness.
An interesting little point is that the sorcerer's wife is in her husband's plot and knows all
about his magic practices. Note that she says: 'Return the child.' The sorcerer has indeed
taken the baby, that is, he has 'eaten' the child's vital strength.
Why did the sorcerer bewitch the child of such a dangerous man? He had to. He was forced to
it by his evil nature as well as by his magical colleagues, who must have blackmailed him. He
had to contribute human meat to the coven, or else he would have been eaten himself.
Presumably there were no other children available at that time.
5. THE SORCERER WHO BEWITCHED HIMSELF
A father had three daughters: Bibombe, Kapinga and Kamuania. One day, Kamuania was
beaten by her husband. Hurt and resentful, she ran away, and went back to her parents'
compound. When she arrived near the door of their sleeping hut it was night and pitch dark.
She told herself: 'I will not disturb them now; they might say: go back at once.' While she
was standing there, hesitating, she could not help hearing what her parents were discussing.
Her mother was saying:
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
171 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 173 ---
[Page [210] ]
'If we go to the witches' meeting-place now, we shall only get a small piece of human meat
because we are not contributing anything ourselves.'
Her husband asked: 'What can we do?'
The mother had a plan: 'Go to visit one of your daughters and take a child so that we can give
it to the witches. Bibombe's children are too clever and so are Kapinga's---they will not be so
easy to bewitch. The best thing will be to get one of Kamuania's children in your power,
preferably the one with the swollen belly, or the youngest baby . They will be easy to get hold
of.'
All the time Kamuania had been standing there in the front porch near the door, horrified.
She withdrew very carefully, without making a sound. When she was far enough away, she
ran back to her husband. No one in her parents' place had seen her. She told her husband what
she had heard, that her parents were planning to bewitch their grandchildren. With death in
their hearts they discussed together what they could do to counteract the grandparents' evil
schemes.
The next morning they told their children: 'Remember that you must never accept anything
from anyone. Don't touch it, for if you eat it you will get sick and die. Even if granny comes
here or granddad, don't speak to them and don't forget that anything they give you may be
poison and give you terrible pains in your tummies!'
After the parents had told their children this, they had to go to the fields to plant their millet.
But they did not go with quiet hearts. That same day, the children's grandfather arrived. He
found the children alone in the compound and asked them: 'Where is your father?'
The children replied: 'Our parents are in the fields.'
The old man then gave a locust to the second child and said: 'Here, go and roast it and eat it.
It's good!' He took the other two children, the eldest and the youngest, and held them near
him, for he did not want them to die too. One was enough, he thought.
[Page [211] ]
The second child took the locust inside, held it over the fire for a while, then hid it in a pot
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
172 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 174 ---
and put a flat stone over the opening. He dawdled a little, and when he emerged from the hut,
his grandfather had gone.
When the parents came home that evening, they asked their eldest child: 'Has anybody been
here?'
'Yes, granddad was here.'
'Did he give something to any of you?'
'Yes, he gave a locust to my little brother, who roasted it and ate it.'
When the mother heard this, she beat her breast, crying out: 'Oh, my child is dying, he has
been hit by witchcraft!' She called the little boy and asked him: 'Did grandfather give you a
locust?'
'Yes.'
'Did you roast it?'
'Yes.'
'Then, did you eat it? Tell me!'
'No, I didn't, it's still there!'
The father took the stone off the pot the boy pointed at, and looked inside. He saw the locust
and put the stone back. The parents were jubilant: 'Our child was saved by his obedience!'
The parents now planned a counter-attack on the wicked grandfather. They killed a big capon
and cooked it. Its inside was stuffed with various delicacies, and the bewitched locust was put
inside the stuffing. The meat was to be served in one bowl together with the gravy.
That night, the grandfather was invited to dinner. He loved chicken sauce, so he took a ball of
tapioca and dipped it in the gravy; then he took a piece of the liver, and ate that too. He asked
where his grandchildren were but he was told that they had been sent away to their aunt for
the day. That, of course, had been done to prevent them from eating any of the dangerous
meal. Next, grandfather looked inside the bird and discovered the locust. He was so startled
that his belt fell off. At once he collected all his
[Page [212] ]
things, put them in his bag, and went straight home without saying good-bye.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
173 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 175 ---
His daughter said to her husband: 'Father discovered his locust and did not eat it, but its juice
has gone all through the sauce, so that he has certainly eaten some of it. Now he has eaten
himself, for the evil spirit which he had put in the locust was part of the evil spirit that rules
him too.'
She was right. A few days later her father fell ill, and he died shortly after. The spirit which
he had sent to his grandchild, so that it might kill him as soon as he had eaten it, was now
inside his own stomach. It had to have satisfaction, for spirits pine for human flesh. It ate him
from the inside: it killed its host.
When his daughter was informed of her father's death, she wailed loudly: 'Oh my father! The
strong one had destroyed himself; his own magic was too powerful for him!' The people
asked her to explain her words, and she told them the story . The bodies of sorcerers are not
buried: they are not worth any ceremony. So grandfather's corpse was thrown away in the
bush. The hyenas ate it---and you can still see the evil spirits glowing in the hyenas' eyes.
6. WHEN THE WITCH IS FOUND
This story is historical.
A woman had a miscarriage, the second one in two years. Her husband was in despair. He
threw himself on to the floor, crying and wailing:
'Why do I deserve this, why can I not have children, why may I have no young ones around
me to support me when I grow old and bent? I gave all my cattle to acquire this woman, my
goats and my spear-blades! She was not given to me free, she was not exchanged for a mere
pot of meat! She was not found on the
[Page [213] ]
roadside! I am not a thief. I have never squeezed money out of my neighbours; why am I
punished thus?'
There he lay , desperate, in the dust. Then he jumped up and ran outside to where his relatives
were standing, listening to his sad lamentations. Furious, he shouted:
'Don't just stand there, it's your own family that is dying, your own flesh that cannot come to
life! Someone is killing us and you know it, someone is sending us unhappiness for some evil
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
174 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 176 ---
reason! Go to the diviner and find out! Do you think I would want to kill an innocent person?'
The next day, the relatives went to see the woman-diviner. They brought cloth, tobacco, some
fish and a chicken as payment for the consultation. The diviner's husband received them
courteously, spreading mats and putting stools ready for the guests to sit on, saying: 'Rest a
while, the diviner is home and will be ready to hear your case in a short time.'
After a while, the diviner herself entered the reception-room and sat down solemnly.
The eldest gentleman among the relatives said: 'Mother diviner, we have come, we are your
guests.'
She said: 'Thank you for coming. My eyes see you.'
She went back into her inner room and reappeared with a bowl which was decorated with
strange figures: squares and triangles. It contained seeds, leaves, pepper, powder, the eye of a
partridge and several small figurines made of wood. She shook the bowl to and fro so that the
powder flew about in the room. The visitors coughed and sneezed, saying to each other: 'This
famous lady surely uses potent medicines!' That remark showed admiration.
Now the diviner began her inquiries. These were held in a singsong voice, as if she was
intoning a prayer:
Now, let us begin the asking,
asking why these people came here,
Did they come here for a person?
For a man or for a woman?
[Page [214] ]
For a woman? Was she healthy?
Was she pregnant with a baby?
Pregnant from her lawful husband?
Why then is the husband angry?
Was the child duly delivered?
Did the mother die in childbirth?
Did the child not see the daylight?
Has the household no survivor?
No one to inter the parents? ...
In this manner, by singing rhythmically, the diviner arrived at the correct conclusion. After
each question she watched her audience attentively, to see if anyone gave away the answer by
nodding or shaking their heads. As soon as she had a clue she would sing the next line, in this
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
175 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 177 ---
manner narrowing down the number of possibilities at each question; sometimes an
involuntary gesture would indicate such circumstances as anger, or pregnancy.
Her visitors, in the stuffy atmosphere of the hut, were duly impressed by her unexpected
knowledge, as shown by the mysterious singing. No one spoke, but eyebrows and mouths
showed admiration whenever her questions hit the mark. Having confirmed the reason for the
visit (she would probably have heard about the miscarriages already), she proceeded to
answer the terrible question: Who has bewitched the poor mother?
She indicated a lonely old woman in the same family as the witch, using no specific terms
and no names, just hinting vaguely and leaving the conclusions to her audience.
The visitors certainly did jump to conclusions. They all agreed that the witch must be aunt
So-and-so. 'She is old and she lives alone, she is an aunt of the ill-starred woman. We do not
know what she does or what she lives on, no one knows her really ...'
So the people worked themselves up into a frenzy. The husband was glad that his anger had
found an object, and so he whipped up the feelings of the relatives by saying: 'This might
have happened to any one of you! One day she will do it to you, the next day to you!'
[Page [215] ]
So he went on ranting until the decent clan had been turned into a raging mob. They surged
into the old woman's compound and destroyed everything they found. They set fire to the hut,
believing that in this way they were purifying it from all the evil powers. The poor woman
came running on her old legs when she saw her hut burning. She had been working her
cassava field, and in fact she had had nothing to do with the whole affair. She hardly knew the
people she was supposed to have bewitched.
As soon as the mob saw her they cried: 'There she is, the evil hag!' Some of the people,
wishing to be fair and just, proposed to hold the ceremony of the poison test, but the mob was
already beyond that point. They were totally blinded by their hatred of witchcraft and their
terror of sorcerers.
Crying 'Kill her! We've got the witch! Cut off her head! Who has a knife? Burn her alive!
Throw her in the fire! Hack her to pieces!' they dragged her to the village square. Someone
gouged out her eyes, someone else tied her with a rope. She was beaten with sticks, prodded
with spear-blades, and scorched with flaming torches.
At some point during this torture she died. She may have fainted with fright when she saw
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
176 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 178 ---
the mob stampeding towards her. She was probably quite dead as soon as the first heavy stick
hit her head. She was only a frail old woman, eking out a meagre existence with a little
cultivation. She did not cling to life, for she had no one to live for. And it was just that
miserable loneliness that made her suspect.
When the mob had done with her, when it had vented its anger on her emaciated body, she
was a terrible sight. Soon the dogs arrived: before long they were fighting over her body,
tearing it to pieces. There were piebald crows too, each flying away with a piece of flesh.
Finally came the vultures.
[Page [216] ]
7. THE FIRST MAN
God created the earth first; then he took two stones and made them into the sun and the
moon. He further created Rain, whose greatest desire it was to fall down and inundate the
earth, and Darkness, over whom Moon scattered a basketful of seeds: the stars.
At the time when there were still no people on earth, God sent Kiyomba to earth with two
women. According to another tradition, the first man had only one human wife, whereas the
other wife was Kizimu, the serpent.
Kizimu gave her younger co-wife two fruits, saying: 'Give one to your husband, then eat them
together. Remember that you must not open the fruit before you are with your husband.' Of
course, on the way, curiosity overcame the young woman, so she opened the fruit. Inside it
there was a magic powder which made the woman feel ashamed. She felt for the first time
that she was a woman. Kizimu now gave the man another fruit, which made him desire
women, and so he started quarrelling with his wife.
Kiyomba had one son with Kizimu, whom he loved very dearly. One day Kizimu felt old and
told her husband she wanted to live in seclusion for a period of time until she was quite well
again. So Kiyomba built a hut for her and told their son that his mother was sick and should
not be disturbed. In the hut, Kizimu underwent a process of rejuvenation. She had borrowed
a winnowing basket from her co-wife. Now she began to strip off her skin and put it in the
window. First the skin of her legs, then her arms, then her trunk, so that only her head and
neck were left to be stripped. At that moment the young co-wife arrived, and opened the
outer door, then the second, then the third.
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
177 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 179 ---
Kizimu cried: 'Do not come here, do not enter! If you do, we shall all have to die!'
Of course the young woman did not know what death was, so
[Page [217] ]
she opened the innermost door, saying she wanted her winnowing basket back. Kizimu died
on the spot, and therefore mankind, not having learned the art of casting off the skin, was
doomed to die, one after the other.
Kiyomba received many different kinds of seeds from God, which he plaited into his hair.
When he arrived home, he scattered the seeds over his land and found that all sorts of
vegetables and cereals grew up from them.
According to another version of the story, man and snake were given the choice between two
baskets. Man, not knowing what he did, chose the basket that contained death; the serpent
was left with the Basket of Life, which contained the secret of casting the old slough and
growing a new skin.
Annoyed at man's failure to achieve immortality, God withdrew from earth and went up to
heaven. Kiyomba, the first man, felt an unconquerable desire to rejoin God, to be near him
again. He started out with his young wife towards the horizon, where the sky rests on copper
pillars, standing in the land of sunset. Of course he never reached the land of his desire. On
their way, they had many children who populated the earth.
God said to man: 'You will not be allowed to join me as long as you are on earth. But I have
created a new thing, 
mutima , the soul, which will be the kernel of life for you. I will also
give you the sticks with which you can rub fire, the spear to hunt with, and the hoe to
cultivate with. Finally, I give you all the fruits of the earth. You may eat them all, except one,
the banana, which men may not eat.'
Of course, woman, Kiyomba's wife, picked some bananas and ate them. Soon God discovered
that some bananas were missing. He called his four children together: Sun, Moon, Man and
Woman. He told them: 'I will bury you all. The one who has not stolen my bananas will rise
soon afterwards.' So he did.
The sun was the first to rise. After only one night, he rose again out of the earth. The moon
took four days before she reappeared
[Page [218] ]
in the sky, very thin and slender but growing steadily. Man and woman did not rise at all. So
they remained buried till the present day. Only mutima , the soul, was taken to heaven by
African Writers Series - Print V iew http://collections.chadwyck.com.proxy2.ulib.iupui.edu/display/printV iew.do
178 of 180 7/9/2011 1:27 PM


--- PAGE 180 ---
God, because it was his youngest creation, which he loved dearly.
One young man, a cripple, succeeded in entering Heaven, because he had learned to pray.
God asked him what he wanted, and the cripple said he wanted to be a normal man like all
the others---because, he said, they all mocked him, and this he found hard to endure. God
healed him, sent him back and said:
'No one should mock his fellow, as they will all die.'
